### [1] [iFLYTEK-Embodied-Omni Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02542)
*Yuan Zhang,Jingfei Ni,Guanchen Lu,Shiqi Zhang,Qingshan Xu,Chi Liu,Xin Nie,Wenjie Xu,Lin Gao,Zhiyuan Cheng,Mingxin Zhou,Jiajia Wu,Diyuan Liu,Jia Pan,Chao Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: iFLYTEK-Embodied-Omni is a unified multimodal foundation model that jointly models vision, language, and action in a single framework, using shared multimodal self-attention and a brain-cerebellum collaboration design to integrate visual-language reasoning, video generation, and action generation without cascaded pipeline bottlenecks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing embodied AI approaches rely on cascaded pipelines (first synthesize future observations, then infer actions) which introduce interface bottlenecks and compound prediction errors. A unified model that jointly handles visual-language reasoning, world modeling, and action generation is needed for general-purpose embodied agents.

Method: The model has three components (VLM, VGM, AGM) communicating via shared multimodal self-attention in a brain-cerebellum architecture. It is trained on a combined dataset of action-annotated/action-free embodied videos, human demonstrations, robot interactions, and general image-text data, using a four-stage progressive training strategy.

Result: The unified Omni framework eliminates cascaded pipeline bottlenecks and compound prediction errors by enabling joint modeling and communication between visual-language reasoning, video-based world modeling, and action generation through shared multimodal self-attention.

Conclusion: Jointly modeling vision, language, and action within a single Omni framework with brain-cerebellum architecture is a promising direction for building general-purpose embodied agents capable of understanding multimodal instructions and producing precise control actions over extended horizons.

Abstract: General-purpose embodied agents must understand multimodal instructions, anticipate how their environment will evolve, and produce precise control actions over extended horizons. Existing approaches typically specialize in visual-language reasoning, video-based world modeling, or action generation, while cascaded pipelines that first synthesize future observations and then infer actions can introduce interface bottlenecks and compound prediction errors. We present iFLYTEK-Embodied-Omni, a unified multimodal foundation model that jointly models vision(videos and images), language, and action within a single Omni framework. Its modality-specific visual-language, video-generation, and action-generation components communicate through shared multimodal self-attention. This design establishes brain-cerebellum collaboration: the vision-language modeland video generation model form a high-level brain for instruction understanding, task planning, progress tracking, and future visual-state prediction, whereas the action generation modelserves as a low-level cerebellum that directly converts planned subgoals and shared multimodal context into executable action chunks. To develop these capabilities, we combine action-annotated and action-free embodied videos from human demonstrations and robot interactions with embodied reasoning, embodied perception, and general-purpose image-text data to construct a comprehensive dataset. We further adopt a four-stage strategy that progressively trains the VLM, VGM, and AGM before jointly fine-tuning the complete model.

</details>


### [2] [Internal Pluralism and the Limits of Pairwise Comparisons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02672)
*Bailey Flanigan,Michelle Si*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper challenges two assumptions of local pairwise comparisons in preference learning (that local comparisons suffice to capture preferences and that people can always answer decisively) by introducing a model of internal pluralism where individuals hold multiple authoritative priorities. It identifies failures from global priorities and internal conflict, shows that allowing indecision reduces needed queries, and proposes directly eliciting priorities for more faithful preference learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate how local pairwise comparisons—a standard tool for learning decision rule preferences (e.g., in alignment or participatory design)—fail under internal pluralism, where individuals hold multiple authoritative priorities that may be global in nature or in tension with each other.

Method: Formal modeling of pluralistic preferences over decision rules, identifying two failure modes of forced local pairwise comparisons: (1) global priorities (e.g., proportionality, egalitarianism) whose implications are context-dependent across cases, and (2) internal conflict between strongly-held priorities causing behavioral distortions under forced choice. The model is then used to analyze allowing indecision as an alternative.

Result: Global priorities cannot be captured by local comparisons alone. Even when priorities are local, forced comparisons produce costly behavioral distortions due to internal conflict. Allowing indecision significantly reduces the number of queries needed for accurate preference learning.

Conclusion: The paper points toward preference-learning methods that directly elicit underlying priorities rather than relying on pairwise comparisons, yielding more faithful and interpretable accounts of human values.

Abstract: Local pairwise comparisons are a standard tool for learning how people want decision rules to work, e.g., in participatory design or alignment. However, their use builds in two strong assumptions: that local comparisons are sufficient evidence about how a person wants an automated decision rule to behave, and that people can always answer those comparisons decisively. We investigate how these assumptions may be compromised under internal pluralism: the idea that an individual evaluates decision rules according to multiple authoritative priorities about how the rule should behave. We provide a formal model of such pluralistic preferences over decision rules, which then lets us identify two distinct failures of forced local pairwise comparison data. First, priorities such as proportionality, egalitarianism, and equal treatment are inherently global: what they imply in one case can depend on what happens elsewhere, so local comparisons may fail to capture them. Second, even when priorities are representable locally, tension between strongly-held priorities can generate internal conflict, producing potentially costly behavioral distortions when comparisons are forced. We then use our model to investigate the alternative -- allowing people to report indecision -- and our findings suggest that doing so can considerably reduce the number of queries needed to learn preferences accurately. We conclude by describing how our model points toward preference-learning methods that elicit these priorities directly, yielding more faithful and interpretable accounts of what people value.

</details>


### [3] [ASK in the Dark: Uncertainty-Gated LLM Assistance under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02686)
*Juarez Monteiro,Nathan Gavenski,Guilherme Lima,Francisco Galuppo,Odinaldo Rodrigues,Adriano Veloso*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASK+ improves SLM-guided reinforcement learning in POMDPs by replacing bare egocentric prompts with trajectory-aware context and structured chain-of-thought reasoning, enabling effective uncertainty-gated assistance that outperforms vanilla approaches and matches larger models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning agents under partial observability could benefit from SLM guidance, but existing uncertainty-gated approaches fail because the overwrite rate is near zero. The authors trace this to insufficient context in bare egocentric prompts, identifying it as a context problem rather than a capacity problem.

Method: ASK+ supplies SLMs with trajectory-aware context (partially revealed map, visited positions, action history) and structured chain-of-thought reasoning, converting the SLM from a passive redundancy check into an informative consultant. The approach uses predictive entropy for selective querying, which measures action uncertainty rather than state uncertainty and remains informative in POMDPs.

Result: On DoorKey, ASK+ reaches 93% success (vs 89% for vanilla ASK and PPO). On FourRooms, success climbs from 53% to 70%. On HigherLower, accuracy reaches 73.7%, matching the SLM-only upper bound. Qwen3.5-2B matches or exceeds Qwen3.5-4B across all environments.

Conclusion: Prompt design and selective gating dominate the impact of model scale, enabling effective SLM guidance without large models. Predictive entropy remains informative for selective querying in POMDPs, making uncertainty-gated assistance viable beyond fully observable settings.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning agents operating under partial observability must act on incomplete information, making them natural candidates for guidance from small language models (SLMs) that carry broad reasoning priors. Yet integrating SLM guidance into this setting has proven difficult: across all test environments, vanilla uncertainty-gated approaches achieve an overwrite rate at or near zero, meaning the SLM almost never contributes an independent action. We trace this failure to the bare egocentric prompt, which provides insufficient context for genuine reasoning, and identify it as a context problem rather than a capacity problem. We propose ASK+, which supplies the SLM with trajectory-aware context (a partially revealed map, visited positions, and action history) and structured chain-of-thought reasoning, converting it from a passive redundancy check into a more informative consultant that occasionally corrects the policy. We further establish that the predictive entropy signal used for selective querying measures action uncertainty rather than state uncertainty and remains informative in POMDPs, making uncertainty-gated assistance viable beyond fully observable settings. The stateful prompt drives substantial gains: on DoorKey, where vanilla ASK matches PPO (both 89%), ASK+ reaches 93% success; on FourRooms, success climbs from 53% to 70%; on HigherLower, accuracy reaches 73.7%, matching the SLM-only upper bound. Across all environments, Qwen3.5-2B matches or exceeds Qwen3.5-4B, confirming that prompt design and selective gating dominate the impact of model scale, enabling guidance without large models.

</details>


### [4] [Automated Data Readiness for Scientific AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02771)
*Sean R. Wilkinson,Valentine G. Anantharaj,Jong Youl Choi,Ketan Maheshwari,Marshall McDonnell,Massimiliano Lupo Pasini,Polina Shpilker,Renan Souza,Patrick Widener,Sarp Oral,Wesley Brewer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: REDI is an open-source framework that unifies automated transformation, readiness assessment, provenance tracking, and agent-native deployment for preparing large-scale scientific datasets for AI training, demonstrated across climate, proteomics, materials science, and nuclear fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Leadership computing facilities steward large-scale scientific datasets that require substantial transformation before serving as AI training data, yet no existing framework fully unifies automated transformation, readiness assessment, provenance tracking, and agent-native deployment.

Method: REDI implements a unified five-stage pipeline (ingest, preprocess, transform, structure, output) with per-stage instrumentation for reproducibility and agent-callable skill deployment; companion tool SetGo automates FAIR compliance and catalog publication.

Result: REDI transforms datasets from raw to AI-ready across four scientific domains, validated against domain-expert references; near-ideal parallel scaling to 100 nodes on Frontier for the climate case; file I/O identified as the dominant pipeline cost with format selection as a first-order optimization lever.

Conclusion: REDI establishes a cross-domain platform providing automated data readiness for scientific AI, transforming data preparation bottlenecks into reproducible, reusable community assets.

Abstract: Leadership computing facilities steward large-scale scientific datasets that routinely require substantial transformation before serving as AI training data. However, no existing framework fully unifies automated transformation, readiness assessment, provenance tracking, and agent-native deployment. We present REDI, an open-source framework that addresses this gap through a unified five-stage pipeline (ingest, preprocess, transform, structure, and output) with per-stage instrumentation for reproducibility and deployment as an agent-callable skill; companion tool SetGo automates FAIR compliance and catalog publication. Evaluated across climate, proteomics, materials science, and nuclear fusion, REDI transforms all datasets from raw to AI-ready, with outputs validated against domain-expert references, and preliminary results show near-ideal parallel scaling to 100 nodes on Frontier for the climate case. Provenance-instrumented profiling reveals file I/O as the dominant pipeline cost, with format selection a first-order optimization lever. These results establish REDI as a cross-domain platform providing automated data readiness for scientific AI, transforming data preparation bottlenecks into reproducible, reusable community assets.

</details>


### [5] [SwarmResearch: Orchestrating Coding Agents for Open-Ended Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02807)
*Yuvraj Virk,Zack Edds,Chunqiu Steven Xia,Lingming Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SwarmResearch is a multi-agent coding framework where a Shepherd Agent orchestrates a population of Search Agents working in parallel git branches, achieving superior exploration and results on 13/15 tasks compared to existing LLM-guided evolution and multi-agent techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-running coding agents tend to converge onto a single high-level approach and miss superior alternatives, due to accumulating context in a single agent and only exposing a single program state to edit.

Method: SwarmResearch uses a two-level orchestrator-subagent harness: a Shepherd Agent maintains global context to steer a population of Search Agents, each operating with local context in their own git branch, enabling parallel exploration of diverse approaches.

Result: SwarmResearch discovers better or comparable solutions to state-of-the-art LLM-guided evolution and multi-agent techniques on 13/15 open-ended optimization tasks, with superior performance through orchestrator-guided adaptive parallelism.

Conclusion: Orchestrator-guided scaling with adaptive parallelism at different search depths outperforms fixed scaling of serial and parallel agents, enabling higher-level exploration and avoiding convergence on suboptimal approaches.

Abstract: Long-running coding agents such as autoresearch can persistently discover optimizations for open-ended problems. However, they tend to converge onto a single high-level approach, then proceed with low-level edits while missing other superior approaches to the problem. We hypothesize two harness-level design choices contribute to this behavior: accumulating context in a single long-running agent and only exposing a single program state to edit. We introduce SwarmResearch, an orchestrator-subagent harness in which a Shepherd Agent uses global context to steer a population of Search Agents, each operating with local context in their respective git branch. On open-ended optimization tasks, SwarmResearch discovers better or comparable solutions to state-of-the-art LLM-guided evolution and multi-agent techniques on 13/15 tasks, driven by higher-level exploration. Compared with fixed scaling of serial and parallel agents, SwarmResearch's orchestrator-guided scaling discovers better-performing solutions by adapting parallelism at different search depths.

</details>


### [6] [Object-Centric Environment Modeling for Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02846)
*Yiyang Li,Tianyi Ma,Zehong Wang,Yijun Ma,Yanfang Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OCM organizes LLM agent experience into an executable object-centric environment model with two connected code bases (object knowledge and procedure knowledge), achieving better performance and fewer invalid actions through online learning and progressive knowledge disclosure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Free-form textual memories in LLM agents are difficult to maintain, validate, and reuse as interactions grow; existing symbolic approaches either store local procedures or assume simplified dynamics.

Method: OCM maintains object knowledge (Python classes for environment entities/mechanisms) and procedure knowledge (reusable interaction patterns). After each episode, it reflects on trajectories, updates both knowledge bases, and verifies procedures execute against the updated model. During interaction, progressive knowledge disclosure first inspects compact code signatures, then reads source code only when needed.

Result: OCM achieves the best average rank across benchmarks and reduces invalid actions.

Conclusion: Agents can benefit from building object-centric environment models for more maintainable and reusable experience accumulation.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents can improve through accumulated experience, but free-form textual memories become difficult to maintain, validate, and reuse as interactions grow. Recent symbolic approaches learn executable skills or programmatic world models, yet often store local procedures or assume simplified dynamics. We propose Object-Centric Environment Modeling (OCM), which organizes experience into an executable object-centric environment model. OCM maintains two connected code bases: object knowledge, which defines environment entities and mechanisms as Python classes, and procedure knowledge, which records reusable interaction patterns that must import and use the object model. OCM works in an online setting: after each episode, OCM reflects on the trajectory, updates both knowledge bases, and verifies that all procedures execute against the updated object model. During future interaction, the agent uses progressive knowledge disclosure to inspect compact code signatures first and read source code only when needed. Experiments show that OCM achieves the best average rank across benchmarks and reduces invalid actions, demonstrating that agents can benefit from building object-centric environment models.

</details>


### [7] [MedCalc-Pro: Solving Complex Medical Calculations with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02879)
*Siran Zhao,Ruihui Hou,Ziyue Huai,Chennuo Zhang,Tong Ruan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedCalc-Pro is a new medical calculation benchmark with 2,268 clinical cases across 3 task settings (single, multi, nested calculator), alongside a generalizable agent framework for multi-tool selection and nested-tool calling that outperforms existing LLMs in complex clinical calculation scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medical calculation benchmarks oversimplify real clinical needs by using single-calculator tasks with explicitly specified tools, failing to capture complex scenarios requiring multiple calculators, nested-scale calculations, and fuzzy queries.

Method: The authors propose MedCalc-Pro (2,268 cases, 77 calculators, 14 departments) covering single-, multi-, and nested-calculator settings, and a generalizable agent framework supporting multi-tool selection, nested-tool calling, structured validation, and evidence review to mitigate parameter error propagation.

Result: Systematic comparisons across open-source, closed-source, and medical-specialized LLMs show the proposed agent framework achieves best performance across all three task settings.

Conclusion: This work introduces a new benchmark and method for evaluating and applying LLMs in challenging medical calculation scenarios, highlighting the need for multi-tool and nested-calculator handling capabilities.

Abstract: Current benchmarks for evaluating large language models (LLMs) in medical calculation are largely based on simplified settings, where each patient case corresponds to a single calculator and the required tool is explicitly specified in the query. However, real clinical scenarios often require multiple calculators for joint evaluation, nested-scale calculation, and fuzzy queries that do not directly specify the target calculator. To this end, we propose a new medical calculation benchmark, MedCalc-Pro, which covers three progressively challenging task settings: single-calculator, multi-calculator, and nested-calculator calculation settings. MedCalc-Pro contains 2,268 real-world clinical cases, covering 77 medical calculators across 14 clinical departments. Meanwhile, to address the limited performance of existing frameworks and methods in complex clinical scenarios, we further propose a more generalizable agent framework that supports multi-tool selection and nested-tool calling, while suppressing parameter error propagation through structured validation and evidence review. We conduct systematic comparisons across open-source, closed-source, and medical-specialized LLMs, and the results show that our framework achieves the best performance across all three task settings. This work provides a new benchmark and method for evaluating and applying LLMs in challenging medical calculation scenarios.

</details>


### [8] [Oyster-II: Reinforcement Learning for Constructive Safety Alignment in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02914)
*Jiyang Guan,Yong Xie,Jun Chen,Jiexi Liu,Zipeng Ye,Defeng Li,Jiayu Shen,Jialing Tao,Hui Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Oyster-II uses multi-stage RL (Zero-RL paradigm) for constructive safety alignment, overcoming SFT-based limitations of poor generalization and over-generalization of safety reasoning, outperforming prior models and matching much larger ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional refusal-oriented alignment strategies fail to serve legitimate user needs by withholding safe, constructive responses to sensitive queries. The prior SFT-based constructive safety approach (Oyster-I) suffers from insufficient safety generalization to out-of-distribution scenarios and safety chain-of-thought over-generalization (excessive safety reasoning applied to benign queries).

Method: Oyster-II proposes a reinforcement learning (RL)-based constructive safety alignment framework adopting a Zero-RL paradigm combined with a multi-stage reinforcement learning strategy, addressing limitations of the prior SFT-based scheme (insufficient safety generalization and safety CoT over-generalization).

Result: Oyster-II comprehensively surpasses Qwen3-14B and Oyster-I on safety dimensions and achieves cross-scale performance comparable to Qwen3-Max and Qwen3.5-397B.

Conclusion: Oyster-II, an RL-based constructive safety alignment framework, outperforms both Qwen3-14B and Oyster-I across safety dimensions, achieving cross-scale performance comparable to much larger models like Qwen3-Max and Qwen3.5-397B.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across diverse applications, yet ensuring their simultaneous safety, helpfulness, and trustworthiness remains a persistent challenge. Conventional refusal-oriented alignment strategies mitigate harmful content generation but systematically fail to serve legitimate user needs, often withholding information that could safely and constructively address the underlying intent of sensitive queries. Building upon the constructive safety paradigm pioneered by Oyster-I, which moves beyond blanket refusal toward thoughtful, response-oriented safety alignment, we identify two critical limitations of its Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT)-based scheme: insufficient safety generalization to out-of-distribution scenarios and a phenomenon we term safety chain-of-thought (CoT) over-generalization, wherein safety-oriented reasoning patterns are excessively applied to benign queries, degrading helpfulness and user experience. To address these limitations, we propose Oyster-II, a reinforcement learning (RL)-based constructive safety alignment framework that adopts a Zero-RL paradigm combined with a multi-stage reinforcement learning strategy.Evaluated across extensive benchmarks, Oyster-II comprehensively surpasses both Qwen3-14B and its predecessor Oyster-I on safety dimensions, achieving cross-scale performance comparable to Qwen3-Max and Qwen3.5-397B.

</details>


### [9] [VERITAS: Towards a General-Purpose Replication Tool for Scientific Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02931)
*Haokun Liu,Filbert Aurelian Tjiaranata,Chenhao Tan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VERITAS is a domain-agnostic replication framework using CLI coding agents that extracts claims from papers, runs methodology with automatic issue resolution, and produces importance-weighted Replication Scores. It achieves state-of-the-art performance on 65 papers across CS, social science, medicine, and astrophysics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI tools accelerate publication while review systems lag; manual replication is slow and expensive. Existing agent-based replication efforts are limited to benchmark-specific pipelines with no general-purpose tool available.

Method: VERITAS is a CLI coding agent framework that: (1) extracts claims from a paper/codebase, (2) runs the experimental methodology with automatic issue resolution, (3) judges each claim against experiment evidence, and (4) returns an importance-weighted Replication Score, severity-rated fix log, and patched codebase.

Result: Evaluated on CORE-Bench and ReplicationBench (65 papers across CS, social science, medicine, astrophysics). Outperforms two strong Claude Code baselines on the same model/host environment, achieving state-of-the-art on every metric on both benchmarks.

Conclusion: VERITAS provides the first general-purpose, domain-agnostic replication tool that automates claim verification, issue resolution, and scoring, significantly advancing automated research reproducibility assessment.

Abstract: AI tools are accelerating scientific publication while the systems that review it struggle to keep up, and independent verification of published research has become both harder and more important. As manual replication is slow and expensive, a growing line of work uses coding agents to automate parts of the process. Existing efforts are largely packaged as benchmarks with companion agents that only run inside the benchmark's own pipeline, and no general-purpose replication tool exists. We present VERITAS, a domain-agnostic replication framework built around CLI coding agents. Given a paper, a code repository, or both, VERITAS extracts the paper's claims, runs the methodology while resolving issues as they arise, and judges each claim against the evidence from experiment runs. The pipeline returns an importance-weighted Replication Score, a severity-rated log of every fix applied, and the patched codebase. We evaluate VERITAS on CORE-Bench and ReplicationBench, 65 papers spanning computer science, social science, medicine, and astrophysics. Against two strong Claude Code baselines on the same model and host environment, VERITAS achieves state-of-the-art performance and leads on every metric on both benchmarks.

</details>


### [10] [A Sliding-Window-Based Reinforcement Learning for Dynamic Assembly Flow Shop Scheduling with Multi-Product Delivery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02941)
*Junhao Qiu,Jianjun Liu,Ting Liu,Rongjie Liao,Zhantao Li,Qingfu Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a sliding-window-based reinforcement learning (SWRL) framework for end-to-end online scheduling in flexible assembly flow shops with multi-product kitting delivery constraints, achieving consistent tardiness reductions over classical and deep RL baselines on real-world manufacturing instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-product kitting delivery imposes significant real-time scheduling challenges in hybrid manufacturing systems due to dynamic order arrivals that simultaneously alter supply dependencies and feasible job-machine assignments, and existing methods struggle with the sparse reward landscape caused by tail-product bottleneck dynamics.

Method: SWRL integrates three key components: (1) a sliding-window filtering mechanism to filter inactive nodes and prioritize kitting-critical operations, (2) a spatiotemporal graph encoding network to track bottleneck shifts across consecutive decision states, and (3) a dynamic action mapping module with a constrained waiting strategy to adapt to changing action spaces under variable topologies. The problem is formulated as a heterogeneous graph-based Markov decision process capturing dual-layer kitting structure.

Result: Experiments on real-world instances from a home appliance manufacturer show SWRL achieves consistent tardiness reductions over classical dispatching rules and existing deep reinforcement learning methods, with robust performance across varying resource configurations, order loads, and arrival concentrations.

Conclusion: The SWRL framework effectively addresses sparse rewards and dynamic action spaces in kitting-constrained flexible assembly flow shop scheduling, offering a practical end-to-end solution for real-time online scheduling in hybrid manufacturing systems.

Abstract: Multi-product kitting delivery imposes significant challenges for real-time scheduling in hybrid manufacturing systems that integrate processing and assembly, as dynamic order arrivals simultaneously alter supply dependencies and the set of feasible job-machine assignments. This paper proposes a sliding-window-based reinforcement learning (SWRL) framework for end-to-end online scheduling in the flexible assembly flow shop scheduling problem with complex kitting constraints. The problem is formulated as a heterogeneous graph-based Markov decision process that captures the dual-layer kitting structure and the tail-product bottleneck dynamics that produce a sparse reward landscape. To address the resulting challenges, SWRL integrates a sliding-window filtering mechanism that filters inactive nodes and prioritizes kitting-critical operations, a spatiotemporal graph encoding network that tracks bottleneck shifts across consecutive decision states, and a dynamic action mapping module with a constrained waiting strategy that adapts to the changing action space under variable topologies. Experiments on real-world instances from a home appliance manufacturer demonstrate that SWRL achieves consistent tardiness reductions over classical dispatching rules and existing deep reinforcement learning methods, and exhibits robust performance across varying resource configurations, order loads, and arrival concentrations.

</details>


### [11] [Evaluating Generative Agents with Actions Grounded in Socially Distributed Task Environments using Incognita](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02975)
*Dan C. Hsu,Luke Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Incognita, a framework for evaluating AI agents in socially distributed task environments where knowledge is partitioned across role-isolated participants, and agents must communicate to gather information before acting. Tests on 18 retail tasks show progress in knowledge elicitation and action attempts but overall low reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing grounded benchmarks provide executable actions and verifiable outcomes, while social simulation environments enable rich interaction. However, no evaluation setting combines both requirements—where task-relevant knowledge is partitioned across role-isolated participants and consequential actions are only accessible through them.

Method: Incognita separates social interaction from grounded execution: the agent routes messages to users/specialists; specialists mediate admissible operations; a deterministic sub-environment executes accepted operations; an offline evaluator scores outcomes. Incognita-Retail adapts tau-bench retail into a multi-entity environment. Three generative agent models were evaluated on 18 tasks stratified by social breadth (540 trials).

Result: Success rates rose from 0% to 8.9% and 17.2% across model tiers. Premature finalization fell from 100% to 87% and 58%. Stronger models elicited more hidden knowledge, contacted more entities, and attempted more grounded writes, yet overall reliability remained low.

Conclusion: Socially distributed task environments expose agent behavior before reliable success—including knowledge elicitation, source selection, grounded action attempts, and premature completion belief—revealing a gap between behavioral competence and task success.

Abstract: Effective agency in social environments depends on when an agent seeks knowledge, when it acts, and whether its actions are justified by acquired information. Existing grounded benchmarks provide executable actions, persistent state, and verifiable outcomes, while social simulation environments provide rich interaction among language agents. We study an evaluation setting that combines these requirements. We define socially distributed task environments as interactive environments where task-relevant knowledge is partitioned across role-isolated participants and consequential actions are accessible only through them. Communication serves as exploration over role-partitioned knowledge, while grounded action serves as exploitation over environment state. We introduce Incognita, a Concordia-based framework that separates social interaction from grounded execution. The evaluated agent routes messages to a user or specialist entities; specialists mediate admissible operations; a deterministic sub-environment executes accepted operations over a canonical state; and an offline evaluator scores outcomes with inherited rewards. Incognita-Retail transforms tau-bench retail into a multi-entity environment while preserving final-state reward semantics. We evaluate three generative agent models on 18 tasks stratified by social breadth, with 540 trials. Progress appears in reward and behavior: success rises from 0 percent to 8.9 percent and 17.2 percent, while premature finalization falls from 100 percent to 87 percent and 58 percent. Stronger models elicit more hidden knowledge, contact more entities, and attempt more grounded writes, yet reliability remains low. These findings show that socially distributed task environments expose behavior before reliable success, including knowledge elicitation, source selection, grounded action attempts, and premature completion belief.

</details>


### [12] [Reinforcement Learning for Evidence-Seeking Diagnostic Reasoning with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02983)
*Shengyi Hua,Kangzhe Hu,Conghui He,Xiaofan Zhang,Shaoting Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes medical diagnosis as an Iterative Evidence-Seeking Task, using Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) and a Retrieval-Augmented Generation-based Examination Simulator (RAGES) to transform LLMs from passive responders into autonomous clinical assistants that strategically acquire evidence through iterative questioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reasoning-centric LLMs operate on a passive-inference pattern assuming complete information, whereas real-world clinical diagnosis requires strategic, iterative evidence acquisition. This gap motivates formalizing diagnosis as an iterative evidence-seeking task.

Method: The framework formalizes medical diagnosis as an Iterative Evidence-Seeking Task, employs RLVR to elicit intrinsic reasoning within a closed-loop environment using a novel suite of rewards enforcing diagnostic precision and examination consistency, and introduces RAGES as a high-fidelity clinical oracle for realistic follow-up evidence.

Result: The proposed model achieves comparable performance to larger and reasoning-enhanced baselines. RAGES demonstrates superiority over vanilla LLMs in generating biologically plausible clinical feedback.

Conclusion: The framework enables LLMs to transition from passive responders to autonomous clinical assistants capable of iterative evidence-seeking behavior, bridging the gap between passive inference and real-world iterative diagnostic processes.

Abstract: Recent reasoning-centric Large Language Models (LLMs) have made significant strides, yet they predominantly operate on a passive-inference pattern that assumes complete information. In contrast, real-world clinical intelligence is inherently an iterative investigative process requiring strategic evidence acquisition. To bridge this gap, we formalize medical diagnosis as an Iterative Evidence-Seeking Task. We leverage Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) to elicit intrinsic reasoning within a closed-loop environment, guided by a novel suite of rewards that enforce diagnostic precision and examination consistency. To facilitate this, we introduce the Retrieval-Augmented Generation-based Examination Simulator (RAGES), a high-fidelity clinical oracle that provides realistic, knowledge-grounded follow-up evidence. Empirical results across diverse datasets demonstrate that our framework enables LLMs to transition from passive responders to autonomous assistants. Notably, our model demonstrates comparable performance to larger and reasoning-enhanced baselines, while RAGES proves superior to vanilla LLMs in generating biologically plausible clinical feedback.

</details>


### [13] [Beyond Forecasting: The Belief-to-Trade Layer in Prediction-Market Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03015)
*Yishu Wang,Yuxuan Wang,Jiaqi Deng,Hanyang Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Raven-Agent is the first autonomous trading agent for prediction markets that achieves positive returns and risk-adjusted returns, outperforming all tested policies on a controlled replay of archived decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecasting future events is a testbed for general-purpose AI, but existing benchmarks show a gap between calibrated probability scores and actual trading results. There is a need for autonomous agents that can trade effectively in prediction markets.

Method: The authors propose Raven-Agent, an autonomous trading agent designed for prediction markets. They evaluate it via controlled replay over an archived decision set.

Result: Raven-Agent achieves the only positive return and the only positive risk-adjusted return among all tested policies.

Conclusion: Raven-Agent demonstrates that autonomous trading agents can effectively bridge the gap between probabilistic forecasting and profitable trading in prediction markets, outperforming existing policies.

Abstract: Forecasting future events has attracted growing attention as a testbed for general-purpose AI. A natural way to ground this evaluation is let the models trade in the prediction markets. Trading, however, requires more than forecasting. Moreover, recent benchmarks report a substantial gap between calibrated probability scores and the trading results. We propose Raven-Agent, to the best of our knowledge, the first autonomous trading agent for prediction markets. On a controlled replay over an archived decision set, our architecture achieves the only positive return and the only positive risk-adjusted return among all tested policies. We have released our code in https://github.com/Alchemist-X/predict-raven .

</details>


### [14] [Human-Centric Reflective Architecture for Human-AI Collaborative Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03025)
*Andreas Kouridakis,Dimitrios Patiniotis Spyropoulos,George Vouros*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces HCRA, a human-AI collaborative decision-making framework that frames the task as a stochastic game and uses reinforcement learning with linguistic feedback to improve alignment with human expectations and reduce over/under-reliance on AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humans frequently over- or under-rely on AI recommendations, and current AI systems are poorly calibrated to human expectations, especially in safety-critical applications where non-determinism poses risks.

Method: Formulates collaborative decision-making as a stochastic game between an AI agent and a human player; proposes HCRA integrating human-calibrated models with reinforcement learning agents leveraging iterative linguistic feedback.

Result: HCRA enhances decision-making effectiveness and delivers high-quality recommendations.

Conclusion: The proposed HCRA framework improves human-AI collaboration by better aligning AI recommendations with human preferences, reducing mis-reliance, and improving decision outcomes.

Abstract: The use of Large Language Models (LLMs) across diverse areas of human activity-ranging from everyday tasks to safety-critical applications-aims to enhance decision-making effectiveness with minimal human feedback. Concurrently, it seeks to align decisions with human expectations, preferences, and needs while mitigating risks associated with AI non-determinism. However, humans frequently over- or under-rely on AI recommendations, and current AI systems remain poorly calibrated to human expectations. To address these challenges, we introduce a human-AI collaborative decision-making framework designed to augment human capabilities and align AI agents with human preferences and expectations. Specifically, this paper (a) formulates the collaborative decision-making task as a stochastic game between an AI agent and a human player, and (b) proposes the Human-Centric Reflective Architecture (HCRA), which integrates human-calibrated models with reinforcement learning agents that leverage linguistic feedback in an iterative, reflective process. Evaluation results demonstrate that HCRA enhances decision-making effectiveness and delivers high-quality recommendations.

</details>


### [15] [Silicon Sampling via Cross-Survey Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03091)
*Chan-Tung Ku,Chan Hsu,Pei-Cing Huang,Frank Cheng-shan Liu,I-Ling Cheng,Yihuang Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes cross-survey transfer as a rigorous framework for evaluating LLM-based "silicon sampling" of human survey respondents, finding that zero-shot LLMs achieve 52% accuracy (within 6pp of supervised random forest), revealing a construct predictability hierarchy (67% for partisan attitudes to 23% for sovereignty), and showing that variance collapse and safety alignment effects are more nuanced than previously reported.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLMs as survey respondents rely on distributional comparisons rather than individual-level prediction, which risks conflating pattern matching with coherent respondent-level prediction. A more rigorous evaluation framework is needed.

Method: Cross-survey transfer evaluation: LLMs are given a respondent's answers to one set of questions and must predict answers to entirely different questions from the same survey. Data from TEDS 2024, three open-weight LLMs (27B-120B parameters), with supervised machine learning baselines.

Result: (1) Zero-shot LLMs achieve 52% accuracy on unseen items, within 6pp of supervised random forest; (2) Construct predictability hierarchy: 67% for partisan attitudes to 23% for sovereignty; (3) Variance collapse affects supervised models too, and alignment effects vary dramatically across model families.

Conclusion: The findings clarify both the promise and boundaries of silicon sampling for survey research.

Abstract: Silicon sampling-using large language models (LLMs) to simulate human survey respondents-has emerged as a promising approach for augmenting traditional survey research. However, most evaluations rely on distributional comparisons rather than individual-level prediction, which risks conflating pattern matching with coherent respondent-level prediction. We propose cross-survey transfer, a more rigorous evaluation framework in which an LLM is given a respondent's answers to one set of questions and must predict their answers to entirely different questions from the same survey. Using data from the Taiwan Election and Democratization Study (TEDS) 2024, three open-weight LLMs (27B-120B parameters), and supervised machine learning baselines, we find that: (1) zero-shot LLMs achieve 52% accuracy on genuinely unseen items, closing to within 6 percentage points (pp) of a supervised random forest trained on same-population data; (2) a stable construct predictability hierarchy emerges, from 67% for partisan attitudes to 23% for sovereignty; and (3) variance collapse and safety alignment effects-two commonly cited LLM limitations-turn out to be more nuanced than previously reported, with variance collapse affecting supervised models as well and alignment effects varying dramatically across model families. These findings clarify both the promise and boundaries of silicon sampling.

</details>


### [16] [APeB: Benchmarking Personalization Ability of Large Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03162)
*Garry Yang,Zizhe Chen,Xinru Chen,Yongqiang Chen,Jianxiang Wang,Deyu Zou,Linyi Ding,Jialiang Wu,Yunzhong He,Yu Gong,James Cheng,Huaixiao Tou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents struggle to personalize under raw, underspecified queries due to poor history utilization. The paper introduces the Personalized Product Search (PPS) testbed and APeB benchmark, showing that models fail at early-stage queries requiring intent discovery. A history-aware refinement pipeline (VQRA) yields consistent improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-powered agents fail at personalization when given raw, underspecified queries because they must infer latent intent, extract preferences from noisy histories, and rank alternatives — but existing benchmarks rely on refined queries or simplified histories, failing to test this critical capability.

Method: The authors (1) define the Personalized Product Search (PPS) testbed for agentic personalization; (2) construct the Agent Personalized Benchmark (APeB) from action logs with underspecified intents, rich histories, and candidate items; (3) evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs with multi-step agent workflows; and (4) propose VQRA, a history-aware query-refinement pipeline.

Result: Models perform well on explicit queries but struggle significantly on early-stage queries requiring intent/preference discovery. Rubric analysis shows the primary failure is ineffective use of history. VQRA yields consistent gains across settings.

Conclusion: Dedicated history-utilization modules are critical for personalized LLM agents. The PPS/APeB benchmark reveals a key gap, and VQRA demonstrates that explicitly incorporating history into query refinement can bridge it.

Abstract: LLM-powered agents struggle with personalization when users issue raw, underspecified queries. In this setting, agents must infer latent intent, extract preferences from noisy interaction histories, and select among competing alternatives. Existing benchmarks rarely test this capability, as they often rely on user-refined queries or simplified histories. We introduce personalized product search (PPS), a testbed for agentic personalization under raw queries and diverse histories. We construct Agent Personalized Benchmark (APeB) from action logs, pairing underspecified intents with rich histories and user-viewed candidate items. Evaluating state-of-the-art LLMs with multi-step agent workflows, we find that models handle explicit queries well but struggle with early-stage queries requiring intent and preference discovery. Rubric analysis attributes this gap mainly to ineffective history use. A simple history-aware query-refinement pipeline, VQRA, yields consistent gains, highlighting the need for dedicated history-utilization modules in personalized agents.

</details>


### [17] [Organizational Memory for Agentic Business Process Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03228)
*Lukas Kirchdorfer,Adrian Rebmann,Christian Warmuth,Timotheus Kampik,Theiss Heilker,Gregor Berg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an organizational memory architecture—a shared, governed, agent-consumable reference layer of evolving procedural knowledge—to overcome scalability and knowledge silo challenges when using LLM-based agents for business process execution in enterprises.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose LLMs lack organization-specific knowledge needed for reliable business process execution. Existing approaches of encoding this knowledge into individual prompts or agent-specific retrieval setups do not scale in enterprises, creating knowledge silos, rule duplicates, and difficulties in consistent updates and learning across agents.

Method: The authors derive requirements for an organizational memory, propose an architecture for its curation and consumption, and validate via a proof-of-concept based on a procurement scenario.

Result: The proof-of-concept demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed organizational memory architecture for agentic business process execution in a procurement context.

Conclusion: A shared, governed organizational memory is needed to enable scalable, consistent, and maintainable LLM-based agent execution of business processes in enterprise settings.

Abstract: LLM-based agents offer new opportunities for automating business process execution beyond the limits of rule-based systems. However, general-purpose LLMs lack the organization-specific knowledge required for reliable execution, which is typically fragmented across human-oriented artifacts such as policies, process models, and standard operating procedures. While such knowledge can technically be encoded in individual prompts or agent-specific retrieval setups, this approach does not scale in enterprises, as it gives rise to knowledge silos and rule duplicates, and makes consistent updates and learning across agents difficult. We argue that this calls for an organizational memory for agentic business process execution: a shared, governed, and agent-consumable reference layer of evolving organization-specific procedural knowledge about how work should be executed. We derive requirements for such a memory, propose an architecture for its curation and consumption, and demonstrate its effectiveness in a proof-of-concept based on a procurement scenario.

</details>


### [18] [Embodied Operators and Benchmarking: Toward Reusable and Deployable Embodied Intelligence Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03283)
*Junwu Xiong,Jiaxuan Gao,Wei Chai,Renxing Chen,Yuzhen Li,Yu Guo,Yucheng Guo,Mingxi Luo,Wenyang Ma,Yiyun Mou,Yifei Zhang,Chen Zhou,Yongjian Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper defines "embodied operators" as reusable, composable functional modules in embodied intelligence pipelines (e.g., detection, spatial understanding, hand motion recovery, task-decision operators, planning/control). It provides a taxonomy, proposes a multi-dimensional benchmark framework, and discusses challenges like composition, standardization, VLA safety, and edge deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied intelligence systems need more than end-to-end policy models; they require reusable functional modules that can be independently optimized, composed, and evaluated across different tasks and platforms.

Method: The paper defines embodied operators with clear boundaries (task semantics, standardized I/O contracts, deployability, reusability, multi-layer optimizability), constructs a five-category taxonomy, and proposes a multi-dimensional benchmark (correctness, efficiency, resource usage, temporal stability, portability, interface compatibility, deployment reliability, downstream utility).

Result: Five categories identified: (1) detection and segmentation, (2) spatial localization and 3D understanding, (3) hand motion recovery, (4) embodied foundation models and task-decision operators, (5) planning, control, and system support operators. A multi-dimensional benchmark framework is proposed.

Conclusion: Embodied operators should be optimized and evaluated as holistic deployable components, forming a foundation for reusable, scalable, and verifiable embodied intelligence systems. Open challenges remain in operator composition, data standardization, world models, VLA safety, and edge deployment.

Abstract: Embodied intelligence systems require not only end-to-end policy models, but also reusable functional modules that transform multimodal observations, robot states, human demonstrations, and task contexts into structured representations, decisions, trajectories, control references, and system services. This work defines these modules as embodied operators and studies them as independent yet composable units in embodied intelligence pipelines. We clarify their definition boundary, emphasizing task semantics, standardized input-output contracts, deployability, reusability, and multi-layer optimizability. We further construct a taxonomy covering five categories: detection and segmentation, spatial localization and 3D understanding, hand motion recovery, embodied foundation models and task-decision operators, and planning, control, and system support operators. For each category, we summarize representative functions, technical paradigms, application roles, and practical limitations. Beyond taxonomy, we propose a multi-dimensional benchmark framework that evaluates embodied operators in terms of correctness, end-to-end efficiency, resource usage, temporal stability, portability, interface compatibility, deployment reliability, and downstream task utility. We also discuss workflow-level operator acceleration and open challenges in operator composition, data standardization, world models, VLA safety, edge deployment, and real-world application value. Overall, this work argues that embodied operators should be optimized and evaluated as holistic deployable components, providing a foundation for reusable, scalable, and verifiable embodied intelligence systems.

</details>


### [19] [Reflective Dialogue or Prompt Refinement? Effects of Tutor Scaffolding on Students' Independent LLM Use for Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03303)
*Jerome Brender,Laila El-Hamamsy,Kim Uittenhove,Aitor Perez,Patrick Jermann,Francesco Mondada,Engin Bumbacher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study compares Socratic-Guidance (SG) and Prompt-Refinement (PR) LLM tutors in a graduate robotics course, finding that SG tutors lead to higher learning gains and more understanding-driven prompting strategies when students later use unconstrained LLMs, despite being perceived as less efficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLMs offer personalized learning support, concerns exist about their educational use. Learning outcomes depend on how students engage with LLMs, motivating investigation into how different LLM tutor designs shape prompting practices and learning.

Method: Two-phase study in a graduate-level mobile robotics course: Phase 1 (6-week intervention) had 66 students using either a Socratic-Guidance (SG) tutor or Prompt-Refinement (PR) tutor; Phase 2 (3-week project) had 52 students using an unconstrained LLM. Learning outcomes, prompting patterns, and subsequent LLM-use were measured.

Result: SG and PR tutors led to similar task performance and prompting patterns during guided use. However, SG students achieved higher learning gains in later sessions and adopted more understanding-driven prompting strategies when using unconstrained LLMs, which predicted higher understanding. Learners perceived the SG tutor as less efficient.

Conclusion: Socratic guidance supports the development of students' capacity to learn with LLMs over time, highlighting its importance for LLM tutor design despite perceived inefficiency.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) can provide personalized support in learning, several studies have raised concerns regarding their use in education. Importantly, learning depends on how students engage with LLMs. This study examined how two types of LLM-based tutors shape students' prompting practices, learning, and subsequent LLM-use: a Socratic-Guidance (SG) tutor, which structures interaction through dialogic questioning, and a Prompt-Refinement (PR) tutor that guides the formulation of effective prompts. We conducted a two-phase study in a graduate-level mobile robotics course: 66 students used either the SG or PR tutor during a 6-week intervention, followed by 52 students using an unconstrained LLM during a 3-week course project. Results show that while the SG- and PR tutors led to similar task performance and prompting patterns during guided use, they differ in learning outcomes and later LLM-use. SG-students, relative to PR-student, achieved higher learning gains in later sessions, and were more likely to adopt understanding-driven prompting strategies, which are predictive of higher understanding, when using an unconstrained LLM. Although learners perceived the SG tutor as less efficient, the findings suggest that Socratic guidance supports the development of students' capacity to learn with LLMs over time, highlighting its importance for LLM tutor design.

</details>


### [20] [When Aggregate Alignment Misleads: Auditing Policy Repair Without Per-State Expert Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03386)
*Peiying Zhu,Sidi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An LLM-based agentic policy editor, using only region-level diagnostic feedback (not per-state labels), can repair pricing policies in a hotel simulator to near-benchmark performance (RevPAR 108.47 vs. 108.75). The editor's key contribution is reducing episode composition distance rather than raw revenue lift alone, and it outperforms non-semantic proposers and shuffled-diagnostic controls, though a tree editor achieves better alignment at the cost of revenue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating agentic policy edits is difficult when per-state expert action labels are unavailable; this work studies policy repair under only region-level diagnostic feedback.

Method: A multi-restart LLM editor proposes constrained edits to a target-action table using region-level diagnostic feedback (price distribution summaries vs. a benchmark) in a hotel-pricing simulator. Comparisons are made against non-semantic proposers, shuffled-diagnostic controls, and a tree editor.

Result: The LLM editor achieves RevPAR 108.47 (95% CI 107.61–109.34), close to benchmark 108.75, with a paired gap of -0.276 (95% CI [-0.692, 0.146]). It reduces episode composition distance from 1.153 to 0.609. Non-semantic proposers fall 8.77–14.57 RevPAR short; shuffled-diagnostic control drops to 94.30. A tree editor achieves better alignment but revenue falls to 98.91.

Conclusion: Agentic policy repair should be evaluated by whether diagnostic feedback becomes a reliable closed-loop outcome, not by a single behavioral distance metric.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems are increasingly used to edit, refine, and repair decision policies, but evaluating these edits is difficult when per-state expert action labels are unavailable. We study this problem in a hotel-pricing simulator where an agentic policy editor receives only region-level diagnostic feedback: summaries of how its price distribution differs from a benchmark policy across time, inventory, and market regions. The editor cannot observe benchmark actions, benchmark source code, reward numbers, or held-out outcomes, and may only propose constrained edits to a target-action table. On 5,000 held-out episodes, a multi-restart LLM editor reaches RevPAR 108.47 (95% CI 107.61 - 109.34), close to the benchmark policy's 108.75 (107.81 - 109.68), with paired gap (LLM minus benchmark) -0.276 and 95% CI [-0.692, 0.146]. A cheap diagnostic projection already recovers much of the revenue (107.90), so the LLM editor's distinctive gain is not raw revenue lift alone: it also reduces episode composition distance from 1.153 to 0.609. This is the strongest non-benchmark repair result. This profile is not explained by restart search alone: non-semantic proposers with up to 2,500 evaluations fall 8.77 - 14.57 RevPAR points short. Nor is it explained by plausible prompt format: a shuffled-diagnostic control breaks region-error correspondence and falls to RevPAR 94.30. The match is genuine but partial. A tree editor achieves stronger pooled alignment, 0.214 versus 0.266, and stronger reference-state D1, 0.328 versus 1.197, yet revenue falls to 98.91. These results show that agentic policy repair should be evaluated by whether diagnostic feedback becomes reliable closed-loop outcome, not by a single behavioral distance.

</details>


### [21] [From Mobile Data to Business Insights: An End-to-End Analytics Framework for Large-Scale Urban Mobility Analysis and Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03394)
*Thiago Andrade,Shazia Tabassum,Miguel E. P. Silva,Ricardo Dinis,Joao Gama*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a comprehensive data platform leveraging mobile application location data, built with Google BigQuery, Vertex AI, and Power BI, to analyze urban mobility patterns across tourism, transportation, and retail domains, producing actionable insights for urban planning and business strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address diverse urban challenges (tourism planning, parking, bus routing, resource allocation) and commercial needs (location-based services, market analysis, behavioral profiling) by harnessing real-time mobile location data, which offers fine-grained spatiotemporal insights not traditionally available.

Method: The approach involves building a modular data platform from scratch: formulating use cases, designing architecture, implementing ETL pipelines, employing data anonymization, using Google BigQuery for processing and Vertex AI for ML model development, and applying Power BI for interactive visualization. A modular architecture of reusable analytical building blocks generates data products for multiple use cases.

Result: The framework effectively captures user mobility dynamics at fine spatial and temporal resolutions. Models developed include mobility profiling, frequent trajectory mining, area of influence analysis, traffic anomaly detection, and origin-destination pattern analysis.

Conclusion: The platform provides actionable insights for urban planning and strategic business decision-making, demonstrating its value across tourism, transportation, and retail domains through scalable, reusable analytical components.

Abstract: Real time location data derived from mobile applications is a powerful tool for addressing various urban challenges, including tourism planning, parking management, bus route optimization, and resource allocation. Besides, it offers invaluable insights for shaping strategic decisions in commercial domains such as location based services, market share analysis, and behavioral profiling. In this expansive study, we aim to address all of the aforementioned challenges by investigating the behaviors and patterns of smartphone users within urban environments, particularly in the domains of tourism, transportation, and retail. Our approach encompasses the development of a sophisticated data platform from inception to implementation, which includes the formulation of use cases, architectural design, and implementation of modules. We employ state of the art techniques and technologies, including data anonymization, ETL pipelines, and utilizing Google BigQuery and Vertex AI for data processing and machine learning model development. A modular architecture based on reusable analytical building blocks was developed to generate data products that support multiple stakeholder driven use cases. Additionally, we apply interactive data visualization techniques via Power BI to facilitate the effective interpretation of analytical findings by stakeholders. The developed models address a wide range of mobility analytics tasks, including mobility profiling, frequent trajectory mining, area of influence analysis, traffic anomaly detection, and origin destination pattern analysis. The results demonstrate the framework's ability to capture user mobility dynamics at fine spatial and temporal resolutions, providing actionable insights for urban planning and strategic business decision making.

</details>


### [22] [Efficient bias mitigation in T2I diffusion models using Concept Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03397)
*Mansi,Avinash Kori,Francesco Leofante*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CO-ALIGN is a bias mitigation method for Text-to-Image diffusion models that aligns concepts within the model's internal ontology (text encoder and denoiser) to reduce harmful bias while preserving generative quality, achieving 30% fairness improvement, ΔFID=11.4, 2.8% fidelity gain, and 88% reduction in incoherent outputs, while also benefiting downstream tasks like concept unlearning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing bias mitigation techniques in Text-to-Image diffusion models either intervene only at the text encoder or provide inference-time guidance, often resulting in semantically incoherent outputs. There is a need for a method that reduces bias without collapsing generative quality.

Method: CO-ALIGN (Concept Ontology Alignment) performs concept-graph alignment on the model's internal concept ontology, operating across three paradigms: text-encoder alignment, denoiser alignment, and joint text-denoiser ontology alignment.

Result: CO-ALIGN outperforms state-of-the-art methods, improving fairness by 30%, achieving ΔFID=11.4 in image quality, 2.8% improvement in image fidelity, and reducing semantically incoherent outputs by 88%. It also enhances concept unlearning robustness across multiple unlearning techniques.

Conclusion: Concept-graph alignment is an effective approach for bias mitigation in diffusion models, and better-aligned internal ontologies yield benefits for downstream tasks such as concept unlearning.

Abstract: Text-to-Image diffusion models often propagate harmful bias inherited from the training data. Existing bias mitigation techniques typically intervene only at the text encoder or provide inference-time guidance, often leading to generations that collapse into semantically incoherent outputs. To address these limitations, we introduce CO-ALIGN (Concept Ontology Alignment), a novel bias mitigation approach based on concept-graph alignment that operates on the model's internal concept ontology. By aligning concepts within the text encoder and denoiser, CO-ALIGN achieves substantial bias reduction while preserving generative integrity. We demonstrate the effectiveness of concept-graph alignment across three paradigms: text-encoders, denoisers and joint text-denoiser ontology alignment. CO-ALIGN outperforms the state of the art, improving fairness by $30\%$, $ΔFID=11.4$ in image quality, $2.8\%$ in image fidelity, all while reducing semantically incoherent outputs by $88\%$. Beyond bias mitigation, we show that CO-ALIGN benefits other downstream tasks as well. In particular, our experiments demonstrate that better-aligned internal ontologies enhance concept unlearning robustness across multiple unlearning techniques.

</details>


### [23] [Personalized Causal Recourse: A Human-In-The-Loop Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03425)
*Denise Tampieri,Giovanni De Toni,Paolo Giudici*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a human-in-the-loop framework that uses Bayesian inference to iteratively approximate a user's structural causal model through interactive queries, enabling personalized algorithmic recourse that is plausible, cost-effective, and aligned with individual causal dependencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional algorithmic recourse methods rely on closest counterfactual explanations or assume prior knowledge of causal structures, overlooking individual contexts and specific feature interactions. This limits personalization and practical applicability.

Method: A human-in-the-loop framework that iteratively approximates the user's structural causal model via interactive queries and Bayesian inference, using human feedback to refine causal effect identification before generating recourse recommendations.

Result: Simulations across linear and non-linear causal models show promising results, but challenges remain in capturing complex non-linear structures, highlighting the need for accurate approximations and robust noise distribution modeling.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables personalized, plausible, and cost-effective algorithmic recourse that aligns with individual causal dependencies, though further work is needed to handle non-linear complexities.

Abstract: Algorithmic recourse addresses the challenge of providing tailored recommendations to users affected by unfavorable machine learning decisions, in potentially high-stakes scenarios. Traditional approaches to recourse often rely on the closest counterfactual explanations or assume a priori knowledge of a user's causal structure, resulting in interventions that overlook individual contexts and specific feature interactions. To overcome these limitations, we study a human-in-the-loop framework that iteratively approximates the user's structural causal model through interactive queries via Bayesian inference before producing recourse recommendations. This framework exploits humans' feedback to improve the identification of causal effects, allowing personalized recourse that is plausible, cost-effective, and aligned with the actual causal dependencies of each user. As a proof of concept, we evaluate this framework through simulated human responses. Our simulations across linear and non-linear causal models show promising results, though challenges remain in capturing complex, non-linear structures, emphasizing the importance of accurate approximations and robust noise distribution modeling.

</details>


### [24] [Demonstrating Generalization Failures via Mixtures of Conditional Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03478)
*Jou Barzdukas,Jack Peck,Julian Schulz,Paulius Rauba,Steven Basart,Lennie Wells*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method to construct language models that exhibit controllable generalization failures when trained with RL by using SFT on a mixture of conditional policies, demonstrating that RL training can actively degrade performance on out-of-distribution tasks (even to zero), and illustrating two novel failure modes related to task coverage and temporal context distribution shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training of frontier LMs on curated task suites creates distribution shifts between training and deployment, leading to poorly understood generalization failures. The community needs clean, simplified demonstrations to better study such failures.

Method: The authors use Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) on a dataset of mixed transcripts from a collection of 'conditional policies,' each independently assignable behaviors per task distribution. This yields a model approximating a 'mixture of conditional policies.' RL training is then applied on a given training distribution.

Result: RL training selects policies that maximize reward on the training distribution, which can produce striking failures: e.g., in a controlled setting with two identical question sets prepended with different trigger strings, RL on either distribution actively degrades performance on the other to zero. Two additional failure modes (task coverage shift and temporal context shift) are demonstrated.

Conclusion: The constructed 'model organisms' serve as existence proofs that training success and generalization can systematically come apart, providing useful tools for alignment stress-testing and generalization science, though they may not closely resemble natural failures.

Abstract: Post-training of frontier language models is conducted on curated task suites, and inevitably leaves a distribution shift between training and deployment environments. This exposes developers to generalization failures, which are relatively poorly understood. To better understand such generalization failures, we believe the community should construct clean demonstrations under simplified conditions. To facilitate this, we propose a simple and flexible way to construct language models which fail to generalize in controllable ways when subsequently trained with Reinforcement Learning (RL) on a given distribution of training tasks. Our construction uses Supervised Fine-Tuning on a dataset of a mixture of transcripts corresponding to a collection of 'conditional policies', which can each independently be assigned certain behaviors on each different task distribution, to obtain a model that is then well approximated as a 'mixture of conditional policies.' We observe that RL training then selects for policies that obtain the highest reward on the training distribution. This can produce striking behaviors: in a controlled setting with two distributions containing identical questions prepended with two different 'trigger strings', RL training on either distribution actively degrades performance on the other to zero, even though the underlying task is identical. We also use our construction to illustrate two novel ways in which generalization may fail in future language models, corresponding to distribution shifts of task coverage and temporal context respectively. While our construction is deliberately simple and may not closely resemble 'natural' generalization failures, the resulting 'model organisms' are of interest for alignment stress-testing and generalization science, and can be used as existence proofs that training success and generalization can come apart in structured ways.

</details>


### [25] [MentalThink: Shaping Thoughts in Mental SVG World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03530)
*Kangheng Lin,Jisheng Yin,Dingming Li,En Yu,Yana Wei,Han Zhou,Liang Zhao,Hongyu Zhou,Hongbo Peng,Jianjian Sun,Zheng Ge,Xiangyu Zhang,Daxin Jiang,Jingyu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MentalThink is a visual-symbolic reasoning paradigm that uses SVG code generation, rendering, and interpretation as an intermediate visual workspace for multimodal LLMs, trained via SFT + multi-turn RL, achieving strong results on spatial reasoning benchmarks (55.1% VSIBench, 76.0% MindCube).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Multimodal LLMs lack an explicit "mental visualization" mechanism for dynamic spatial reasoning, unlike humans who can form, inspect, and manipulate mental imagery when solving spatial problems.

Method: A think-with-SVG pipeline: MLLMs generate SVG code as intermediate visual representations, render them deterministically, and interpret the rendered graphics for multi-turn reasoning. Trained with two-stage framework: (1) Supervised Fine-Tuning for SVG syntactic alignment, (2) multi-turn Reinforcement Learning for iterative inspection, revision, and refinement of visual hypotheses.

Result: 55.1% on VSIBench, 76.0% on MindCube, outperforming prior methods on spatial understanding and reasoning benchmarks.

Conclusion: Executable vector graphics (SVG) provide a verifiable visual workspace that enables dynamic perspective taking, visual reflection, and compositional scene construction in MLLMs, effectively mimicking human mental imagery processes.

Abstract: We introduce MentalThink, a visual-symbolic reasoning paradigm that equips Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) with an executable mechanism for "mental" visualization. The core of MentalThink is a think-with-SVG pipeline, where the model learns to generate, render, and interpret scalable vector graphics (SVG) code as an intermediate visual representation for multi-turn reasoning. By creating structured vector sketches, the model can externalize spatial hypotheses, inspect them through deterministic rendering, and reason within a constrained geometric space, effectively mimicking the human process of mental imagery. We instantiate this paradigm through a two-stage training framework, combining Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) for SVG syntactic alignment with multi-turn Reinforcement Learning (RL) to encourage iterative inspection, revision, and refinement of intermediate visual hypotheses. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that MentalThink achieves superior performance on spatial understanding and reasoning benchmarks (e.g., 55.1% on VSIBench, 76.0% on MindCube), showing that executable vector graphics provide a verifiable visual workspace for dynamic perspective taking, visual reflection, and compositional scene construction.

</details>


### [26] [Applying Answer Set Programming with Fuzzy Membership Functions: a Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03550)
*Luca Ferragina,Ilenia Galati,Lorena Gullone,Francesco Scarcello*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper explores a fuzzy-logic-based qualitative extension of Answer Set Programming (ASP) that bridges numerical information and qualitative reasoning, using learning-based membership functions and semantically enriched predicates to combine expert knowledge, context, and subjective interpretations in a unified declarative framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human reasoning relies on vague, context-dependent qualitative concepts (e.g., "high," "cheap") rooted in numerical data, yet existing frameworks often impose rigid thresholds. The paper aims to bridge numerical information and qualitative reasoning through a principled fuzzy-logic-based approach.

Method: The paper presents a fuzzy-logic-based qualitative extension of Answer Set Programming (ASP) that integrates numerically grounded inputs (e.g., from machine learning models) with symbolic reasoning. Key features include learning-based membership functions and semantically enriched predicates, enabling the combination of expert knowledge, contextual factors, and subjective interpretations within a unified declarative setting.

Result: The framework avoids rigid thresholds and supports robust reasoning under vagueness. A representative use case demonstrates how numerically grounded inputs are integrated with symbolic reasoning over qualitative labels.

Conclusion: The proposed fuzzy-logic-based ASP extension provides a principled framework for robust qualitative reasoning under vagueness, bridging numerical data and symbolic reasoning while incorporating expert knowledge and context.

Abstract: Human reasoning often operates through qualitative concepts expressed by linguistic labels such as high, low, expensive, or cheap, whose interpretation depends on context and is usually vague, despite being rooted in numerical data. This paper explores a novel fuzzy-logic-based qualitative extension of Answer Set Programming (ASP) to bridge numerical information and qualitative reasoning.
