The underlying language, formally introduced in a separate work, provides a principled framework that avoids rigid thresholds and supports robust reasoning under vagueness. Focusing on a representative use case, we illustrate how the framework integrates numerically grounded inputs (such as outputs of machine learning models) with symbolic reasoning over qualitative labels. Key features, including learning-based membership functions and semantically enriched predicates, enable the combination of expert knowledge, contextual factors, and subjective interpretations within a unified declarative setting.

</details>


### [27] [How to Avoid Debate: Scalable AI Safety via Doubly-Efficient Interactive Proofs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03561)
*Liyan Chen,Yael Tauman Kalai,Zoe Xi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces single-prover interactive proofs for AI safety verification, eliminating the need for debate between two equally capable AI models. The authors develop doubly-efficient interactive proofs for oracle-aided computations under conditions of robustness (noise-tolerant) or low-degree polynomial oracles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing debate-based verification methods assume two equally powerful AI models with one being truthful, which is unrealistic. The authors aim to remove this assumption by developing single-prover interactive proofs suitable for AI safety verification.

Method: The paper presents doubly-efficient single-prover interactive proofs and arguments for oracle-aided computations (relativizing proofs) in two settings: (1) robust computations where output is unchanged under small fractions of incorrect oracle answers, and (2) oracles that are low-degree polynomials.

Result: Single-prover interactive proofs can work for AI safety without debate, under conditions of structured or noise-tolerant oracle access.

Conclusion: Interactive verification is possible without debate, provided the oracle access is either noise-tolerant or low-degree polynomial structured.

Abstract: As AI models continue to develop powerful capabilities, it becomes critical that we are able to verify that their output is aligned with our intentions. A recent line of work focuses on verification via debate, a model of interactive proofs where two competing powerful provers, or AI models, debate each other to convince a weak verifier, or a human, of the correctness of their claim. However, debate assumes that the two AI models possess equal abilities and that one of them is truthful, which may not be realistic.
  In this work, we show \emph{how to avoid debate}: we initiate the study of \emph{single-prover} interactive proofs for AI safety. Prior results in single-prover interactive proofs do not immediately carry over to the AI safety setting: for example, they do not work when the computation has access to an oracle, such as to human judgment or an external database such as the web. We present doubly-efficient single-prover interactive proofs and arguments for oracle-aided computations (also known as relativizing proofs), in the settings where (1) the computation is robust, in the sense that the output does not change if at most a small fraction of the answers to oracle queries are incorrect, or (2) the oracle is a low-degree polynomial. These results suggest that interactive verification is possible even without debate, under structured or noise-tolerant oracle access.

</details>


### [28] [The Role of Rigor in Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03634)
*Timothy Nguyen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes AI through a three-part framework of conceptual, epistemic, and operational rigor, arguing that modern deep learning's primacy of operational rigor explains both its rapid progress and persistent limitations, and clarifies challenges for maturing AI into a proper science.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI has achieved remarkable capabilities without the conceptual and scientific foundations typical of mature disciplines, creating a tension between performance-driven progress and scientific understanding that motivates a systematic analysis of rigor in AI.

Method: The authors introduce a three-part framework—conceptual rigor (clarifying foundational concepts), epistemic rigor (establishing scientific understanding), and operational rigor (ensuring reliable performance and deployment)—and use it to analyze conceptions of intelligence, empirical deep learning approaches, benchmarks, and theory development obstacles.

Result: The analysis reveals that the distinctive trajectory of AI arises from how forms of rigor interact across paradigms, with operational rigor dominating in modern deep learning, which explains both AI's rapid advances and persistent uncertainties.

Conclusion: The framework helps clarify the challenges involved in transforming AI into a mature science and reliable technology, highlighting the need to balance operational rigor with conceptual and epistemic rigor.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence (AI) has achieved extraordinary capabilities despite lacking many of the conceptual and scientific foundations associated with mature disciplines. Unlike traditional sciences, where reliable technology typically emerges from theoretical understanding, modern AI has progressed largely through performance-driven iteration and "alchemical" experimentation. This tension motivates a systematic analysis of AI through the lens of rigor. We introduce a three-part framework consisting of conceptual rigor (clarifying foundational concepts), epistemic rigor (establishing scientific understanding), and operational rigor (ensuring reliable performance and deployment). Using this framework, we analyze competing conceptions of intelligence and understanding, the strengths and limitations of the empirical approach to deep learning, the power and pitfalls of benchmarks, and the obstacles to theory development posed by modern AI systems. We argue that the distinctive trajectory of AI arises from how forms of rigor interact across paradigms, resulting in the primacy of operational rigor in modern deep learning. This perspective helps explain both AI's rapid advances and its persistent uncertainties, while clarifying the challenges involved in transforming AI into a mature science and reliable technology.

</details>


### [29] [Robust Feasible Route Construction through Collaborative Partition Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03694)
*Oguzhan Karaahmetoglu,Hyong Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoRC is a routing framework that enables independently solved CVRP subproblems to exchange customers and vehicles during optimization, consistently producing feasible solutions for large-scale instances (up to 200,000 customers) where partition-based and end-to-end methods fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale CVRPs are typically solved by partitioning into independent subproblems, but this can leave customer demand unserved even when sufficient fleet resources exist elsewhere.

Method: Collaborative Routing Constructors (CoRC) allows subproblems to exchange customers and vehicles dynamically during optimization rather than relying on a fixed partition or post-hoc global re-optimization.

Result: CoRC consistently constructs feasible solutions across all partitioning strategies on AGS and synthetic instances up to 200,000 customers, outperforming independent routing, post-routing re-optimization, and state-of-the-art end-to-end frameworks.

Conclusion: Collaboration between routing subproblems offers a robust and scalable approach for feasible large-scale route construction.

Abstract: Large-scale Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problems (CVRPs) are commonly solved by partitioning customers into smaller routing problems that can be optimized independently. While this substantially reduces computational complexity, independently constructed routing solutions may leave some customer demand unserved even when sufficient resources exist elsewhere in the fleet. We present Collaborative Routing Constructors (CoRC), a routing framework that enables independently solved subproblems to exchange customers and vehicles during optimization rather than relying solely on a fixed partition or a subsequent global re-optimization stage. Computational experiments on AGS benchmark instances and synthetic instances containing up to 200,000 customers compare CoRC against independent routing, post-routing global re-optimization, and state-of-the-art, end-to-end routing frameworks. Across all evaluated partitioning strategies, CoRC consistently constructs feasible routing solutions where competing partition-based methods do not. Furthermore, it remains effective on problem instances for which the evaluated end-to-end routing frameworks did not produce solutions under the same computational budget. These results demonstrate that collaboration between routing subproblems provides a robust and scalable approach for feasible large-scale route construction.

</details>


### [30] [Agent Reinforcement Learning via Pivotal-Aware Self-Feedback Retry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03702)
*Weiyang Guo,Zesheng Shi,Longhui Zhang,Zeen Zhu,Min Zhang,Jing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PivoARL is a self-feedback retry framework for LLM agents that identifies pivotal erroneous turns and performs local retry only from those states, reducing interaction costs while improving task success rates through pivotal-aware credit assignment and implicit reflection returns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents struggle to effectively leverage failed trajectories: full retries incur high interaction costs, while experience retrieval tends to dilute critical experience signals.

Method: PivoARL identifies pivotal erroneous turns via structured reflection, performs local retry from the corresponding pivotal state (reusing correct prefixes), uses pivotal-aware credit assignment that rewards correct prefixes and isolates erroneous suffixes, and optimizes reflection quality through implicit reflection returns.

Result: Significant improvements on Pass@2/3 across all 4 agent tasks and 7 search-based QA benchmarks, with ~11.5% average gain over MetaRL. Improves Pass@1 on over 80% of tasks. On Minesweeper, improves over GiGPO by >45% and reduces interaction turns by ~42% vs full-retry methods.

Conclusion: PivoARL effectively exploits failed trajectories by concentrating useful experience signals near error boundaries, mitigating signal dilution, and achieving both higher success rates and lower interaction costs across diverse agent and QA tasks.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents have shown strong decision-making capabilities in long-horizon interactive tasks, yet they still struggle to effectively leverage failed trajectories: full retries incur high interaction costs, while experience retrieval tends to dilute critical experience signals. To address this, we propose PivoARL, a self-feedback retry framework for experience exploitation in LLM agents. PivoARL identifies the pivotal erroneous turn through structured reflection and performs local retry only from the corresponding pivotal state, thereby reusing the correct prefix and reducing redundant interactions. From an information-gain perspective, we further show that pivotal retry concentrates useful experience signals near the error boundary, mitigating the signal dilution caused by state-agnostic experience utilization. Based on this insight, we design a pivotal-aware credit assignment mechanism that rewards correct prefixes while isolating erroneous suffixes, and optimize reflection quality through implicit reflection returns. We conduct a systematic evaluation on 4 agent tasks and 7 search-based QA benchmarks. Results show that PivoARL achieves significant improvements on Pass@2/3 across all tasks, with an average gain of about 11.5\% over MetaRL. Moreover, benefiting from contrastive preference signals induced by pivotal turns, PivoARL also consistently improves Pass@1 on over 80\% of the tasks. On Minesweeper environment, PivoARL improves over GiGPO by more than 45\% and reduces interaction turns by about 42\% on average compared with full-retry methods. Code is available at https://github.com/yuki-younai/PivoARL.

</details>


### [31] [Explainable Reinforcement Learning for Adaptive Traffic Signal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03703)
*Dickens Kwesiga,Nishu Choudhary,Angshuman Guin,Michael Hunter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces an explainable entity-centric RL framework for traffic signal control that uses dual-stage attention networks to process lane entities and phase configurations, producing interpretable affinity matrices. It integrates deterministic action masking into PPO for safety compliance, outperforms baselines in delay minimization, and yields attention weights that align with traffic engineering principles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep RL for traffic signal control achieves high performance but suffers from black-box opacity, hindering adoption by transportation agencies due to lack of interpretability, regulatory compliance issues, and difficulties in troubleshooting and fine-tuning safety-critical infrastructure.

Method: An entity-centric RL framework that: (1) disaggregates traffic states into lane entities and phase temporal configurations to preserve intersection topology; (2) uses a dual-stage attention network with sequential multi-head cross-attention and self-attention to extract relational dependencies and produce a real-time affinity matrix; (3) integrates deterministic action masking directly into the PPO pipeline to block invalid phase transitions and enforce safety constraints.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in delay minimization in microscopic simulation. The learned attention weights align precisely with established traffic engineering principles, providing full visual and analytical interpretability.

Conclusion: The proposed architecture bridges high-performance RL optimization with human-comprehensible interpretability, offering an auditable, trust-enabling, and deployable solution for next-generation adaptive traffic control systems.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for adaptive traffic signal control. However, in safety-critical infrastructure like traffic control, the opaque, black-box nature of deep RL models poses challenges for transportation agency acceptance, regulatory compliance, operational trust, troubleshooting, and fine-tuning. To bridge this gap between high-performance optimization and human-comprehensible interpretability, this effort introduces a novel, explainable entity centric RL framework for safe and transparent traffic signal control. Rather than processing traffic states through monolithic, flat vectors, the proposed architecture disaggregates real-time intersection observations into distinct, high-dimensional lane entities and phase temporal configurations to inherently preserve the structural topology and geometric configurations of the intersection. Relational dependencies and inter-lane conflicts are dynamically extracted via a dual-stage attention network featuring sequential multi-head cross-attention and self-attention blocks. This design yields a real time affinity matrix that quantifies the direct influence of signal phases on specific approach volumes and queues, providing full visual and analytical interpretability. To ensure strict operational reliability, a deterministic action-masking interface is integrated directly into the Proximal Policy Optimization pipeline, explicitly blocking invalid phase transitions to guarantee absolute compliance with established signal timing and safety constraints. Evaluated in a microscopic simulation environment, outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in delay minimization. More importantly, the emergent attention weights align precisely with established traffic engineering principles, offering an auditable, trust-enabling, and deployable architecture for next-generation adaptive traffic control systems.

</details>


### [32] [Can Conversational Temporal Dynamics Improve Depression Detection in Dyads? A Preliminary Investigation in Multi-Modality Perspectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03744)
*Hanie Kang,Huang-Cheng Chou,Sudarsana Reddy Kadiri,Shrikanth Narayanan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper shows that conversational turn-pair timing between clinician and participant can effectively complement self-supervised semantic/acoustic models for automatic depression detection. A compact 24-dimensional timing module alone outperforms full-sized WavLM-large and RoBERTa-large baselines on the DAIC-WOZ dev set, and late fusion (macro-F1 0.804 dev, 0.669 test) reveals that acoustics contribute zero weight, confirming timing as a lightweight, interpretable modality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing depression detection models leverage semantic content and acoustics from clinical interviews, but the interactional timing (e.g., turn-pair intervals) between clinician and participant is under-explored. The authors aim to investigate whether dyadic conversational dynamics alone can serve as a competitive or complementary modality for depression screening.

Method: The authors construct a 24-dimensional feature module capturing dyadic turn-pair timing (e.g., pause, gap, overlap durations) extracted from the DAIC-WOZ dataset. This timing module is compared against frozen WavLM-large (acoustic) and RoBERTa-large (semantic) encoders. A convex-weighted late fusion strategy is used to combine all three modalities.

Result: The timing module achieves the highest single-modality macro-F1 on the development set, outperforming WavLM-large and RoBERTa-large. Convex-weighted late fusion yields 0.804 (dev) and 0.669 (test) macro-F1. The learned fusion weights assign zero weight to the acoustic modality, indicating that timing subsumes acoustics in this setting.

Conclusion: Conversational turn-pair timing is a lightweight, interpretable, and highly effective modality for dyadic depression screening. It can replace or supplement heavier self-supervised acoustic models, offering practical advantages for real-world clinical deployment.

Abstract: Automatic depression detection from clinical interviews typically models the semantic content and acoustic characteristics of participant speech. However, the interactional timing between the clinician and participant remains comparatively under-modeled. We investigate conversational temporal dynamics, specifically dyadic turn-pair timing, as a primary modality fused with self-supervised encoders. Evaluated on the DAIC-WOZ dataset, we compare a compact 24-dimensional timing module against frozen WavLM-large and RoBERTa-large baseline detectors. This temporal module achieves the highest single-modality performance on the development set. Furthermore, a convex-weighted late fusion strategy improves overall performance to 0.804 and 0.669 macro-F1 on the development and test sets, respectively. The learned fusion effectively assigns zero weight to acoustics, demonstrating that conversational timing serves as a lightweight, interpretable complement for dyadic depression screening.

</details>


### [33] [Bridging Interleaved Multi-Modal Reasoning as a Unified Decision Process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03748)
*Zican Hu,Xuyang Hu,Yiming Liu,Zuwei Long,Wei Liu,Yunzhuo Hao,Jiawei Gu,Linjie Li,Yu Cheng,Zhenhong Sun,Weibo Gu,Xing Sun,Zhi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BRAID is a framework that unifies multi-modal text-image reasoning under a single Markov decision process, enabling joint RL optimization of both text and image generation with dense VLM-based feedback, outperforming baselines on spatial reasoning and visual perception tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods for UMMs only optimize text steps and use supervised surrogates for image generation, preventing policy gradients from flowing through the full interleaved multi-modal trajectory, leaving RL's potential untapped.

Method: BRAID casts interleaved text-image-text reasoning as a unified MDP, computes a shared trajectory-level advantage, and propagates it into both text tokens (via policy gradient) and image denoising paths (via modality-native gradient). A VLM judge provides dense turn-level rewards by scoring intermediate images on reasoning utility.

Result: BRAID consistently outperforms various baselines on spatial reasoning and visual perception benchmarks.

Conclusion: A unified MDP formulation with vision-thinking guidance is essential for effective multi-modal reasoning via RL.

Abstract: Unified multi-modal models (UMMs) have shown promising interleaved text-image reasoning capabilities, yet effectively optimizing such multi-turn generation via reinforcement learning (RL) remains an open challenge. Existing approaches apply RL exclusively to text steps, relegating image generation to supervised surrogates, preventing policy gradients from propagating through the full interleaved trajectory across heterogeneous modalities. This leaves the potential of RL for UMMs largely untapped. In the paper, we introduce \textbf{BRAID} (\textbf{B}ridging inte\textbf{R}le\textbf{A}ved mult\textbf{I}-modal reasoning as a unified \textbf{D}ecision process), a simple framework that casts multi-turn text-image-text reasoning as a unified Markov decision process (MDP), enabling joint optimization of textual and visual generation via a single, principled RL objective. BRAID computes a shared trajectory-level advantage and propagates it coherently into both text tokens and image denoising paths, each optimized through its modality-native policy gradient mechanism. To further address long-horizon credit assignment, BRAID employs a vision-language model (VLM) judge that scores each intermediate image on its reasoning utility, supplying dense turn-level feedback to sharpen learning at critical visual branches. Experiments on spatial reasoning and visual perception benchmarks show that BRAID consistently outperforms various baselines, confirming that a unified MDP formulation with vision-thinking guidance is essential for effective multi-modal reasoning.

</details>


### [34] [Folding, Reasoning, and Scaling with Open-source Drug Discovery Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03787)
*Aureka AI OpenDDE project*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OpenDDE is an open-source, all-atom biomolecular foundation model that uses co-folding as a structural reasoning layer for drug discovery, achieving IsoDDE-level accuracy while identifying scaling laws and releasing all code, pipelines, and benchmarks openly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurately modeling biomolecular interactions is a central bottleneck in biology and therapeutic discovery; current approaches treat structure prediction as an isolated endpoint rather than a reusable reasoning layer.

Method: OpenDDE integrates advances in all-atom architecture, atomic latent reasoning, inference optimization, and large-scale data processing, using co-folding as the entry point for a scalable AI-driven drug discovery engine designed as a shared structural reasoning layer.

Result: Achieves IsoDDE-level co-folding accuracy within a reproducible and open framework; identifies two scaling-law directions for co-folding models pointing to practical routes for improvement through data, model, inference, and training scaling.

Conclusion: OpenDDE democratizes access to frontier biomolecular intelligence, accelerates global collaboration, and lays an open foundation for next-generation drug discovery systems that can move from prediction toward designing, scoring, and optimizing therapeutic candidates.

Abstract: Accurately modeling biomolecular interactions is a central bottleneck in biology and therapeutic discovery. Here, we introduce Open Drug Discovery Engine (OpenDDE), an open-source, all-atom biomolecular foundation model that uses co-folding as the entry point to a scalable AI-driven drug discovery engine. Rather than treating structure prediction as an isolated endpoint, OpenDDE is designed as a shared structural reasoning layer for modeling sequence-structure-function relationships across biomolecular complexes, enabling complex structure prediction today while providing a foundation for de novo design, affinity estimation, structure-conditioned optimization, and more. OpenDDE integrates advances in all-atom architecture, atomic latent reasoning, inference optimization, and large-scale data processing to achieve IsoDDE-level co-folding accuracy within a reproducible and openly accessible framework. We also identify two scaling-law directions for co-folding models, revealing practical routes for continued improvement through data, model, inference, and training scaling. By releasing training code, inference pipelines, checkpoints, and benchmarks, OpenDDE aims to democratize access to frontier biomolecular intelligence, accelerate global collaboration, and lay an open foundation for next-generation drug discovery systems that can move from predicting molecular structures toward designing, scoring, and optimizing therapeutic candidates for human health.

</details>


### [35] [Evaluating LLM Uncertainty in Long-Form Generation Using Deterministic Ground Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03870)
*Ido Amit,Ido Galil,Ran El-Yaniv*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SALT is a benchmark of six procedurally generated tasks with deterministic ground truths for evaluating uncertainty estimation in long-form LLM outputs at fine-grained resolutions. Analysis of 50+ LLMs reveals that confidence ranking breaks at atomic resolution, error propagation has two separable drivers (prefix corruption and length-induced degradation), and reasoning (CoT/training) introduces an accuracy vs. confidence ranking trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-form generation benchmarks rely on fallible labels rather than deterministic ground truth, making fine-grained uncertainty evaluation (token to entire generation) challenging due to sensitivity to label imperfections. There is no zero-noise benchmark enabling unit-level evaluation of correctness, calibration, and ranking without external judges.

Method: The authors introduce SALT (Single-answer Atomic Long-form Target), a benchmark of six procedurally generated tasks with single deterministic long textual ground truths. They analyze 50+ LLMs to evaluate confidence functions at atomic and line-level resolutions and conduct controlled atom-level interventions to study error propagation.

Result: Confidence ranking largely breaks at atomic resolution but clearer separability emerges at coarser line-level units. Two separable drivers of future errors are identified: propagation from corrupted prefixes (dominated by global context correctness) and bounded degradation from increasing answer-context length. Reasoning (CoT or internalized training) improves accuracy but degrades confidence ranking.

Conclusion: Fine-grained uncertainty estimation in long-form generation requires unit-level evaluation, and reasoning introduces a trade-off between accuracy and confidence ranking quality, which directly impacts risk-critical applications requiring reliable error identification and mitigation.

Abstract: As LLMs generate increasingly long outputs, effective uncertainty estimation must identify errors at fine-grained levels rather than discard entire responses. While such methods exist, evaluating uncertainty at any resolution (token to an entire generation) is challenging and highly sensitive to label imperfections, making zero-noise benchmarks essential; yet, long-form generation benchmarks tend to rely on fallible labels rather than deterministic ground truth. We introduce Single-answer Atomic Long-form Target (SALT), a benchmark of six procedurally generated tasks with single deterministic long textual ground truths, enabling unit-level evaluation of correctness, calibration, and ranking without external judges. Equipped with SALT, our analysis of 50+ LLMs reveals key insights: We identify which confidence functions dominate each uncertainty aspect and show that confidence ranking largely breaks at atomic resolution, even when clearer separability emerges at coarser line-level units. SALT further enables controlled atom-level interventions throughout generation, revealing two separable drivers of future errors: propagation from corrupted prefixes, dominated by global context correctness, and bounded degradation from increasing answer-context length. Finally, we demonstrate that reasoning, via Chain-of-Thought prompting or internalized through training, introduces a trade-off, improving accuracy while degrading confidence ranking. These findings directly impact risk-critical applications requiring reliable error identification and mitigation.

</details>


### [36] [Harness-Aware Self-Evolving: Co-Evolving Model Weights, Harness, and Task Solutions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03935)
*Haochen Luo,Yi Huang,Sichun Luo,Fengyuan Liu,Lei Li,Zefa Hu,Junlan Feng,Qi Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HASE is a reinforcement-learning framework where a single model jointly improves task solutions and the surrounding harness (evaluation components), achieving strong performance across text classification, alpha factor mining, and circle-packing algorithm discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-evolving frameworks optimize solutions within a fixed harness, limiting the potential for holistic improvement. HASE aims to unify solution and harness evolution in a single agentic process.

Method: HASE uses a multi-turn action space where a single model can either generate task solutions or edit selected harness components, trained via agentic reinforcement learning.

Result: A single Qwen3-8B model matches GPT-OSS-120B + Claude Code on text classification, outperforms the baseline on alpha factor mining, and achieves SOTA in circle-packing algorithm discovery.

Conclusion: Jointly evolving the harness and solution through a unified agentic process improves performance and generality over fixed-harness self-evolving approaches.

Abstract: Self-evolving frameworks usually optimize task solutions while treating the surrounding harness as fixed. We introduce Harness-Aware Self-Evolving (HASE), an agentic reinforcement-learning framework in which a single model can generate task solutions or edit selected harness components in a multi-turn action space. HASE enables a single Qwen3-8B model to match the text-classification performance of a GPT-OSS-120B model that uses Claude Code as the harness proposer. In alpha factor mining, HASE outperforms the reported GPT-OSS-120B baseline. HASE also repairs imperfect evaluation components and converges to state-of-the-art performance in circle-packing algorithm discovery. These results show that HASE improves the harness and the solution through one unified agentic process.

</details>


### [37] [Online Linear Programming for Multi-Objective Routing in LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03948)
*Zixi Chen,Yinyu Ye,Zijie Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a multi-objective optimization framework for online routing in LLM serving, using online linear programming with bid-price control and warm-started projected first-order updates to dispatch requests under batch-size and KV-cache constraints, outperforming heuristic baselines across multiple SLO metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing routing heuristics in LLM serving are not tied to explicit service-level objectives (SLOs) and offer limited control over latency-throughput trade-offs, motivating the need for a principled optimization framework.

Method: Formulates routing as an online linear programming problem with interpretable decision rewards; applies a bid-price control policy that admits requests when SLO-weighted benefit exceeds shadow prices; uses warm-started projected first-order updates to track dual shadow prices online with millisecond-level runtime.

Result: Substantial improvements over standard baselines across multiple SLO regimes, including end-to-end latency, time-to-first-token, throughput, and tail performance, demonstrated via integration into the Vidur simulator.

Conclusion: A science-based (optimization-driven) approach outperforms heuristic-based methods for online routing in LLM serving.

Abstract: We study the online routing problem in large language model serving, where requests arrive sequentially and must be dispatched to parallel decode workers under tight batch-size and KV-cache constraints. Unlike widely used routing heuristics that are not tied to explicit service-level objectives (SLOs) and offer limited control over latency-throughput trade-offs, we introduce a multi-objective optimization framework that formulates routing as an online linear programming with interpretable decision rewards. We apply an efficient bid-price control policy based on the online linear programming that admits requests when their SLO-weighted benefit exceeds their shadow prices. To meet millisecond decision requirements, we develop a warm-started, projected first-order updates that track the evolving dual shadow prices online with predictable runtime. We integrate our router into the Vidur simulator and demonstrate substantial improvements over standard baselines across multiple SLO regimes, including end-to-end latency, time-to-first-token, throughput, and tail performance. A big picture from our result: a science-based approach outperforms others based on heuristics.

</details>


### [38] [Explainable AI for Screening Abuse-Related Trauma in Bangladeshi Children: A Training-Free Multimodal Framework Evaluated on Noise-Aware Synthetic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04010)
*Salma Hoque Talukdar Koli,Fahima Haque Talukder Jely*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ShishuRaksha AI is a decision-support framework for early screening of child abuse-related psychological trauma in Bangladesh, fusing four screening modalities (questionnaires, Bengali narrative text, HTP drawings, facial affect) with cross-modal attention and clinically weighted explanations, achieving an AUC of 0.874 on a synthetic benchmark of 500 cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bangladesh has only 1.17 mental-health professionals per 100,000 population and six child psychiatrists nationwide, with no Bengali-language, culturally adapted tool for early screening of abuse-related psychological trauma in children, creating an urgent need for a scalable, low-resource screening solution.

Method: The framework fuses four screening modalities (validated questionnaires SDQ/CPSS, Bengali narrative text, House-Tree-Person drawing features, facial affect) using a training-free, clinically weighted approach with cross-modal attention and a single-modality override rule. Each risk score is explained via perturbation-based additive attribution and rendered as bilingual reports with referral routing. Due to ethical constraints on collecting real clinical data, a noise-aware synthetic benchmark (500 cases, 23.2% positive) was constructed with four deliberate noise layers and literature-grounded HTP priors. Tree-ensemble surrogates (facial channel excluded) were evaluated under 5-fold stratified cross-validation.

Result: The fused model achieved an AUC of 0.874 [0.834-0.908] compared to 0.756 [0.705-0.803] for the SDQ-only baseline, with ablation, operating-point, subgroup, and calibration analyses conducted.

Conclusion: This work is a feasibility study and design contribution toward ethically deployable child-protection screening in low-resource settings, with openly stated limitations including synthetic-only data, no held-out set, text-feature circularity, and an urban-rural subgroup gap.

Abstract: Bangladesh has an estimated 1.17 mental-health professionals per 100,000 population and only six child psychiatrists nationwide. No Bengali-language, culturally adapted tool exists for early screening of abuse-related psychological trauma in children. We present ShishuRaksha AI, a decision-support (not diagnostic) framework that fuses four screening modalities: validated questionnaires (SDQ, CPSS), Bengali narrative text, House-Tree-Person (HTP) drawing features, and facial affect. The fusion is training-free and clinically weighted, uses cross-modal attention, and includes a single-modality override rule. Every risk score is explained through clinically weighted, perturbation-based additive attribution and rendered as a bilingual (Bangla/English) report with referral routing to national child-protection services (OCC, DSS, NMHH) under the Children Act 2013. No clinical dataset of abused children can be collected ethically at this stage, so we introduce a noise-aware synthetic benchmark (500 cases, 116 positive [23.2%], four deliberate noise layers, literature-grounded HTP priors) and evaluate tree-ensemble surrogates of the fusion design (facial channel excluded) under 5-fold stratified cross-validation. The fused model reaches an AUC of 0.874 [0.834-0.908], against 0.756 [0.705-0.803] for an SDQ-only baseline, with ablation, operating-point, subgroup, and calibration analyses. We state all limitations openly, including synthetic-only data, no held-out set, text-feature circularity, and an urban-rural subgroup gap. This work is a feasibility study and a design contribution toward ethically deployable child-protection screening in low-resource settings.

</details>


### [39] [What is Left for Us? Second Scholarship Against the Degradation of Research by AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04049)
*Claudio Novelli,Luciano Floridi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that generative AI degrades research by undermining the practices through which scholarly judgement and trust are built—practices like tacit knowledge, personal commitment, socialisation, and deep reading—which cannot be reduced to outputs AI can simulate. Delegating core tasks to AI erodes researchers' intellectual formation; the solution is a renewed commitment to "second scholarship": reclaiming scholarly craft through frictional, participatory, lived practice rather than mere human-in-the-loop oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative AI, particularly LLMs, are increasingly used in research workflows. The authors are motivated by the concern that this delegation, while seemingly efficient, systematically erodes the constitutive practices (tacit knowledge, commitment, socialisation, deep reading) that form scholarly judgement and underwrite academic trust. These practices cannot be captured by outputs alone, so using AI to simulate them risks hollowing out the intellectual formation of researchers.

Method: Conceptual analysis and normative argument. The paper identifies four foundational practices (tacit knowledge, personal commitment, socialisation, deep reading) as non-automatable conditions for knowledge production. It contrasts a "human-in-the-loop" model (prompting, quality-checking) with a "lived practice" model, and proposes "second scholarship" as a deliberate reappropriation of scholarly craft grounded in critical awareness of AI's limits.

Result: The authors conclude that delegating central inquiry tasks to generative AI prevents researchers from developing the judgement formed only through engaging in these practices. AI-generated outputs may appear improved, but the researcher behind them does not develop. Human-in-the-loop approaches are insufficient. The proposed alternative—second scholarship—reclaims intellectual formation through friction, community participation, and valuing what cannot be automated.

Conclusion: Preserving research as a site of intellectual formation requires a renewed commitment to research as lived practice, not merely efficient output generation. The four non-automatable sources of research—tacit knowledge, personal commitment, socialisation, and deep reading—must be actively protected and valued. "Second scholarship" is the reappropriation of scholarly craft chosen from a critical understanding of AI's genuine capabilities and limitations.

Abstract: We argue that generative AI can degrade research by eroding the very practices through which scholarly judgement is formed and academic trust is built. As constitutive conditions for the production and validation of knowledge, these practices cannot be reduced to the final outputs of research, which is what AI so effectively simulate. Accordingly, when researchers delegate central tasks of inquiry to systems like Large Language Models, they may stop enacting these practices and, with them, lose access to the formation they provide. An individual research output generated by AI may even appear improved but the researcher behind it fails to develop. Against this risk, merely keeping humans in the loop as prompters or quality checkers of AI outputs is insufficient to preserve research as a site of intellectual formation. What is needed instead is a renewed commitment to research as a lived practice in which judgement is formed gradually, often through frictions, and participation in a scholarly community. We defend it because it rests on four sources and warrants of research that cannot be automated: tacit knowledge, personal commitment, socialisation, and deep reading. This practice enacts what we call second scholarship, by which we understand the reappropriation of scholarly craft, chosen out of a critical experience of what generative AI can and cannot do. What cannot and should not be delegated becomes what research communities must value and answer for. This is what is left for us.

</details>


### [40] [PLACEMEM: Toward a Compute-Aware Memory Plane for Lifelong Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04089)
*Sukanta Ganguly*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PLACEMEM proposes versioned capsules as a systems architecture for lifelong-agent memory, unifying semantics, provenance, validity, and reusable runtime state under correction-aware identity, with a vLLM-first prototype demonstrating retrieval, KV-aware routing, and cascading invalidation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lifelong agents need persistent, evolvable, correctable memory that avoids recomputing history on every turn or silently reusing stale state — gaps not solved by larger context windows or better retrieval alone.

Method: Versioned capsules as the core abstraction; prototype implements prompt-level text retrieval, KV-aware routing, cascading invalidation over live streamed backends, with an OpenAI-compatible sidecar and typed metadata contract.

Result: An executable prototype demonstrating correction-aware control-plane behavior: persistent capsule state, concurrency-safe invalidation, measurable first-token latency, reuse, and post-correction behavior.

Conclusion: PLACEMEM provides both a current executable artifact and a roadmap toward replay-aware serving integration for future lifelong-agent systems, with layer-frontier replay framed as future work.

Abstract: Lifelong agents need more than larger context windows and better retrieval. They need memories that can persist, evolve, and be corrected without forcing the serving stack to recompute the same history on every turn or silently reuse stale runtime state. We present PLACEMEM as a systems position on lifelong-agent memory, instantiated by an executable control-plane prototype. The central claim is that agent memory should be represented as versioned capsules that unify semantics, provenance, validity, and reusable runtime state under one correction-aware identity. In the current prototype, capsules drive prompt-level text retrieval, KV-aware routing, and cascading invalidation over live streamed backends; prospective layer-frontier replay is intentionally framed as a deeper integration agenda rather than a claimed engine feature. We describe a vLLM-first prototype with persistent capsule state, concurrency-safe invalidation, an OpenAI-compatible routing sidecar, a typed metadata contract, and a benchmark harness that measures live first-token latency, reuse, and post-correction behavior. The result is both an executable artifact that demonstrates correction-aware control-plane behavior today and a concrete roadmap for replay-aware serving integration in future lifelong-agent systems.

</details>


### [41] [Forethought: Verifiable Reasoning from Neurosymbolic Primitive Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04096)
*Vishvesh Bhat,Jay Vaghasiya,Emmanuel Anaya Gonzalez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Forethought is a neurosymbolic reasoning system that treats reasoning as explicit, verifiable programs composed from symbolic and neural primitives via a DSL, improving base-model accuracy by ~30% relative across five benchmarks and enabling small models to match frontier models with far less post-training investment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agentic workflows rely on test-time scaling (e.g., long chains of thought) which entangles reasoning in model weights, is not verifiable step-by-step, and is costly at inference. The authors aim to create an alternative that is explicit, auditable, and model-agnostic.

Method: Forethought uses a neurosymbolic approach: a domain-specific language composes symbolic and neural primitives into explicit reasoning programs, executed by a tool-calling kernel. These programs are inspectable and modifiable before deployment.

Result: Forethought improves base-model accuracy by ~30% relative across five benchmarks, outperforms vanilla prompting, RL scaffolds, and prompt-evolution methods. A non-reasoning model augmented with Forethought competes with a dedicated reasoning model while requiring ~3 orders of magnitude less post-training investment.

Conclusion: Explicit, verifiable reasoning programs can substitute for costly test-time scaling, enabling small models to match frontier model performance with greater auditability and efficiency.

Abstract: Current agentic workflows usually involve decomposing user requests into sequences of tool calls with correctly resolved parameters, the results of which are processed through reasoning traces in the language model's context window. The prevailing route to improve such reasoning is test-time scaling, which trains models to search over long chains of thought; but the resulting capability is entangled in model weights, is not verifiable step-by-step, and is costly at inference. We present Forethought, a neurosymbolic reasoning system that instead treats reasoning as an explicit, verifiable program, that builds from a library of symbolic and neural primitives which are composed through a domain-specific language. The result are reasoning programs, which are concrete representations of the model's work, and as such can be inspected and modified before deployment. Instantiated as a tool-calling execution kernel and evaluated across five benchmarks, Forethought improves base-model accuracy by about 30% relative and outperforms vanilla prompting, reinforcement learning scaffolds, and prompt-evolution methods, enabling small models to match or exceed frontier models capabilities. In a direct comparison, a non-reasoning model augmented with Forethought competes with a dedicated reasoning model while requiring roughly three orders of magnitude less post-training investment, and remains model-agnostic and auditable.

</details>


### [42] [Language models guide symbolic equation discovery by controlling search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04156)
*Zikai Xie,Wenmei Li,Man Luo,Jun Jiang,Linjiang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes LLM-PySR, where language models control the search space (variables, operators, transformations, depth) while symbolic regression handles equation fitting and deterministic metrics govern retention. This controller role outperforms other role specifications (author, decider, end-to-end LM) across 74 AI-Feynman equations and seven complex tasks, achieving the best balance of accuracy, complexity, stability, and cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symbolic regression suffers from combinatorial explosion, while LM-based systems for equation discovery lack rigorous numerical validation. The paper tests whether a division of labor—where LMs shape hypothesis exploration but not equation survival—can achieve better results.

Method: The authors compare four role specifications: (1) LM as equation author (direct formula generation), (2) LM as candidate decider (selecting among candidates), (3) LM as search controller (LLM-PySR: LM specifies variables, operators, transformations, search depth; symbolic regression enumerates/fits expressions; deterministic metrics govern retention), plus end-to-end LM and purely numerical baselines.

Result: Across 74 AI-Feynman equations and seven complex formula-recovery tasks, the search controller (LLM-PySR) achieved the strongest balance of accuracy, complexity, stability, and cost. On an independent battery dataset, LLM-PySR discovered a compact piecewise-linear relation between early voltage-curve displacement and cycle life.

Conclusion: Language models should shape hypothesis exploration rather than decide which equations survive; the controller role that separates search-space definition from numerical evaluation outperforms approaches where LMs directly generate or select formulas.

Abstract: Scientific equation discovery must combine broad domain priors with strict numerical testing. Symbolic regression supplies numerical grounding but faces a combinatorial search space, whereas many language-model systems ask the model to propose or select formulas directly. We test a different division of labour. We compare role specifications in which the language model acts as equation author, candidate decider or search controller, alongside end-to-end language-model and purely numerical baselines. In the controller setting we propose here, implemented as LLM-PySR, language models specify variables, operators, transformations and search depth; symbolic regression enumerates and fits expressions; and deterministic metrics govern retention. Across 74 AI-Feynman equations and seven complex formula-recovery tasks, search control achieved the strongest observed balance of accuracy, complexity, stability and cost. On an independent battery dataset, LLM-PySR identified a compact piecewise-linear relation between early voltage-curve displacement and cycle life. The results suggest that language models should shape hypothesis exploration rather than decide which equations survive.

</details>


### [43] [A Clustering-Based Framework for Identifying Suspicious Trading Patterns in Capital Market](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04184)
*Asif Zaman,Romona Magdalene Sarkar,Sabiha Khair Ohi,Iftekharul Mobin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An unsupervised fraud-detection toolkit using K-Means++ clustering identifies 2.02% of ~1M financial transactions (2012-2024) as suspicious, with most being spoofing (51.10%) or unclassified (46.83%), achieving a Silhouette Score of 0.561.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Market manipulation degrades confidence in trading platforms; existing supervised methods are limited due to lack of labeled ground truth data for financial fraud.

Method: An unsupervised clustering-based pipeline using K-Means++ clustering on ~1M financial transactions (2012-2024), with heuristic thresholds derived from market practice to classify fraudulent trades into categories (spoofing, pump and dump, insider trading, fake breakout).

Result: 2.02% of trades flagged as suspicious: 51.10% spoofing, 0.10% pump and dump, 0.55% insider trading, 1.43% fake breakout, 46.83% unclassified. Silhouette Score of 0.561 confirms model performance without ground truth.

Conclusion: K-Means++ clustering combined with heuristic thresholds provides a viable unsupervised approach for detecting market manipulation, though a large portion of suspicious trades remains unclassified, indicating room for improvement.

Abstract: Market manipulation is the dubious practice of manipulating stock prices in order to make a quick profit, which truly degrades confidence on trading platforms. We implemented an unsupervised fraud-detection toolkit that begins with K-Means++ clustering to address this issue. A dataset of roughly one million financial transactions from 2012 to 2024 is used. In order to identify fraudulent trades and categorize them using market practice heuristic thresholds, the study suggests a clustering-based pipeline. The method highlights 2.02% of trades as suspicious where 51.10% clearly indicate spoofing, 0.10% indicate pump and dump, 0.55% indicate insider trading, 1.43% indicate a fake breakout, and 46.83% are unclassified. Despite the lack of ground truth, the model's performance is confirmed by a Silhouette Score of 0.561.

</details>


### [44] [Agentic IoT: Architectures, Applications, and Challenges Toward the Internet of Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04219)
*Rümeysa Hilal Sevinç,Bahaeddin Türkoğlu,İbrahim Kök*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Agentic IoT as a next-generation cognitive IoT paradigm that integrates autonomous AI agents' capabilities (perception, reasoning, planning, learning, action) with cyber-physical systems, aiming to transform IoT from passive data-collection infrastructure into distributed cognitive agent ecosystems across the device/edge-fog-cloud continuum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AIoT solutions rely on task-specific models that infer from sensor data, lacking system-wide capabilities such as real-time reasoning, adaptive planning, autonomous coordination, learning, tool use, and contextual decision-making. This paper aims to address these limitations by proposing Agentic IoT.

Method: The paper grounds the transition as a paradigm shift, positions Agentic IoT in relation to AIoT, edge intelligence, multi-agent systems, and the Internet of Agents, systematically reviews current studies, presents a holistic architectural framework, and discusses domain-specific application potential.

Result: A systematic review of current studies, a holistic architectural framework for Agentic IoT, and identification of domain-specific application potential.

Conclusion: Agentic IoT represents a paradigm shift from data-centric sensing to distributed cognitive agent ecosystems. Key technical, operational, and research challenges are identified along with future research directions for advancing this paradigm.

Abstract: The integration of AI into Internet of Things (AIoT) systems has gradually transformed them from passive data collection infrastructures into intelligent systems capable of anomaly detection, predictive maintenance, classification, forecasting, and optimization. However, most existing solutions still rely on task-specific models that infer from sensor data; thus, system-wide capabilities such as real-time reasoning, adaptive planning, autonomous coordination, learning, tool use, and contextual decision-making remain limited. This paper examines Agentic IoT as a next-generation cognitive IoT paradigm that integrates the perception, reasoning, planning, learning, and action capabilities of autonomous AI agents with cyber-physical systems. Agentic IoT aims to transform IoT from data-centric sensing and inference infrastructures into distributed cognitive agent ecosystems operating across the device/edge-fog-cloud continuum. The paper first grounds this transition as a paradigm shift and positions Agentic IoT in relation to AIoT, edge intelligence, multi-agent systems, and the Internet of Agents. It then systematically reviews current studies, presents a holistic architectural framework, discusses domain-specific application potential, and identifies key technical, operational, and research challenges together with future research directions.

</details>


### [45] [Unsupervised Features Mining via Activation Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04222)
*Amit LeVi,Elad David,Max Fomin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAG is an unsupervised framework that extracts reasoning features from LLM activations by prepending a natural-language instruction Q to inputs, measuring the activation difference (m(Q|p)−m(p)). These features predict model judgments, can be steered via vector manipulation, and achieve 94.7% Top-1 accuracy for selecting prompt-injection training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing interpretability methods rely on labeled examples of human-defined concepts, which may introduce human biases. The authors aim to develop an unsupervised method to extract reasoning features from LLMs without requiring predefined labels.

Method: MAG prepends the same natural-language instruction Q to every input p, where Q defines a reasoning feature (e.g., "Can this object be found in the desert?"). The feature representation is computed as m(Q|p)−m(p) at a single readout point. The extracted features are evaluated for linearity, steering capability, and utility for dataset selection.

Result: Extracted reasoning features predict model's own world understanding and judgments. Some features are more linearly represented than others. Linear approximations enable activation steering to change LLM decisions. For prompt-injection classifier probe training data selection, RFD-based similarity achieves 94.7% Top-1 and 100% Top-2 accuracy, while ordinary activation similarity is unrelated to downstream performance.

Conclusion: MAG provides a simple, unsupervised way to extract interpretable reasoning features from LLMs that are linearly representable, steerable, and useful for practical tasks like training data selection.

Abstract: Interpretability methods aim to reveal the features represented inside large language models (LLMs). Many existing methods begin with labeled examples of a human-defined concept that may reflect human biases, and then identify how that concept is represented within the model, for example in its activation space or through other decomposition methods. We introduce \emph{Mining via Activation Geometry} (MAG), a simple unsupervised framework for extracting reasoning features from model activations by prepending the same natural-language instruction $Q$ to every input $p$, where $Q$ defines the reasoning feature of interest, such as ``Can this object be found in the desert?'' or ``Is this prompt malicious?'' We measure how the instruction changes the model's internal representation using $m(Q \mid p) - m(p)$ at a single readout point. We explore eight different MAGs. The extracted reasoning features predict the models' own world understanding and judgment, can be approximated into a single activation direction, we found that some features are more linearly represented and some less, this linear representation, which is vector steering, can change the LLMs' decisions through activation steering by injecting reasoning features. Finally, we use the same method to select the best training datasets for prompt-injection classifier probes: while similarity between ordinary activations is almost unrelated to downstream performance, RFD-based similarity achieves $94.7\%$ Top-1 and $100\%$ Top-2 accuracy.

</details>


### [46] [Biological Motifs for Agentic Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04240)
*Bogdan Banu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a formal correspondence between biological control motifs (from systems biology) and agentic LLM architectures using polynomial functors and wiring diagrams. It introduces the Agentic Operad for composable agent design, an epistemic topology with four predictive theorems, and a six-layer developmental progression, with a reference implementation of 1,813 tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs transitioning to autonomous agents suffer from reliability issues (hallucination cascades, infinite loops, prompt injection) due to ad-hoc architectures lacking principled design. The paper seeks to provide a formal, biologically-inspired framework to analyze and mitigate these failure modes.

Method: Typed interface correspondence between Gene Regulatory Networks and agentic systems via polynomial functors and wiring diagrams. Five biological motifs are mapped to software patterns. An epistemic topology derives Kripke-style knowledge operators from wiring diagram observation structures, yielding four theorems.

Result: (1) Agentic Operad with provable error suppression bounds; (2) Epistemic topology with four theorems (error amplification, sequential penalty, parallel acceleration, tool density scaling) consistent with multi-agent benchmarks; (3) Six-layer developmental progression; (4) Reference implementation with 1,813 tests and 116 examples.

Conclusion: Biological control motifs, when abstracted to typed interfaces and coordination structures, provide a rigorous foundation for designing reliable, secure, and scalable multi-agent LLM systems. The formal framework yields quantitatively testable predictions and a practical implementation.

Abstract: The transition of Large Language Models (LLMs) from passive generators to autonomous agents has introduced significant challenges in reliability, security, and state management. Current agentic architectures are often constructed ad-hoc, prone to hallucination cascades, infinite loops, and prompt injection attacks. This paper argues that many of these failure modes can be analyzed using control motifs long studied in systems biology, provided the comparison is made at the level of typed interfaces and coordination structure rather than literal biological mechanism.
  We develop a typed interface correspondence between Gene Regulatory Networks and agentic software systems using polynomial functors and wiring diagrams. Five biological motifs are mapped to composable software design patterns: Coherent Feed-Forward Loops for noise suppression, Adaptive Immunity for layered security, Mitochondrial Signaling for resource governance, Endosymbiosis for neuro-symbolic integration, and Morphogen Diffusion for spatially varying coordination. An epistemic topology layer derives Kripke-style knowledge operators from the wiring diagram's observation structure and proves four predictive theorems for multi-agent scaling.
  The core contributions are: (1) the Agentic Operad, a typed syntax for agent composition with provable error suppression bounds for feed-forward topologies; (2) an epistemic topology with four theorems (error amplification, sequential penalty, parallel acceleration, and tool density scaling) whose qualitative predictions are consistent with published multi-agent benchmarks; and (3) a six-layer progression from structure through development, grounded in autonomous learning frameworks and convergence proxies from the empirical literature. A reference implementation with 1,813 tests and 116 examples illustrates practical feasibility.

</details>


### [47] [Progress- and Reliability-Oriented Group Policy Optimization for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04242)
*Mingxuan Fan,Peiyang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProGPO is a learned-critic-free step-level group-based RL method that uses exact-prefix action comparison and transition credit from rollout-based state potentials to improve large language model agents on long-horizon interactive tasks, outperforming baselines on ALFWorld and WebShop with Qwen2.5 models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Step-level advantage estimation in group-based RL is sensitive to group formation: grouping by broad state keys improves coverage but may compare actions under different histories, while historical consistency yields fairer comparisons but fragments groups and loses peer-comparison signal.

Method: ProGPO keeps exact-prefix action comparison, complements sparse peer comparisons with transition credit derived from rollout-based state potentials, and combines semantic expansion with inverse-variance fusion across history depths to estimate potentials reliably.

Result: On ALFWorld and WebShop with Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, ProGPO improves over matched agentic RL baselines under comparable computational overhead.

Conclusion: ProGPO provides a learned-critic-free solution for context-consistent step-level learning in group-based RL, achieving better performance on long-horizon interactive agentic tasks while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Group-based reinforcement learning (RL) has become an effective paradigm for improving large language model agents on long-horizon interactive tasks. To obtain finer-grained policy updates than trajectory-level optimization, recent work has moved toward step-level group-based RL, where intermediate steps are grouped and compared within a rollout batch. However, step-level advantage estimation is sensitive to how groups are formed: grouping by broad state keys improves coverage but may compare actions taken under different histories, while enforcing historical consistency yields fairer comparisons at the cost of fragmented groups and missing peer-comparison signal. In this paper, we propose ProGPO (Progress- and Reliability-Oriented Group Policy Optimization), a learned-critic-free method for context-consistent step-level learning. ProGPO keeps exact-prefix action comparison, and complements sparse peer comparisons with transition credit derived from rollout-based state potentials. To estimate these potentials reliably, ProGPO combines semantic expansion with inverse-variance fusion across history depths. We evaluate ProGPO on two challenging agentic tasks, ALFWorld and WebShop, with Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct. Results show that ProGPO improves over matched agentic RL baselines under comparable computational overhead, and additional Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct experiments further test the scalability of the proposed method.

</details>


### [48] [Shortcut Learning in Legal Judgment Prediction: Empirical Evidence from the UK Employment Tribunal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04261)
*Joe Watson,Joana Ribeiro de Faria,Marcus Tomalin,Måns Magnusson,Huiyuan Xie,Hao Tian Yeung,Felix Steffek*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Legal Judgment Prediction models trained on post-hoc judicial texts exhibit shortcut learning, where performance is artificially inflated by outcome-revealing linguistic cues. However, when leakage features are masked, models retain predictive capability with minimal performance loss, suggesting the research agenda remains viable with proper auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LJP approaches rely on post-hoc judicial materials, making it unclear whether models perform true forecasting or retrospective classification. The authors investigate shortcut learning in this context to determine if reported performance is exaggerated by linguistic artefacts.

Method: Using 33,158 UK Employment Tribunal claims, the authors predict outcomes from claim texts and LLM-extracted summaries. They evaluate models from interpretable TF-IDF classifiers to black-box LLMs, stratify test data by human judgments of leakage, and compare against human experts. They retrain models after masking leakage features.

Result: Performance increases where outcome-revealing cues are embedded in the narrative. A model trained on just 4% of leakage-identified features outperforms human experts. After masking leakage features, retrained models show only negligible Macro-F1 reduction.

Conclusion: LJP performance can be exaggerated by linguistic artefacts, but this is not fatal. Post-hoc judgments should be treated as potentially contaminated texts requiring active auditing. Models remain capable of extracting useful predictive signals when artefacts are removed.

Abstract: Current Legal Judgment Prediction (LJP) is constrained by its reliance on post-hoc judicial materials, increasing the likelihood that models perform retrospective classification rather than true forecasting. This paper empirically investigates shortcut learning in this context by studying claim-level outcome prediction in UK Employment Tribunal (UKET) decisions. Using a corpus of 33,158 individual claims, we predict outcomes from claim texts and LLM-extracted case summaries, evaluating models ranging from interpretable TF-IDF-based classifiers to black-box LLMs. While headline predictive performance figures appear strong, we demonstrate that such performance in LJP systems trained on post-hoc judicial text can be driven by the retrospective nature of the source material. Stratifying the test data by human judgments of leakage reveals that performance increases where outcome-revealing cues are embedded in the narrative. Moreover, a model trained on just the 4% of features identified as leakage achieves high performance, outperforming human experts. These findings substantiate concerns that LJP performance may be exaggerated by linguistic artefacts. Yet this vulnerability is not fatal to the research agenda. Instead, post-hoc judgments might be treated as potentially contaminated texts, requiring active auditing. Retraining models after masking leakage features results in only a negligible reduction in Macro-F1. Hence, while models will opportunistically exploit shortcuts when available, they remain capable of extracting useful predictive signals when these artefacts are removed.

</details>


### [49] [Agentic SABRE: An Uncertainty-Aware Neuro-Symbolic Multi-Agent Framework for Adaptive Ransomware Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04292)
*Henry Kabuye,Biju Issac,Jeyamohan Neera*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic SABRE is a neuro-symbolic, multi-agent framework for adaptive ransomware detection that combines semantic and behavioural evidence with uncertainty quantification, enabling risk-aware triage with automated containment of high-confidence threats and escalation of uncertain cases to analysts, achieving AUC=1.0 on semantic datasets and up to 4.9% reduction in false escalations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ransomware has evolved into complex, adaptive adversaries where static signatures and monolithic classifiers fail under concept drift, evasion, and behavioural polymorphism, necessitating a more robust and adaptive detection approach.

Method: The framework uses a multi-agent architecture fusing semantic (representation-based) and behavioural (time-window forensic telemetry) evidence. It employs Monte Carlo Dropout for epistemic uncertainty quantification, a decision-layer orchestrator with risk score and uncertainty budget thresholds, and post-hoc explainability mechanisms (gradient saliency, permutation importance, counterfactual analysis).

Result: Achieves perfect discrimination (AUC=1.0) on saturated semantic datasets (RDset, RanSMAP), up to 4.9% relative reduction in false escalations at equal recall, calibrated predictive uncertainty, and stable decision boundaries reversible with bounded perturbation cost.

Conclusion: Agentic SABRE provides an interpretable, uncertainty-aware, and robust framework for adaptive ransomware detection, establishing a flexible computational contract between autonomous response and human analyst oversight.

Abstract: Ransomware has evolved into a complex, adaptive, and fast-moving adversary category in which static signatures and monolithic classifiers fail to generalise under concept drift, evasion, and behavioural polymorphism. In this paper, we present Agentic SABRE (Semantic-Behavioural Arbitration for Ransomware Evaluation), an uncertainty-aware, neuro-symbolic, multi-agent framework for adaptive ransomware detection. SABRE fuses semantic, representation-based evidence with behavioural, time-window forensic telemetry and employs Monte Carlo Dropout inference to quantify epistemic uncertainty for each agent. We introduce a decision-layer orchestrator that performs risk- and uncertainty-aware triage using two interpretable thresholds: a risk score and an uncertainty budget. High-confidence, high-risk samples are automatically contained, while uncertain or borderline cases are escalated to human analysts, establishing a flexible computational contract between autonomous response and analyst oversight. To support auditability and trust, SABRE integrates post-hoc explainability mechanisms, including gradient saliency, permutation importance, and counterfactual analysis, enabling both local and global interpretation of agent decisions. Extensive evaluation on RDset and RanSMAP demonstrates that Agentic SABRE preserves perfect discrimination on saturated semantic datasets, with AUC equal to 1.0, while improving robustness under weak behavioural signals. It achieves up to a 4.9 percent relative reduction in false escalations at equal recall while maintaining calibrated predictive uncertainty. Counterfactual analysis further shows that semantic and behavioural decisions can be reversed with bounded perturbation cost, indicating stable and interpretable decision boundaries.

</details>


### [50] [HAS-Bench: Evaluating LLM-Based Human-Agent Systems under Configurable Human Participation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04329)
*Yaozu Wu,Wei-Chieh Huang,Jizhou Guo,Dongyuan Li,Renhe Jiang,Henry Peng Zou,Chunyu Miao,Shanghao Li,Weizhi Zhang,WeiWei Ye,Yankai Chen,Meng Zhang,Xue Liu,Philip S. Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces HAS-Framework, a graph-based framework for modeling human-LLM collaboration, and HAS-Bench, a benchmark evaluating Human-Agent Systems across agency levels, interaction channels, and persona policies. Experiments across six domains show human participation improves task completion and failure recovery, but gains depend on timing, method, and source of human input.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models increasingly operate in settings where humans are active collaborators rather than passive task providers, yet existing benchmarks lack support for evaluating configurable human participation in Human-Agent Systems.

Method: HAS-Framework uses graph-based representation with humans and LLM agents as first-class participants having explicit roles, permissions, communication paths, and action authority. HAS-Bench evaluates under configurable human participation across agency levels, interaction channels, and persona policies, measuring both task outcomes and process-level collaboration behaviors.

Result: Experiments across six domains show human participation can substantially improve task completion and failure recovery, but gains depend on when, how, and by whom human input is exercised.

Conclusion: Human participation improves LLM-agent performance, but the effectiveness is contingent on the timing, method, and source of human intervention, highlighting the need for structured evaluation frameworks for Human-Agent collaboration.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly operate in settings where humans are active collaborators rather than passive task providers. We introduce HAS-Framework, a graph-based framework that represents humans and LLM-powered agents as first-class participants with explicit roles, permissions, communication paths, and action authority. Building on this framework, HAS-Bench evaluates Human-Agent Systems under configurable human participation across agency levels, interaction channels, and persona policies. The benchmark measures both task outcomes and process-level collaboration behavior, including clarification quality, feedback utilization, control calibration, safety, initiative, and interaction cost. Experiments across six domains show that human participation can substantially improve task completion and failure recovery, but the gains depend on when, how, and by whom human input is exercised.

</details>


### [51] [Do GUI Agents Believe Their Eyes? Diagnosing State-Belief Reliance on Pixels versus Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04334)
*Guijia Zhang,Harry Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formalizes "visual state reliance" (whether GUI agents' state beliefs come from pixels or structure) and measures the Perception-Fusion Gap (PfG): the fraction of probes where a model correctly perceives an interface but resolves toward structured text under pixel-structure conflict. Across 5 models, PfG is positive for all; textual agents defer to structure, while coordinate-action agents stay pixel-bound. This conflict drives real task failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for multimodal GUI agents measure task success, element grounding, or attack resistance, but do not assess whether an agent's belief about the interface state is actually grounded in visual (pixel) information versus relying on serialized structure (DOM/accessibility tree) or priors.

Method: The authors formalize "visual state reliance" and measure it via paired single-channel interventions (pixels-only vs. structure-only vs. both) on 310 real web, mobile, and desktop probes. Each probe uses deterministic forced choice (no model-generated items or judges). The key metric is the Perception-Fusion Gap (PfG): the fraction of probes where a model correctly perceives the state but resolves toward structure under pixel-structure conflict.

Result: Across five models from three vendors: (1) textual state beliefs defer to structure while image-only accuracy remains near ceiling; (2) PfG is positive for every model; (3) non-text (coordinate-action) agents remain largely pixel-bound; (4) the substitution is specific to the serialized-text and indexed-action channel; (5) the effect traces to a single copied structural value via white-box ablation; (6) in live environments, the conflict drives wrong actions and real task failures.

Conclusion: Visual state reliance is a measurable diagnostic of whether agent state beliefs are visually grounded. The errors exposed by the Perception-Fusion Gap propagate to real actions and task failures, highlighting a critical vulnerability in text-channel-dependent GUI agents.

Abstract: Multimodal GUI agents read an interface through two redundant channels: the rendered pixels of a screenshot and a serialized structure such as a DOM or accessibility tree. Before acting, an agent forms a belief about the current interface state, but existing benchmarks score task success, element grounding, or attack resistance and do not ask whether that belief is drawn from the pixels. We formalize visual state reliance, the attribution of a state belief to pixels, structure, or priors, and measure it with paired single-channel interventions over 310 real web, mobile, and desktop probes. Every probe is scored by deterministic forced choice, with no model-generated item and no model judge. Our central metric is the Perception-Fusion Gap, the fraction of probes a model perceives correctly yet resolves toward structure under conflict. Across five models from three vendors, textual state beliefs defer to structure while image-only accuracy stays near ceiling, and Perception-Fusion Gap is positive for every model; non-text identity, by contrast, stays largely pixel-bound. The substitution is specific to the serialized-text and indexed-action channel, and coordinate-action agents are largely immune.
