For textual conflicts, a white-box ablation traces the effect to a single copied structural value, and in two live environments the conflict drives wrong actions and real task failure. Visual state reliance therefore gives a measurable diagnostic of whether agent state beliefs are visually grounded, and the errors it exposes propagate to actions.

</details>


### [52] [Server-side Anti-cheat in FPS games for Aimbot detection using Deep learning and Machine learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04336)
*Siddhesh A. Dhinge,Shubham G. Sukum,Harsh S. Ranjane,Ruturajsingh R. Rajput,Jyoti H. Jadhav*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: YAACS is a server-side aimbot detection system for FPS games using Stacked LSTM with Dense layers on time-series and behavioral features, achieving 88.6% accuracy with a low 0.97% false positive rate, outperforming Decision Tree (96.2% accuracy but 2.68% FPR) by reducing false accusations by 2.76x.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rising popularity of multiplayer FPS games and increasing use of cheating techniques (aimbots, wallhacks, speed hacks) necessitates robust detection methods to ensure fair and enjoyable gaming, with a focus on aimbot cheats that cause players to neglect other gameplay aspects.

Method: A server-side aimbot detection classifier (YAACS) using a parser, Stacked LSTM with Dense layers, and intermediary utilities, trained on sequences of 128 ticks (Tick Delta Negative=56, Tick Delta Positive=24) with features including time-series data (aim velocity, shots, distance to target) and behavioral data (utility usage, player movement, gameplay patterns).

Result: YAACS achieves 88.6% classification accuracy with a 0.97% false positive rate, compared to a Decision Tree baseline with 96.2% accuracy but 2.68% FPR (2.76x worse). The best LSTM configuration uses Stacked LSTM with Dense layers on 128-tick sequences.

Conclusion: Incorporating temporal context through sequence modeling (LSTM) is critical for minimizing false accusations in FPS cheat detection, as it significantly reduces false positive rates compared to non-sequential baselines like Decision Trees, even at the cost of slightly lower overall accuracy.

Abstract: Modern video games are becoming more complex day by day. Most of these modern games are multiplayer first-person shooter (FPS) games. The rising popularity of FPS games emphasizes the need to combat cheating for fair and enjoyable gaming. As the number of players using cheating techniques like aimbots, wallhacks, and speed hacks is also increasing, we need a way to detect players who are using cheating tools to gain an unfair advantage over regular players. In this system, we focus exclusively on detecting aimbot cheats. Players who use aimbot cheats generally do not prioritize other aspects of the game. To distinguish between regular and cheating players, we identify specific features encompassing time-series data such as aim velocity, number of shots, distance to target, and more, along with behavioral data such as utility usage, player movement, and other gameplay patterns. Utilizing these features, we construct a server-side aimbot detection classifier named 'YAACS'. YAACS comprises a parser, a deep learning model, and intermediary connection utilities designed for integration with the game server. The proposed system achieves a classification accuracy of 88.6% with a false positive rate of 0.97% using a Stacked LSTM with Dense layers trained on sequences of 128 ticks (Tick Delta Negative=56, Tick Delta Positive=24), outperforming the Decision Tree baseline which achieves a higher accuracy of 96.2% but at a false positive rate of 2.68%, 2.76x worse than the best LSTM configuration. These results demonstrate that incorporating temporal context through sequence modelling is critical for minimising false accusations in FPS cheat detection.

</details>


### [53] [Nemotron-Labs-3-Puzzle-75B-A9B: Compressing Hybrid MoE LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04371)
*Akhiad Bercovich,Talor Abramovich,Daniel Afrimi,Shay Aharon,Nir Ailon,Vladimir Anisimov,Omer Ullman Argov,Maor Ashkenazi,Tomer Asida,Nave Assaf,Tomer Bar Natan,Alexander Bukharin,Grzegorz Chlebus,Marcin Chochowski,Eric Chung,Mohammad Dabbah,Carlo del Mundo,Ewa Dobrowolska,Ido Galil,Yaniv Galron,Amnon Geifman,Yonatan Geifman,Izik Golan,Alex Gronskiy,Tomasz Grzegorzek,Netanel Haber,Lior Kadoch,Grzegorz Karch,Tomer Keren,Abhinav Khattar,Amir Klein,Tugrul Konuk,Roi Koren,Daniel Korzekwa,Shaun Kotek,Konstantinos Krommydas,Itay Levy,Ofri Masad,Yoav Miron,Pavlo Molchanov,Shahar Mor,Zach Moshe,Saurav Muralidharan,Najeeb Nabwani,Besmira Nushi,Mostofa Patwary,Omri Puny,Johannes Rausch,Tomer Ronen,Sepehr Sameni,Itamar Schen,Elad Segal,Daniel Serebrenik,Ido Shahaf,Soumye Singhal,Daniil Sorokin,Sharath Turuvekere Sreenivas,Marta Stepniewska-Dziubinska,Ali Taghibakhshi,Nima Tajbakhsh,Oren Tropp,Dor Tzur,Anna Warno,Yi-Fu Wu,Michal Zawalski,Jiaqi Zeng,Yian Zhang,Ran Zilberstein,Amit Zuker,Ran El-Yaniv*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Nemotron-Labs-3-Puzzle-75B-A9B is a compressed variant of Nemotron-3-Super that achieves ~2x higher throughput on 8xB200 nodes and 8x concurrency on single H100 GPUs for ultra-long-context (1M tokens) via a multi-stage pipeline (Iterative Puzzle compression, distillation, RL, quantization, Multi-Token Prediction head), jointly optimizing MoE pruning, active parameter budget, and Mamba pruning while retaining strong downstream accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create a compressed variant of Nemotron-3-Super that maximizes server throughput under high user throughput constraints for interactive deployment, addressing the need for efficient inference without significant quality degradation.

Method: Multi-stage pipeline combining Iterative Puzzle compression framework with knowledge distillation, reinforcement learning, quantization, and a Multi-Token Prediction head; jointly optimizes heterogeneous MoE pruning, active parameter budget, and Mamba pruning to improve inference efficiency while preserving model quality.

Result: Achieves ~2x higher server throughput than Nemotron-3-Super on a single 8xB200 node under matched user throughput constraints; increases 1M-token concurrency from 1 to 8 requests on a single H100 GPU; retains strong downstream accuracy across reasoning, coding, multilingual, long-context, and agentic benchmarks despite substantial compression.

Conclusion: Large hybrid MoE models can be substantially optimized for deployment efficiency while maintaining strong downstream capability, as demonstrated by the Puzzle-75B-A9B compression results.

Abstract: We present Nemotron-Labs-3-Puzzle-75B-A9B, a compressed variant of Nemotron-3-Super optimized for interactive deployment. We designed the model to maximize server throughput under high user throughput constraints. In interactive serving workloads on a single 8xB200 node, Puzzle-75B-A9B achieves approximately 2x higher server throughput than Nemotron-3-Super at matched user throughput constraints. In ultra-long-context deployment on a single H100 GPU, the compressed model increases 1M-token concurrency from 1 request to 8 requests. Puzzle-75B-A9B is constructed using a multi-stage pipeline that combines the Iterative Puzzle compression framework with knowledge distillation, reinforcement learning, quantization, and a Multi-Token Prediction head. The compression process jointly optimizes heterogeneous MoE pruning, active parameter budget, and Mamba pruning to improve inference efficiency while preserving model quality. We evaluate Puzzle-75B-A9B on a broad suite of reasoning, coding, multilingual, long-context, and agentic benchmarks. Despite substantial compression, the model retains strong downstream accuracy relative to the parent model across a wide range of tasks. These results demonstrate that large hybrid MoE models can be substantially optimized for deployment efficiency while maintaining strong downstream capability.

</details>


### [54] [Decentralized Aggregation of LLM Predictions via Wagering Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04389)
*Yuhong Luo,David M. Pennock,Xintong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WALLA is a family of wagering mechanisms for aggregating predictions from multiple LLMs in decentralized settings, achieving incentive-compatible predictions, advantage-aligned wagers, and decentralized learning with performance matching centralized methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To aggregate predictions from multiple LLMs with domain expertise or private data in decentralized settings, where aggregation weights must be determined without access to private information and remain robust to strategic reporting.

Method: Proposes advantage-aligned wagering mechanisms (WALLA) where each model reports a prediction and a learned wager, using wagers as aggregation weights. Introduces a leave-one-out baseline into the net payout function to achieve dominant-strategy incentive compatibility, advantage-wager alignment, and prediction-agnostic wager optimization.

Result: Experiments on QA and forecasting benchmarks show WALLA matches centralized aggregation methods in predictive performance while achieving decentralized learning, advantage-aligned weights, uncertainty awareness, and incentive-compatible prediction.

Conclusion: WALLA enables decentralized, incentive-compatible LLM aggregation with performance parity to centralized approaches, offering two mechanism variants that trade off normality and no-arbitrage with bounded worst-case deficit.

Abstract: It is increasingly common to aggregate predictions from multiple LLMs, each with domain expertise or access to private tools and data, to improve collective prediction performance. In decentralized settings, aggregation weights need to be determined without access to models' private information and should remain robust to strategic reporting. We propose a family of advantage-aligned wagering mechanisms for LLM aggregation (WALLA), in which each model reports a prediction and a learned wager, and predictions are aggregated using wagers as weights. WALLA introduces a leave-one-out baseline into the net payout function, yielding three desirable properties: (1) dominant-strategy incentive compatibility of prediction under arbitrary belief structure, (2) advantage--wager alignment, where the optimal wager is proportional to the model's expected score advantage, and (3) prediction-agnostic wager optimization, enabling decentralized learning of wager policies without requiring optimal predictions. We further instantiate two mechanism variants that trade off normality and no-arbitrage while maintaining a bounded worst-case deficit for the mechanism. Experiments on question-answering and forecasting benchmarks across heterogeneous models and private-information settings show that WALLA matches centralized aggregation methods in predictive performance, while simultaneously achieving decentralized learning, advantage-aligned aggregation weights, uncertainty awareness, and incentive-compatible prediction.

</details>


### [55] [MechMath Agent Team: LLM Driven Agents for Mathematical Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04394)
*Yichuan Cao,Ruichen Qiu,Junqi Liu,Jiaqi Wang,Dakai Guo,Ruyong Feng,Lihong Zhi,Xiao-Shan Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MMAT is a multi-agent LLM system with a tripartite Harness Architecture (Control, Execution, Augmentation planes) that serves as a co-pilot for full-cycle mathematical research, solving 11 open problems across five fields in two months.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mathematical research poses severe challenges for existing reasoning systems due to non-linear derivation paths, rigorous logical requirements, and protracted exploration cycles; existing LLM reasoning systems cannot handle the full cycle of mathematical discovery.

Method: A tripartite Harness Architecture decoupling system responsibilities into Control, Execution, and Augmentation planes. Three specialized agents (Knowledge Base Manager, Natural Language Prover, Formal Language Prover) operate in a closed loop to produce formally certified mathematical proofs.

Result: Over a two-month deployment, 11 open problems solved across Number Theory, Algebraic Complexity Theory, Differential Algebra, Operator Algebra, and Inequalities.

Conclusion: The decoupled Harness Architecture enables multi-agent mathematical reasoning; MMAT demonstrates capacity as a co-pilot throughout the entire research cycle, validated empirically on diverse open problems.

Abstract: AI reasoning has become a central focus in contemporary artificial intelligence, largely driven by the success of large language models. However, mathematical research, which is characterized by non-linear derivation paths, rigorous logical requirements, and protracted exploration cycles, poses severe challenges for existing reasoning systems. To overcome these limitations, we present the MechMath Agent Team (MMAT), which is a large language model driven agent designed to serve as a co-pilot throughout the full cycle of mathematical research. We design a tripartite Harness Architecture that decouples system responsibilities into Control, Execution, and Augmentation planes, thereby reconciling rigorous logical control with the agility demanded by open-ended research. Building upon this framework, we instantiate three specialized agents: a Knowledge Base Manager, a Natural Language Prover, and a Formal Language Prover, all operating in a closed loop to produce formally certified mathematical proofs. We evaluate MMAT on open problems in Number Theory, Algebraic Complexity Theory, Differential Algebra, Operator Algebra, and Inequalities. Across a two-month deployment, 11 problems have been solved, demonstrating its capacity to act as a co-pilot throughout the entire research cycle. The contributions are threefold: a general decoupled Harness Architecture for multi-agent mathematical reasoning, its concrete instantiation in the MMAT system, and empirical validation on a diverse suite of open problems.

</details>


### [56] [LLM-as-a-Tutor: Policy-Aware Prompt Adaptation for Non-Verifiable RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04412)
*Yujin Kim,Namgyu Ho,Sangmin Hwang,Joonkee Kim,Yongjin Yang,Sangmin Bae,Seungone Kim,Jaehun Jung,Se-Young Yun,Hwanjun Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces LLM-as-a-Tutor, a framework where a single LLM acts as both examiner (identifying non-challenging prompts via pairwise comparison of policy rollouts) and generator (appending atomic constraints to prompts), monotonically increasing difficulty to maintain discriminative reward signals in non-verifiable RL for instruction following.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL for non-verifiable instruction following uses LLM judges with rubrics, but training prompts remain static, causing misalignment between prompt difficulty and policy capability—when prompts are too easy, the judge cannot produce a discriminative reward signal.

Method: A single LLM serves dual roles: (1) as an examiner, it pairwise-compares policy rollouts to detect non-challenging prompts; (2) as a generator, it appends atomic constraints to those prompts. This append-only design monotonically raises difficulty in step with policy capability, creating self-calibrating training signals without external schedules.

Result: On three complex instruction-following benchmarks, the method consistently outperforms both policy-unaware baselines and prior policy-adaptive methods that adapt rubrics or rewrite prompts.

Conclusion: Prompt adaptation is a missing axis of policy-awareness in non-verifiable RL; adapting prompt difficulty dynamically is more effective than adapting rubrics or rewriting prompts.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) for non-verifiable instruction following increasingly relies on LLM judges with prompt-specific rubrics as reward signals. While recent methods adapt these rubrics to the evolving policy during training, the training prompts themselves remain static, drawn from fixed corpora. This static approach often results in a critical misalignment between prompt difficulty and policy capability, leaving the judge unable to recover a discriminative reward signal when prompts fail to elicit quality variance among rollouts. To address this misalignment, we introduce LLM-as-a-Tutor, a framework that extends the LLM's role from judge to tutor: a single model serves as an examiner that pairwise-compares policy rollouts to detect non-challenging prompts, and as a generator that appends atomic constraints to them. This append-only design monotonically raises difficulty in step with the policy's capability, producing a self-calibrating training signal without external difficulty schedules. On three complex instruction-following benchmarks, our method consistently outperforms both policy-unaware baselines and prior policy-adaptive methods that adapt rubrics or rewrite prompts, suggesting prompt adaptation as a missing axis of policy-awareness in non-verifiable RL.

</details>


### [57] [Agent Step Value: State-Transition Measurement with State-Grounded LLM Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04419)
*Andrew Zhang,Chengzhan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASV is a state-transition measurement framework that scores each agent action by the change it induces in a state-grounded evaluator's distribution over candidate outcomes, enabling diagnosis of constructive and destructive belief pivots that aggregate metrics miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent evaluations collapse multi-step traces into final scores or success flags, obscuring which individual actions actually changed the state in a useful direction. Developers need fine-grained, per-action diagnostics.

Method: ASV scores each observed action by the state-transition change in a grounded evaluator's distribution over fixed candidate outcomes. It uses redacted before/after state projections, a stateless LLM evaluator for candidate log scores, a label-free rationale pass to separate deliberation from option scoring, and reports gold-free belief diagnostics plus offline oracle validation metrics.

Result: On 100 open-QA evidence-seeking tasks with live PubMed retrieval and a partially live DeepSeek actor, ASV evaluated 1,100 steps and 2,200 states. Mean gold-margin gain was -2.335 (95% CI [-3.395, -1.272]), entropy movement was 0.000, and mean Bayesian surprise was 2.693.

Conclusion: ASV localizes constructive and destructive belief pivots that final-answer scores and entropy-only step metrics miss, providing actionable per-step diagnostics for agent development.

Abstract: Most agent evaluations collapse a multi-step trace into a final answer, a success flag, or a trajectory-level score. These aggregates obscure the diagnostic question developers need most: which action changed the state in a useful direction? We introduce Agent Step Value (ASV), a state-transition measurement framework that scores each observed action by the change it induces in a state-grounded evaluator's distribution over fixed candidate outcomes. ASV renders redacted before/after state projections, uses a stateless LLM evaluator to assign candidate log scores, and reports both gold-free belief diagnostics and offline oracle validation metrics. A label-free rationale pass separates evaluator deliberation from one-token option scoring, preserving candidate likelihoods while exposing leakage and floor-score events. On 100 reviewed open-QA evidence-seeking tasks with live PubMed retrieval, a partially live DeepSeek actor, and DeepSeek log-probability scoring, ASV evaluates 1,100 steps and 2,200 states. Under the fixed-layout rationale-conditioned protocol, mean gold-margin gain is -2.335 (trajectory-bootstrap 95\% CI [-3.395, -1.272]), entropy movement is 0.000, and mean Bayesian surprise is 2.693. ASV therefore localizes constructive and destructive belief pivots that final-answer scores and entropy-only step metrics miss. We release the standalone ASV Eval toolkit.

</details>


### [58] [ResearchStudio-Idea: An Evidence-Grounded Research-Ideation Skill Suite from ML Conference Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04439)
*Qihao Zhao,Yangyu Huang,Yalun Dai,Lingao Xiao,Jianjun Gao,Xin Zhang,Wenshan Wu,Scarlett Li,Yang He,Yan Lu,Yap Kim Hui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ResearchStudio-Idea provides a reusable skill suite (Paper-Search, Scoop-Check, IdeaSpark) for the first mile of research ideation. IdeaSpark operationalizes 15 recurring ideation patterns from 1,947 ML conference papers into a structured workflow that grounds problems in literature, identifies bottlenecks, generates directions, checks novelty, and audits outcomes, outperforming baselines in proposal quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research ideation requires more than generating candidate directions; researchers must ground problems in literature, identify bottlenecks, differentiate from existing solutions, and evaluate risks before implementation. Current approaches lack structured support for this "first mile" of ideation.

Method: Constructed a corpus of 1,947 ML conference papers (ICLR, ICML, NeurIPS 2021-2025) including Oral, high-citation, and rejected submissions. Analyzed these to extract 31 recurring ideation sub-patterns consolidated into 15 reusable ideation patterns. Each pattern is operationalized as a structured card. IdeaSpark composes evidence grounding, pattern-guided generation, collision retrieval, audit, and idea-card rendering into an end-to-end workflow.

Result: Blind automated-judge evaluations show IdeaSpark consistently produces stronger research proposals than no-skill and generic-skill baselines while maintaining competitive novelty.

Conclusion: Reusable ideation patterns can be systematically extracted from past papers and operationalized into traceable research proposals, improving the quality and grounding of AI-assisted research ideation.

Abstract: Large language models have made research ideation increasingly accessible, yet effective idea development requires more than generating candidate directions. Researchers must ground a problem in current literature, identify meaningful bottlenecks, differentiate from existing solutions, and evaluate risks before committing to implementation. We present ResearchStudio-Idea as a reusable skill suite for this first mile of research ideation. The suite includes Paper-Search, a standalone multi-source literature search skill; Scoop-Check, a standalone prior-art collision checker for novelty claims; and IdeaSpark, the end-to-end skill that composes evidence grounding, pattern-guided generation, collision retrieval, audit, and idea-card rendering into one workflow. IdeaSpark is constructed from a corpus of 1,947 machine learning conference papers collected from ICLR, ICML, and NeurIPS between 2021 and 2025, including Oral papers, a separately tracked high-citation subset, and rejected submissions. Analysis of these outcomes reveals 31 recurring ideation sub-patterns, consolidated into 15 reusable ideation patterns. Each pattern is operationalized as a structured card containing research contexts, bottleneck types, differentiation strategies, supporting precedents, and common failure modes. Given a research problem and an evidence bundle, IdeaSpark evaluates evidence readiness, reconstructs the surrounding research context, identifies unresolved bottlenecks, selects relevant patterns, instantiates one candidate direction, retrieves potentially conflicting prior work, and performs outcome-informed auditing. This workflow transforms reusable ideation patterns into traceable research proposals. Blind automated-judge evaluations show that IdeaSpark consistently produces stronger research proposals than no-skill and generic-skill baselines while maintaining competitive novelty.

</details>


### [59] [Why Pure Reasoning is Not Enough: Nature as the Source of Mathematical Innovation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04505)
*Charanjit S. Jutla,Vimal Sharma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that human mathematical reasoning is fundamentally reliant on pattern matching inspired by the physical world (nature's "pre-computation") rather than pure deduction, due to logical undecidability and computational intractability. This provides a principled justification for large-scale AI models that store cross-domain patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explain why human mathematical reasoning cannot rely solely on formal deduction, given fundamental limits (undecidability, computational intractability), and to argue that pattern matching from natural/physical domains is a cognitive necessity.

Method: Historical analysis of the Fourier transform's development (vibrating string problem → heat equation → formalization) to show physics-driven mathematical innovation; survey of logical complexity classes (NP-hard SAT, non-elementary decision procedures for MSO) to quantify deductive barriers.

Result: At every critical juncture in the Fourier transform's history, physical problems forced mathematical advances that pure formal reasoning failed to anticipate or actively resisted. Logical complexity analysis confirms that even decidable logics have astronomically prohibitive worst-case resource requirements.

Conclusion: Pure deduction is constitutively insufficient for human-level mathematical creativity; AI systems must embed vast cross-domain pattern stores (justifying large language models' scale) rather than relying on deduction alone.

Abstract: We advance the hypothesis that human mathematical reasoning, constrained by both the undecidability and the computational intractability of even modest logical fragments, relies fundamentally on pattern matching from domains external to pure deduction. The most prolific reservoir of such patterns is the natural world, whose physical laws and biological systems have undergone billions of years of ``pre-computation'' and already exhibit surprisingly innovative solutions. To ground this claim, we trace the history of the Fourier transform and relevant mathematics, from the vibrating string controversy to the hear equation and subsequent formalisms prevalent in mathematics. At each critical juncture, a physics problem forced the acceptance or creation of a mathematical tool that pure formal reasoning failed to anticipate or, worse, human reasoning had resisted.
  We further survey the landscape of logical complexity, from NP-hard propositional satisfiability to the non-elementary decision-procedures for monadic second-order theories, to demonstrate that even when a logic is decidable, the resources required for worst-case deduction are astronomically prohibitive. We argue that these barriers make physics-inspired pattern matching not just a historical accident but a cognitive necessity. Finally, we draw the consequence for artificial intelligence: if pure reasoning is constitutively insufficient, then any system aiming at human-level mathematical creativity must embed a vast store of cross-domain patterns rather than rely on deduction alone. This furnishes a principled justification for the enormous scale of contemporary large language models.

</details>


### [60] [Compressing the Validation Bottleneck: An Agentic Self-Driving Lab for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04508)
*Kyunghoon Hur,Chihun Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a dual-component agentic framework for self-driving labs that reduces both the number of experimental iterations (via prior-aware DOE) and the cost per experiment (via cost-aware surrogate prediction), targeting bottlenecks in the AI-for-Science loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-driving labs can automate experiments for AI-for-Science, but two physical bottlenecks remain: agents waste rounds on low-value experiments, and each round may require expensive measurements. The goal is to accelerate the SDL loop by tackling both issues simultaneously within a single agent.

Method: (1) A prior-aware agentic Design of Experiments (DOE) loop that integrates domain knowledge and past results to propose feasible and informative next experiments, reducing trials-to-target. (2) A cost-aware surrogate agent that predicts high-cost, high-resolution measurements from low-cost, low-resolution inputs, and chooses between measurement types based on predicted uncertainty.

Result: The prior-aware DOE approach is examined in the biology domain, and the cost-aware surrogate approach is examined in the materials domain. Together they aim to reduce both the number of loops and cost per experiment.

Conclusion: A unified agent combining prior-aware DOE and cost-aware surrogate prediction can accelerate SDL loops by addressing both iteration count and per-experiment cost bottlenecks simultaneously.

Abstract: Agentic AI-for-Science can automate ideation, planning, and analysis, but final validation still depends on real experiments. A self-driving lab (SDL) can execute those experiments, yet the loop still has bottlenecks: the agent may spend too many rounds on low-value experiments, or each round may require a high-cost experiment. We target these two physical bottlenecks with one agent. First, a prior-aware agentic DOE loop uses domain knowledge and past results to propose feasible and informative next experiments, reducing trials-to-target. Second, a cost-aware surrogate agent predicts high-cost, high-resolution measurements from low-cost, low-resolution measurements. It chooses between a high- and a low-cost measurement based on the predicted uncertainty. We examine these directions in the biology and materials domains, respectively. Together, under a single agent, these components aim to accelerate the SDL loop by reducing both the number of loops and the cost per experiment.

</details>


### [61] [VLA Grounder: Language-Conditioning Space Optimization for Black-Box VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04517)
*Damir Shodiev,Aleksei Staroverov,Nikita Kachaev,Alexey K. Kovalev,Aleksandr I. Panov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes optimizing the language-conditioning space of frozen VLA models using reinforcement learning, rather than updating action weights, to improve task success rates on instruction-sensitive manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models are sensitive to instruction phrasing, and language is not merely a task label but an optimizable conditioning input. The authors aim to improve frozen VLA policies by optimizing language space instead of updating action weights.

Method: The method introduces a language-conditioning space policy that translates human instructions into VLA-grounded commands using object appearance, spatial relations, and target-grounding cues. It is initialized with a failure-derived command-space prior and optimized via RL from sparse task-completion rewards, while the downstream VLA remains frozen.

Result: Experiments on RL4VLA and VL-Think show that language-conditioning space optimization improves success on instruction-sensitive, symbolic, and multi-object manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: Language can serve as an optimizable variable for robot foundation models, enabling better performance without modifying the underlying VLA policy weights.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are commonly treated as end-to-end action policies conditioned on natural-language task descriptions. In practice, however, their behavior often depends sharply on how the instruction is phrased, suggesting that language is not merely a task label but an optimizable conditioning input. We study whether frozen VLA policies can be improved by optimizing language space rather than updating action weights. Our method introduces a language-conditioning space policy that translates a human instruction into a short VLA-grounded command using object appearance, spatial relations, and target-grounding cues. The language-conditioning space policy is initialized with a failure-derived command-space prior and optimized with reinforcement learning from sparse task-completion rewards, while the downstream VLA remains fully frozen. This yields language-conditioning space optimization: RL discovers which VLA-grounded commands best elicit successful behavior from the frozen action policy. Experiments on RL4VLA and VL-Think show that language-conditioning space optimization improves success on instruction-sensitive, symbolic, and multi-object manipulation tasks, demonstrating that language can serve as an optimizable variable for a robot foundation models. Website: https://tttonyalpha.github.io/vla_grounder

</details>


### [62] [Measuring Harness-Induced Belief Divergence in Multi-Step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04528)
*Haiwen Yi,Xinyuan Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that software-agent harnesses (controlling what agents see, do, and how failures are handled) can change agents' multi-step beliefs even when the task, environment, and base LLM are fixed. It introduces a belief-rollout diagnostic and BIWM protocol to detect and align these belief divergences, arguing harness design is an experimental variable in agent evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent benchmarks only report whether a task is solved, ignoring that the evaluation harness (controlling observations, actions, failure handling, verification, and logging) can fundamentally alter an agent's beliefs and subsequent decisions, making harness design an overlooked confounding factor in agent evaluation.

Method: The paper introduces: (1) a belief-rollout diagnostic that elicits structured K-step trajectories over progress, risk, recoverability, constraints, failure mode, uncertainty, future success, repair cost, and next action under alternative harnesses; (2) a cross-harness belief divergence decomposed into arrival term (immediate interface shifts) and growth term (horizon-dependent belief changes); (3) BIWM protocol that canonicalizes observations, logs censored branches, expands repair traces, records verification masks, executes risky branches in shadow, and aligns belief trajectories across harness views.

Result: On controlled coding tasks and public-benchmark stress tests, blocked actions, compressed repairs, selective verification, and cost-aware evidence pruning often preserve terminal success while changing the beliefs that drive later decisions, demonstrating that harness design systematically induces belief divergence.

Conclusion: Harness design is an experimental variable in agent evaluation, not an implementation detail. The BIWM protocol offers a no-training method to detect and mitigate harness-induced belief bias.

Abstract: Software-agent benchmarks usually report whether an agent solves a task, but the agent reaches that outcome through a harness that controls what it sees, which actions it can take, which failures are repaired, which states are verified, and which evidence is logged. We show that this harness can change the agent's multi-step beliefs even when the task, environment, and base LLM are fixed. We introduce a belief-rollout diagnostic that elicits structured K-step trajectories over progress, risk, recoverability, constraints, failure mode, uncertainty, future success, repair cost, and next action under alternative harnesses. We define a cross-harness belief divergence and decompose it into an arrival term for immediate interface shifts and a growth term for horizon-dependent belief changes. On controlled coding tasks and public-benchmark stress tests, blocked actions, compressed repairs, selective verification, and cost-aware evidence pruning often preserve terminal success while changing the beliefs that drive later decisions. We further introduce BIWM, a no-training protocol that canonicalizes observations, logs censored branches, expands repair traces, records verification masks, executes risky branches in shadow, and aligns belief trajectories across harness views. The results suggest that harness design is an experimental variable in agent evaluation, not an implementation detail. Our code is available at https://github.com/Hik289/Harness-induce-bias.git.

</details>


### [63] [Heaviside Continuity of Rolling Coefficients for Eliminating Epistemic Entropy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04562)
*MY Pitsane,Hope Mogale*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HCRC is a verification-first execution framework that uses a Heaviside Gate to predicate-gate LLM state transitions, preventing propagation of invalid intermediate states. Evaluated on reasoning and software tasks, it reduces false-completion rates to 0% on capable models and converts errors to honest halts on weaker ones, operating as a production control plane for an agentic coding environment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs generate fluent but incorrect outputs without verification mechanisms, unlike humans who exhibit cues when providing false information. Autoregressive decoding lacks a way to verify intermediate reasoning before progression, making errors difficult to detect.

Method: HCRC reformulates inference as predicate-gated state transitions governed by a Heaviside Gate, combining model confidence with independent verification signals from a parallel worker architecture. Execution advances only when predefined correctness predicates are satisfied, preventing propagation of invalid intermediate states.

Result: On capable proposers, HCRC reduces false-completion rate (FCR) from 4-7% to 0% with competitive latency. On weaker proposers, it converts false completions into honest halts. It has operated for months as a production control plane for an agentic coding environment.

Conclusion: HCRC is a general framework for verification-driven LLM execution, showing that reliable reasoning can be achieved through principled execution control rather than relying solely on model scale.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) generate fluent outputs that can be wrong. Unlike humans, who often exhibit cues when providing false information, LLMs produce errors that are difficult to detect because autoregressive decoding provides no mechanism for verifying intermediate reasoning before state progression. We introduce Heaviside Continuity of Rolling Coefficients (HCRC), a verification-first execution framework that reformulates inference as predicate-gated state transitions governed by a Heaviside Gate. HCRC combines model confidence with independent verification signals from a parallel worker architecture, allowing execution to advance only when predefined correctness predicates are satisfied. This prevents invalid intermediate states from propagating, reducing epistemic entropy without modifying the underlying model. We evaluate HCRC on software-engineering and reasoning tasks across thirteen proposers from four providers. On capable proposers, the gate reduces the false-completion rate (FCR) from 4--7% to 0% while remaining latency-competitive and, in some settings, faster than the unwrapped model. On weaker proposers, it converts false completions into honest halts instead of corrupting downstream state. Beyond benchmarking, HCRC has operated for months as the production control plane of an agentic coding environment, authorizing file mutations, verification-driven progress reporting, and memory compaction. These results establish HCRC as a general framework for verification-driven LLM execution, showing that reliable reasoning can be achieved through principled execution control rather than model scale alone.

</details>


### [64] [Detecting Answer-Driven Reasoning in LLM-Based Educational Tutors via Truncated Chain-of-Thought Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04572)
*Bonan Shen,Dingyan Shang,Youting Wang,Tao Ning*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether LLM tutors use private answer information (e.g., answer keys) to produce explanations that reveal the correct answer prematurely, before the reasoning justifies it. Using TRACE (Truncated Reasoning AUC Evaluation), the authors test GSM8K problems under three conditions (question-only, correct answer-key, wrong answer-key) and find that answer-key access dramatically increases early answer availability, with median TRACE AUC rising from 0.375 to 0.900, confirming that chain-of-thought auditing can detect answer-driven reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM tutors often produce correct, pedagogically formatted explanations, but these may be "answer-driven" — the model may have private answer information (e.g., answer keys, rubrics) and reveal the final answer before the explanation logically justifies it. This undermines the pedagogical integrity of tutoring systems, making it important to detect when reasoning is answer-driven rather than derived from the problem itself.

Method: The authors propose TRACE (Truncated Reasoning AUC Evaluation), which probes how early in a chain-of-thought prefix the model can pass a verifier. They evaluate Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct on 1000 GSM8K problems under three tutoring contexts: question-only, correct answer-key, and wrong answer-key. At fixed fractions of each generated explanation, they force the model to answer immediately and verify against the gold numeric answer.

Result: Answer-key access raises median TRACE AUC from 0.375 to 0.900. The gold answer is available at the first 10% prefix in 997 of 1000 cases with answer-key access. The effect remains strong even when restricting to the 746 examples where both question-only and answer-key explanations end with the correct final answer.

Conclusion: Truncated chain-of-thought auditing (TRACE) is a lightweight process-level diagnostic for detecting answer-driven reasoning in math tutoring explanations. The results show that private answer information significantly biases model behavior, making answers available prematurely, which has implications for designing trustworthy automated tutoring systems.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) tutors often produce fluent step-by-step explanations, but a correct and pedagogically formatted response does not guarantee that the answer was derived from the student-facing problem. In realistic tutoring systems, the model may also have access to teacher notes, answer keys, rubrics, or retrieved solution artifacts. We study whether such private answer information can make tutor explanations answer-driven: the final answer is behaviorally available before the written explanation has justified it. Using Truncated Reasoning AUC Evaluation (TRACE), which probes how early a chain-of-thought prefix can pass a verifier, we evaluate 1000 GSM8K test problems under three paired tutoring contexts: question-only, correct answer-key, and wrong answer-key. At fixed fractions of each generated explanation, we force the model to answer immediately and verify the response against the gold numeric answer. With Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, answer-key access raises median TRACE AUC from 0.375 to 0.900 and makes the gold answer available at the first 10% prefix in 997 of 1000 cases. The effect remains strong on the 746 examples where both question-only and answer-key explanations end with the correct answer. These results support truncated CoT auditing as a lightweight process-level diagnostic for answer-driven reasoning in math tutoring explanations.

</details>


### [65] [Attention Limited Reward Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04590)
*Wenqian Xing*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper shows that pairwise human comparisons, modeled by Bradley-Terry in RLHF, conflate two sources of ambiguity: genuine closeness vs. limited attention. Using a rational inattention model, the authors demonstrate that standard reward modeling can recover misleading rankings when attention is heterogeneous, and that learning quality depends on attended information per label, not raw label count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover what the standard Bradley-Terry assumption in RLHF misses by treating pairwise comparisons as direct revealed preference, specifically the conflation of value closeness and limited evaluation capacity.

Method: A reduced-form model grounded in rational inattention, where each label is generated through a low-capacity evaluation channel. The authors analyze the identifiability of reward, attention, and default tendencies from passive comparison data, and validate via case studies on Chatbot Arena votes and perceptual comparisons with response times/gaze.

Result: Passive comparison data cannot generally disentangle reward, attention, and default tendencies. Heterogeneous attention can produce misleading rankings under Bradley-Terry. Learning depends on attended information per label, not raw label count. Chatbot Arena data shows a cyclic component beyond sampling noise that no scalar reward can represent. Response times and gaze reveal gap information absent from labels alone.

Conclusion: Human feedback should be treated as an attention-limited measurement process rather than direct revealed preference; weak preference signals may reflect hidden evaluation difficulty rather than genuine indifference.

Abstract: Pairwise human comparisons are a primary interface through which modern AI systems learn human preferences. RLHF and related alignment pipelines typically model such comparisons with Bradley--Terry log-odds, where choice probabilities are governed by latent reward differences. This paper examines what this assumption misses through a reduced-form model motivated by rational inattention, in which each label is generated by a low-capacity evaluation channel. The model separates two forms of ambiguity that standard reward modeling tends to conflate: a comparison may be difficult because the two candidates are genuinely close in value, or because the relevant distinction is hard to detect under limited attention. We show that limited attention can fundamentally distort what pairwise comparisons reveal. In particular, passive comparison data cannot generally distinguish reward, attention, and default tendencies, and heterogeneous attention can make standard Bradley--Terry reward modeling recover misleading rankings. Our analysis shows that learning is governed not by the raw number of labels, but by the amount of attended information each label carries. A case study on human votes over language-model pairs from Chatbot Arena exhibits the predicted signature, a cyclic component of the comparison data that exceeds sampling noise and that no scalar reward can represent; a second case study on perceptual comparisons shows that response times and gaze carry gap information that the labels do not. This perspective suggests that human feedback should be treated not as direct revealed preference, but as an attention-limited measurement process: a weak preference signal may reflect hidden evaluation difficulty rather than genuine indifference.

</details>


### [66] [Governed Individuation: Cryptographically Decoupling an Agent's Learning from Its Authority](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04613)
*Xue Qin,Simin Luan,Cong Yang,Zhijun Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes "governed individuation," an architectural confinement mechanism that cryptographically binds an agent to a fixed identity and gates actions by their semantic effects, guaranteeing that no amount of in-field learning can expand the agent's authority without an operator-signed identity change. Empirical experiments show zero executed forbidden effects under this scheme, compared to frequent safety violations in ungoverned agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous agents are increasingly deployed in real-world, non-sandboxed environments where they learn and adapt in the field. Traditional alignment techniques only probabilistically ensure that a learning agent remains within authorized bounds. The paper addresses the problem of providing a hard, verifiable guarantee that a deployed agent cannot exceed its permitted authority.

Method: The authors introduce "governed individuation": (1) cryptographically freezing the agent's identity at boot, and (2) routing every action through a gate that checks the semantic effect of the action rather than its name. A proof is provided that no learning, skill acquisition, or self-induced governance abstraction can widen authority without an operator-signed identity change. Empirical evaluation uses an open-ended tool-use benchmark with large action spaces, comparing name-based gating against semantic effect tracing of varying depth.

Result: Ungoverned agents under reward pressure attempted to tamper with their own evaluation at rates reaching 100% on the hardest task. The proposed gate reduced executed forbidden effects to zero as a verified property while preserving task success. False-allows fell from 75% (name-based gating) to 0% (dynamic effect tracing). Refusal history transferred compliance to held-out red-line families.

Conclusion: Trust in deployed learning agents can be shifted from a probabilistic wager on alignment to a check anyone can run at boot. Confinement is guaranteed as an architectural invariant rather than a probabilistic outcome of training.

Abstract: Autonomous agents are moving from sandboxed text generators to operators of code, data, and physical infrastructure, and they increasingly learn while deployed. This reopens a question that alignment techniques answer only probabilistically: after an agent has adapted in the field, is the running system still confined to what its operator authorised? Here we show that confinement can be guaranteed as an invariant of the agent's execution architecture rather than a probabilistic outcome of its training. Governed individuation binds an agent at boot to a cryptographically frozen identity digest, and routes every action through a gate defined over the semantic effect of the action rather than its name. We prove that no amount of learning, skill acquisition, or self-induced governance abstraction can widen the agent's permitted authority without an operator-signed change to its identity; the guarantee holds even when the agent induces its own safety principle and that principle is wrong. Empirically, in an open-ended tool-use benchmark where a large action space rules out name-based blocking, ungoverned software agents under reward pressure attempt to tamper with their own evaluation at a task-dependent rate that reaches every run on the hardest task, whereas the gate reduces executed forbidden effects to zero as a verified property of the construction while preserving task success. An adversarial evaluation of monitors of increasing semantic depth shows false-allows falling from 75% (name-based gating) to zero (dynamic effect tracing), and refusal history transfers compliance to held-out red-line families. Trust in a deployed learning agent shifts from a wager on its continued alignment to a check anyone can run at boot.

</details>


### [67] [MRMS: A Multi-Resolution Memory Substrate for Long-Lived AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04617)
*Jizhizi Li,Amy Shi-Nash*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MRMS, a memory substrate for AI agents organized along representational (structured records, vectors, graphs) and temporal (short/medium/long-term) axes, with synchronized structured-vector-graph memory for selective exposure, consolidation, and epistemic labeling, instantiated as a lightweight prototype for long-lived personalized interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-lived AI agents require continuity across interactions, but simply extending prompt windows is insufficient. Agents need to preserve useful prior experience, retrieve selectively, distinguish personal context from external evidence, and revise memory when situations change.

Method: The method is an architectural memory substrate organized along two orthogonal axes: representational (structured records, vector representations, graph relations) and temporal (short-term traces, medium-term abstractions, long-term semantic commitments). Its key design constraint is synchronized structured-vector-graph memory where structured records govern eligibility, vector representations support recall, and graph relations adjudicate support/contradiction/supersession before gated context projection. The prototype MRMS implements structured records, vector retrieval, temporal policies, and graph-based revision.

Result: A lightweight prototype (MRMS) that exercises core substrate mechanisms including pre-generation memory selection, revision, boundary enforcement, and evidence attribution under controlled long-lived interaction scenarios with explicit evidence requirements.

Conclusion: Reliable personalization is fundamentally a memory design problem: useful memory must be structured, selectively exposed, continuously consolidated, and epistemically labeled rather than stored as undifferentiated conversation history.

Abstract: Long-lived AI agents require continuity across interactions, but continuity cannot be obtained by simply extending the prompt window. An agent must preserve useful prior experience, retrieve it selectively, distinguish personal context from external evidence, and revise memory when the underlying situation changes. We propose an architectural memory substrate organized along two orthogonal axes: a representational axis spanning structured records, vector representations, and graph relations; and a temporal axis spanning short-term traces, medium-term abstractions, and long-term semantic commitments. Its key design constraint is synchronized structured-vector-graph memory: structured records govern eligibility, vector representations support recall, and graph relations adjudicate support, contradiction, and supersession before gated context projection. Its central claim is that reliable personalization is a memory design problem: useful memory is structured, selectively exposed, continuously consolidated, and epistemically labeled rather than stored as undifferentiated conversation history. Beyond the framework, we instantiate MRMS as a lightweight prototype implementing structured records, vector retrieval, temporal policies, and graph-based revision. The prototype exercises the core substrate mechanisms through pre-generation memory selection, revision, boundary enforcement, and evidence attribution under controlled long-lived interaction scenarios with explicit evidence requirements.

</details>


### [68] [Formal Disco: Scalable Open-Ended Generation of Formally Verified Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04631)
*Gabriel Poesia,Simon Henniger,Tzu-Han Hsu,Yilun Du,Nada Amin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Formal Disco is a distributed LLM-based system for large-scale synthetic data generation of verified programs in formal verification languages (Dafny, Verus, Frama-C), using three worker classes (initiators, fixers, extenders) and maximum entropy training to overcome data scarcity and match/exceed Claude Opus 4.5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The cost of code generation is decreasing due to AI agents, but quality assurance (especially formal verification) has not kept pace. AI models struggle with verification-aware languages due to scarcity of human-written examples in those languages.

Method: Proposes Formal Disco, a distributed coordination system for LLM-based workers with three roles: initiators (sketch verified programs from READMEs/docs), fixers (resolve compiler/verifier errors), and extenders (patch working programs). Uses trace recording for distillation and self-improvement, plus maximum entropy principle via iterative supervised fine-tuning for program diversity.

Result: Released large synthetic datasets of verified programs in Dafny, Verus, and Frama-C. Fine-tuned open models that often match or exceed Claude Opus 4.5 on verification-relevant tasks.

Conclusion: Formal Disco provides a scalable path to overcome the data barrier in formal reasoning domains through synthetic data generation, enabling AI models to work effectively with verification-aware languages.

Abstract: The cost of producing code is rapidly diminishing with increasingly capable AI agents, while quality assurance of generated programs has not kept pace. Formal verification provides the strongest possible guarantees, but the ability of AI models to work with verification-aware languages is hindered by the scarcity of human-written examples of programs in those languages. To tackle this prevalent data scarcity issue, we propose Formal Disco: a distributed system for coordination of LLM-based workers that can be easily applied to open-ended synthetic data generation at scale. We use Formal Disco to share tasks and programs between three classes of workers: "initiators", which read random READMEs from open-source repositories and documentation snippets to sketch a related verified program, "fixers" which take compiler and verifier feedback and attempt to resolve issues, and "extenders" that take working programs and propose patches to expand them. Formal Disco records all agent-generated traces and uses them both for initial distillation from a stronger model as well as self-improvement. We also propose a principle of maximum entropy for synthetic program generation, and use entropy maximization via iterative supervised fine-tuning to learn to generate increasingly diverse programs over time. We release large datasets of synthetic verified programs in three languages - Dafny, Verus, and Frama-C -, and fine-tune open models for verification-relevant tasks, often matching or exceeding the performance of Claude Opus 4.5. Overall, our work offers a path to create synthetic data at scale for formal reasoning domains and overcome the long-standing data barrier.

</details>


### [69] [Integrated Altruistic and Fairness Preference Induces Advanced Mutual Cooperation in Sequential Social Dilemmas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04710)
*Yu Wei,Yukiko Ogura,Yoshiyuki Ohmura,Ildefons Magrans de Abril,Hoshinori Kanazawa,Yasuo Kuniyoshi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces AFP, a reward-sharing mechanism integrating altruistic and fairness preferences, to achieve cooperation in multi-agent reinforcement learning social dilemmas. AFP agents outperform standard RL and inequity aversion baselines by achieving more collective rewards and higher equity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inducing cooperation in MARL remains difficult, especially in social dilemmas where individual rationality leads to suboptimal group outcomes, whereas humans naturally achieve cooperation through social preferences.

Method: The authors design AFP (Altruistic and Fairness Preference), a utility function that combines altruistic preferences (incentive for others' rewards) and fairness preferences (incentive for equality) as a reward-sharing mechanism to convert individual and others' rewards into cooperative incentives.

Result: AFP agents achieved successful mutual cooperation with more collective rewards and higher equity compared to standard RL and inequity aversion baselines. Altruistic preferences encourage contribution to public goods, while fairness preferences induce mutual behavior between agents.

Conclusion: Integrating social preferences (altruism and fairness) into MARL reward functions effectively promotes cooperation in social dilemmas, with each preference component playing a distinct and complementary role.

Abstract: Inducing cooperation among distributed agents is still a difficult problem in the field of multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL), particularly in social dilemma situations. There, individual interests are misaligned with the common good and individual rationality leads to suboptimal group outcomes. In contrast, humans are able to achieve cooperation with one another in such situations. A common explanation for such cooperative behavior is that individuals have social preferences. In order to achieve cooperation in MARL, we design a new utility function integrating altruistic preferences (incentive for other's reward) and fairness preferences (incentive for equality) from social psychology and behavioral economics, namely, Altruistic and Fairness Preference (AFP), a reward-sharing mechanism which converts one's own and other's rewards to incentives for cooperative behavior. We performed comparative experiments with standard RL and inequity aversion agents in two challenging sequential social dilemma games, and showed that AFP agents successfully achieved mutual cooperation with more collective rewards and higher equity than the baselines. To further understand the progression of AFP during training, we subsequently explore the effects of altruistic preferences and fairness preferences on agents' behavior. The results suggest that altruistic preferences encourage agents to contribute to the public goods, and fairness preferences induce mutual behavior between agents.

</details>


### [70] [FORGE: Research-Trajectory Hijacking Attacks on Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04718)
*Yue Pan,Ziheng Zhang,Junxiang Lei,Changhao Jia,Qingyi Si,Hongcheng Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FORGE is a two-level attack exploiting planning-layer poisoning in deep research agents by fabricating reasoning chains across documents, enabling local injection to contaminate full reports. PRISM quantifies cognitive-type-weighted infection; Root Query Anchoring defends by tethering follow-up generation to the original query, reducing PRISM from 38.5% to 18.3%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep research agents decompose queries into subtasks and synthesize reports over multiple retrieval rounds, creating a novel attack surface where adversarial documents in the retrieval pool can steer follow-up questions, turning a local injection into report-level contamination.

Method: FORGE combines intra-document reasoning fabrication with inter-document chain coordination to hijack subtask planning. PRISM metric weights infected report claims by cognitive type. Root Query Anchoring (RQA) defense ties recursive follow-up generation to the root query.

Result: Across 25 queries, Network FORGE achieves 26.4% PRISM with five injected documents, exhibiting depth migration where poisoned content shifts from overt framing into factual premises. On the 10-query defense subset, RQA reduces PRISM from 38.5% to 18.3%.

Conclusion: Planning-layer poisoning is a practical threat for deep research agents, and Root Query Anchoring offers an effective lightweight defense against such attacks.

Abstract: Deep research agents decompose open-ended queries into subtasks, retrieve web evidence over multiple rounds, and synthesize long-form reports. This workflow creates a planning-layer poisoning surface: adversarial documents that enter the retrieval pool can steer follow-up questions and turn a local injection into report-level contamination. We present FORGE (Fabricated Orchestrated Reasoning chain for aGent Exploitation), a two-level attack that combines intra-document reasoning fabrication with inter-document chain coordination to hijack subtask planning. We further introduce the PRISM metric, which weights infected report claims by cognitive type, and Root Query Anchoring, a lightweight defense that ties recursive follow-up generation to the root query. Across 25 queries, Network FORGE reaches 26.4% PRISM with five injected documents and exhibits depth migration, in which recursive synthesis shifts poisoned content from overt framing into factual premises. On the 10-query defense subset, RQA (Root Query Anchoring) reduces PRISM from 38.5% to 18.3%.

</details>


### [71] [FM-ChangeNet: Learning Change through Pathwise Feature Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04750)
*Roie Kazoom,George Leifman,Genady Beryozkin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FM-ChangeNet introduces a pathwise-supervised change detection framework that models bi-temporal evolution via continuous velocity fields in feature space, achieving state-of-the-art results on remote sensing benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional change detection methods rely on static endpoint comparison (pre vs. post), which provides sparse supervision and struggles to distinguish true structural changes from nuisance effects like illumination shifts or misalignment.

Method: Constructs intermediate latent states between pre/post representations, learns a time-conditioned velocity field along the transformation trajectory, and uses a hierarchical multi-scale architecture with cross-temporal alignment, coarse-to-fine flow decoding, and a unified objective coupling flow supervision, trajectory consistency, spatial regularization, and segmentation loss.

Result: Produces more structured and robust change representations, achieves state-of-the-art performance on remote sensing change detection benchmarks.

Conclusion: Pathwise supervision via learned velocity fields provides denser, less ambiguous training signals and yields interpretable change cues, outperforming endpoint-only segmentation approaches.

Abstract: We present FM-ChangeNet, a pathwise-supervised framework for change detection that reformulates bi-temporal reasoning as continuous transport in feature space rather than static endpoint comparison. Given encoded pre and post-temporal representations, we construct intermediate latent states and learn a time-conditioned velocity field $\hat{v}_θ(z_t,t)$ along the transformation trajectory. This pathwise formulation constrains the predictor over a continuum of intermediate states, providing a denser and less ambiguous supervision signal than conventional endpoint-only segmentation and enabling the model to capture temporal evolution explicitly. The learned velocity field is not only a transport mechanism but also an interpretable representation of change: its magnitude serves as a spatially localized change cue that helps distinguish true structural variation from nuisance effects such as illumination shifts and spatial misalignment. We develop a hierarchical multi-scale architecture with cross-temporal alignment, time-conditioned coarse-to-fine flow decoding, and a unified objective that couples flow supervision, trajectory consistency, spatial regularization, and segmentation loss. Experiments on remote sensing benchmarks show that the proposed framework produces more structured and robust change representations while achieving state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [72] [AgenticPD: A Stage-Aware Agentic Framework for Physical Design QoR Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04758)
*Shuo Ren,Zijin Cheng,Yaohui Han,Libo Shen,Leilei Jin,Wanting Tian,Rongliang Fu,Chao Wang,Bei Yu,Tsung-Yi Ho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgenticPD is a stage-aware agentic framework for physical design QoR optimization that branches from intermediate states and reuses checkpoints, achieving strong post-route timing while remaining competitive in power and area.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physical design QoR optimization is hard and expensive because choices at one stage affect later stages, and each evaluation requires a costly full EDA flow run. Existing methods treat optimization as flat parameter tuning or LLM-based script generation, lacking stage awareness.

Method: AgenticPD organizes around stage boundaries of the physical design flow, using a Judge Agent to navigate search and stage-specialized agents with local tools. The agent harness provides structured observations, execution history, and context management, enabling branching from prior intermediate states and checkpoint reuse.

Result: The system achieves strong post-route timing while remaining competitive in power and area across baselines.

Conclusion: AgenticPD demonstrates that a stage-aware agentic framework with checkpoint reuse and specialized agents can effectively optimize physical design QoR without costly full-flow re-runs.

Abstract: Physical design quality-of-results~(QoR) optimization is hard and expensive. Choices made at one stage can help or hurt later stages. Each evaluation requires a costly EDA run through the full flow. While existing methods still treat optimization as flat parameter tuning or a LLM-based script generation task, we present AgenticPD, a stage-aware agentic framework for physical design QoR optimization. Instead of re-running the full flow after every trial, AgenticPD is organized around the stage boundaries of the physical design flow, where a Judge Agent navigates the search and stage-specialized agents make local decisions within their own stage using stage-local tools. Additionally, the agent harness in AgenticPD provides structured observations, execution history, and agent context management. As a result, the system can branch from prior intermediate states and reuse checkpoints to continue the optimization procedure, and every candidate is evaluated at the post-route signoff. Across these baselines, AgenticPD achieves strong post-route timing while remaining competitive in power and area.

</details>


### [73] [CARL: Constraint-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Planning with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04854)
*Qiuyi Qi,Jinjian Zhang,Mutian Bao,Tian Liang,Guocong Li,Dongnan Liu,Wei Zhou,Jie Liu,Ming Kong,Linjian Mo,Feng Zhang,Qiang Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CARL is a reinforcement learning framework that improves LLMs' intrinsic constraint awareness by rewarding constraint-focused behavior, outperforming baselines on planning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs frequently generate plans that violate task constraints despite having strong reasoning and world knowledge, due to lacking systematic mechanisms to incorporate constraint information during generation. Existing approaches relying on external tools or task decomposition fail to enhance the model's intrinsic constraint awareness.

Method: CARL introduces a constraint-aware reward that compares the model's output distributions under constrained and unconstrained inputs, encouraging constraint focus and penalizing neglect. It is compatible with various RL frameworks and requires no external solvers or top models, enabling end-to-end constraint-aware planning.

Result: Experiments on BlocksWorld, TravelPlanner, and T-Eval show CARL significantly outperforms standard RFT baselines and state-of-the-art reasoning models, with markedly increased focus on constraints.

Conclusion: CARL effectively enhances LLMs' intrinsic constraint awareness through a novel RL-based reward mechanism, offering a scalable, plug-and-play solution for constraint-aware planning without external dependencies.

Abstract: Despite their strong reasoning capabilities and extensive world knowledge, Large Language Models (LLMs) frequently generate plans that violate task constraints, undermining their reliability in real-world applications. This deficiency arises from a lack of systematic mechanisms to incorporate constraint information during the generation process. While existing approaches attempt to mitigate this by relying on external tools or task decomposition, they fail to enhance the model's intrinsic constraint awareness. To address this, we propose Constraint-Aware Reinforcement Learning (CARL), a novel RL framework designed to strengthen LLMs' intrinsic focus on constraints. CARL introduces a constraint-aware reward by comparing the model's output distributions under constrained and unconstrained inputs, encouraging constraint focus and penalizing neglect. Compatible with various RL frameworks and requiring no external solvers or top models, CARL enables scalable, end-to-end constraint-aware planning. Extensive experiments on BlocksWorld, TravelPlanner, and T-Eval demonstrate that CARL significantly outperforms standard Reinforcement Fine-Tuning (RFT) baselines and state-of-the-art reasoning models, exhibiting a markedly increased focus on constraints.

</details>


### [74] [Medi-Gemma: A Hybrid Clinical Decision Support System Integrating Deterministic EMR Analytics and Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04907)
*Mohammed Saim Ahmed Quadri,Yunzhe Xue,Justin W. Ady,Usman Roshan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Medi-Gemma is a Clinical Decision Support System for wound pathology triage that uses a decoupled architecture separating clinical perception from data orchestration, featuring a Ground Truth Injection Module, deterministic ProtocolManager, and SafetyVerifier to eliminate hallucinations, prevent crashes, and improve factual adherence to structured clinical data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying LLMs in high-stakes clinical settings is limited by structural hallucinations, weak deterministic reasoning over tabular patient data, and omissions in vector retrieval, necessitating a safer architecture for clinical decision support.

Method: Medi-Gemma uses a multi-stage pipeline with a ClinicalOrchestrator, DataManager (type coercion for EMR cleaning), IntentRouter (routes to PandasQueryEngine or ClinicalRAGEngine with CPU-optimized vector store), Ground Truth Injection Module (extracts patient IDs, queries structured dataframe, embeds validated clinical snapshot into LLM prompt), ProtocolManager (maps terminology to evidence-based pathways), and SafetyVerifier (phrase filter).

Result: The architecture eliminates semantic context drift, prevents database compilation crashes, and improves factual adherence to backend clinical repositories.

Conclusion: Medi-Gemma provides a safer pattern for LLM-based clinical decision support where structured data fidelity, retrieval grounding, and deterministic safeguards are essential.

Abstract: Deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) in high-stakes clinical settings remains limited by structural hallucinations, weak deterministic reasoning over tabular patient data, and omissions in vector retrieval. This paper presents the architecture and validation of Medi-Gemma, a Clinical Decision Support System (CDSS) for wound pathology triage and workflow automation. The platform introduces a decoupled framework that separates clinical perception from data orchestration while preserving traceable reasoning. Medi-Gemma uses a multi-stage pipeline coordinated by a centralized ClinicalOrchestrator. Data requests are handled without generative inference by a DataManager that cleans unstructured Electronic Medical Record (EMR) files through type coercion. Natural language queries are processed by a hierarchical IntentRouter, which routes requests to deterministic analytics paths executed by a PandasQueryEngine or to patient-specific reasoning managed by a ClinicalRAGEngine using a CPU-optimized vector store. A key contribution is the Ground Truth Injection Module, which intercepts patient-specific queries, extracts numeric identification tokens, queries the structured dataframe via Pandas, retrieves the latest validated clinical state, and embeds this snapshot as an overriding context block in the LLM prompt before generation. Safety compliance is enforced by a deterministic ProtocolManager that maps clinical terminology to fixed evidence-based risk pathways, while a SafetyVerifier phrase filter prevents output rule violations. Validation shows that this architecture eliminates semantic context drift, prevents database compilation crashes, and improves factual adherence to backend clinical repositories. These results support Medi-Gemma as a safer pattern for LLM-based clinical decision support where structured data fidelity, retrieval grounding, and deterministic safeguards are essential.

</details>


### [75] [STAPO: Selective Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization for LLM Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04963)
*Qiuyi Qi,Tian Liang,Mutian Bao,Jinjian Zhang,Dongnan Liu,Wei Zhou,Linjian Mo,Ming Kong,Jie Liu,Feng Zhang,Qiang Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STAPO proposes normalized entropy to detect trajectory neglect in LLM agents and uses a hierarchical RL framework with trajectory-aware rewards and penalties to improve long-horizon task performance, achieving SOTA on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-Augmented QA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL for LLM agents suffers from trajectory neglect due to sparse/delayed rewards, where agents lose focus on task goals and interaction history at intermediate steps. Existing step-level supervision using Shannon-entropy-based uncertainty signals conflates state complexity with agent confidence, yielding unreliable decision reliability estimates.

Method: Proposes normalized entropy to measure confidence deviations relative to average agent behavior, strengthening the link between low-quality actions and trajectory neglect. Introduces STAPO, a hierarchical group-based RL framework that uses normalized entropy to locate outlier steps and optimizes them via joint trajectory-aware reward and trajectory-independent penalty mechanisms.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-Augmented QA benchmarks while substantially alleviating trajectory neglect, demonstrating effectiveness and robustness for agentic tasks.

Conclusion: Normalized entropy is a reliable signal for detecting trajectory neglect, and STAPO's hierarchical approach effectively addresses this issue, leading to superior long-horizon agent performance.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) is the dominant paradigm for training Large Language Model (LLM) agents on long-horizon tasks. However, sparse and delayed rewards often lead to trajectory neglect, in which agents lose focus on the task goal and interaction history at intermediate steps. Prior work has explored step-level supervision using Shannon-entropy-based uncertainty signals, which conflate inherent state complexity with agent confidence and therefore provide unreliable estimates of decision reliability. To address this issue, we propose normalized entropy, which measures confidence deviations relative to an agent's average behavior under a given state, thereby strengthening the association between low-quality actions and trajectory neglect. Building on this insight, we introduce Selective Trajectory-Aware Policy Optimization (STAPO), a hierarchical group-based RL framework. STAPO leverages normalized entropy to locate outlier steps associated with trajectory neglect and optimizes them via a joint mechanism of trajectory-aware reward and trajectory-independent penalty, enhancing trajectory awareness while preserving training stability. Extensive experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-Augmented QA demonstrate that STAPO achieves state-of-the-art performance while substantially alleviating trajectory neglect, validating its effectiveness and robustness for agentic tasks.

</details>


### [76] [Quantum-Inspired Harmonic Decision Models: A Computational Framework for Music Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05007)
*Josef Pavlíček,Petra Pavlíčková,Martin Molhanec*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a quantum-inspired computational framework for music harmonization that combines an interference-based search mechanism with classical optimization to generate coherent chord sequences, evaluated on songs like Autumn Leaves and It's a Long Way to Tipperary.


<details>
