Specifically, a novel adaptive feature fusion framework is proposed that leverages the complementary strengths of a self-supervised vision transformer encoder for its superior semantic understanding and a large-scale generative model encoder for its spatially-aware capability. Furthermore, we introduce a hierarchical prompt learning mechanism to adapt to varying procedural contexts. Finally, a scene-guided attention decoder is proposed to focus on critical surgical areas while suppressing background distractions. To validate the effectiveness, we established a new dataset, derived from publicly available surgical datasets with affordance annotations for three basic surgical actions: aspiration, clipping, and retraction. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance. Moreover, we validate our framework's applicability for downstream automation on a realistic lung and prostate phantom, and results show that the predicted affordance maps successfully enable autonomous surgical actions.

</details>


### [154] [ACE-Brain-0.5: A Unified Embodied Foundational Model for Physical Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04426)
*ACE-Brain Team,:,Ziyang Gong,Haoming Gu,Zehang Luo,Tianyi Zhang,Tao Tao,Yixiao Chi,Zhe Liu,Lingsi Zhu,Jingyuan Liu,Anke Tang,Songze Li,Yilun Kong,Ningjing Liu,Tianyu Zhu,Yunpeng Qing,Shuang Luo,Xiang Liu,Shi Fu,Dawei Nie,Sixiang Liu,Zhexi Wen,Feng Pan,Xiaofeng Wang,Zhi Hou,Chunxiao Liu,Xue Yang,Junchi Yan,Hengshuang Zhao,Dacheng Tao,Xiaogang Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ACE-Brain-0.5 is a unified embodied foundation model (8B parameters) that integrates five coupled functions—spatial perception, decision making, embodied interaction, self-monitoring, and self-improvement—into a closed-loop system, using a novel SSR+ training method to avoid cross-task interference, achieving strong results across 15 benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing embodied AI systems are fragmented: end-to-end policies lack spatial reasoning and execution assessment, while robot-agent systems orchestrate tools without learning shared representations, limiting progress toward general Physical Agentic AI.

Method: Built on ACE-Brain-0's spatial intelligence scaffold, ACE-Brain-0.5 uses a single 8B backbone to instantiate four functions (spatial perception, decision making, embodied interaction, self-monitoring) via the proposed SSR+ method—extending Scaffold-Specialize-Reconcile with a Reactivate stage after task-vector merging to prevent cross-task interference. The fifth function (self-improvement) is realized by a companion framework updating external execution state from rollouts.

Result: Across 15 benchmarks, ACE-Brain-0.5 improves over ACE-Brain-0 on 14 of 18 spatial perception/grounding benchmarks, achieves competitive navigation and manipulation performance, and provides strong progress estimation in both in-distribution and out-of-distribution settings.

Conclusion: ACE-Brain-0.5 demonstrates that unifying perception, planning, action, monitoring, and learning in a single foundation model is feasible, marking an early step toward general Physical Agentic AI.

Abstract: Embodied AI is moving from isolated perception or action modules toward physical agents that understand, plan under goals, act through robot bodies, monitor progress, and improve from experience. Existing systems address this loop only in parts: end-to-end policies generate actions but often lack spatial reasoning, planning, and execution assessment, while robot-agent systems orchestrate tools or specialists but do not learn a shared representation. This fragmentation limits general Physical Agentic AI. We present ACE-Brain-0.5, a unified embodied foundation model that organizes robot intelligence into five coupled functions: spatial perception, decision making, embodied interaction, self-monitoring, and self-improvement. Built on ACE-Brain-0, which established spatial intelligence as a shared scaffold across robot platforms, ACE-Brain-0.5 extends an understanding-centric model into a closed-loop foundation model. A single 8B backbone instantiates the first four functions: grounding objects and affordances, reasoning over 3D and egocentric spatial relations, decomposing instructions into subgoals, generating navigation and manipulation actions, and estimating progress for verification and recovery. To unify these capabilities without cross-task interference, we introduce SSR+, which extends Scaffold-Specialize-Reconcile with a Reactivate stage after task-vector merging. The fifth function, self-improvement, is realized by a companion framework that updates external execution state, including task schemas, spatial memory, and failure-recovery cases, from rollouts. Across fifteen benchmarks, ACE-Brain-0.5 improves over ACE-Brain-0 on 14 of 18 spatial perception and grounding benchmarks, achieves competitive navigation and manipulation performance, and provides strong progress estimation in ID and OOD settings. Together, these results mark an early step toward general Physical Agentic AI.

</details>


### [155] [RoboDojo: A Unified Sim-and-Real Benchmark for Comprehensive Evaluation of Generalist Robot Manipulation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04434)
*Tianxing Chen,Yue Chen,Zixuan Li,Junyuan Tang,Kailun Su,Weijie Wan,Baijun Chen,Haoran Lu,Haowen Yan,Honghao Su,Zhiyang Dou,Kaixuan Wang,Dandan Zhang,Yunze Liu,Yan Qin,Qiwei Liang,Qiwei Wu,Zijian Lin,Wenwei Lin,Yuran Wang,Minghua He,Tianshu Wu,Ruihai Wu,Jingquan Zhou,Kai-Chong Lei,Haibao Yu,Yuanfeng Ji,Weiyang Jin,Guanyu Lin,Xiaofan Li,Qi Xiong,Renjing Xu,Zhongyu Li,Wenhao Chai,Enze Xie,Ziwei Wang,Yao Mu,Hao Dong,Wojciech Matusik,Mingyu Ding,Wenbo Ding,Ping Luo,Masayoshi Tomizuka*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RoboDojo is a unified sim-and-real benchmark with 42 simulation and 18 real-world tasks for evaluating generalist robot manipulation policies across five capability dimensions, supported by parallel simulation, reproducible real-world evaluation with cloud access, and a policy integration framework (XPolicyLab).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robot manipulation benchmarks are limited: they use simple, short-horizon, or skill-narrow tasks with limited capability coverage, and are conducted only in simulation or only in the real world. Simulation misses physical deployment challenges, while real-world evaluation is costly, time-consuming, and hard to reproduce.

Method: RoboDojo provides 42 simulation and 18 real-world tasks covering diverse manipulation capabilities. Simulation evaluates generalization, memory, precision, long-horizon execution, and open-vocabulary instruction following. Real-world exposes policies to physical deployment challenges. It uses heterogeneous parallel simulation in Isaac Sim and RoboDojo-RealEval for reproducible real-world evaluation with remote cloud access, standardized hardware, scene reset, protocol, and deployment interface. XPolicyLab enables one-time policy integration across sim and real.

Result: Integrated 30 policies into XPolicyLab, evaluated them on RoboDojo, and established a public leaderboard with systematic analysis of current policy performance.

Conclusion: RoboDojo enables comprehensive, reproducible, and scalable evaluation of generalist robot manipulation policies across both simulation and real-world settings, bridging the gap between simulated and physical deployment evaluation.

Abstract: Generalist robot manipulation policies have advanced rapidly, yet existing benchmarks remain limited in systematically evaluating their capabilities. Many rely on simple, short-horizon, or skill-narrow tasks with limited capability coverage, and are often conducted only in simulation or only in the real world. Simulation enables scalable feedback but misses physical deployment challenges, while real-world evaluation is costly, time-consuming, and difficult to reproduce. We introduce RoboDojo, a unified sim-and-real benchmark for comprehensive evaluation of generalist robot manipulation policies. RoboDojo includes 42 simulation tasks and 18 real-world tasks covering diverse and complementary manipulation capabilities. The simulation benchmark evaluates five dimensions: generalization, memory, precision, long-horizon execution, and open-vocabulary instruction following, while the real-world benchmark exposes policies to challenging physical-world deployment conditions. RoboDojo supports scalable evaluation through heterogeneous parallel simulation in Isaac Sim and provides RoboDojo-RealEval, a reproducible real-world evaluation system with remote cloud access, standardized hardware, scene reset, evaluation protocol, and deployment interface. Together with XPolicyLab, policies can be integrated once and evaluated across simulation and real-world settings with minimal adaptation. We integrate 30 policies into XPolicyLab and evaluate them on RoboDojo, establishing a public leaderboard and systematic analysis of current policy performance. The website is available at http://robodojo-benchmark.com/.

</details>


### [156] [Robustness Verification of an Autonomous Underwater Vehicle-based Plankton Classifier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04453)
*Abdelrahman Sayed Sayed,Pierre-Jean Meyer,Asgeir J. Sørensen,Mohamed Ghazel*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a robustness verification framework using reachability analysis and a neural ODE classifier for in-situ plankton imaging on AUVs, providing formal guarantees against environmental perturbations to reduce misclassifications and manual validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI-based plankton classifiers on AUVs lack robustness in dynamic, unstructured underwater environments, where noise and non-biological artifacts cause frequent misclassifications that require time-consuming manual validation by marine biologists.

Method: Introduces a robustness verification framework based on reachability analysis, coupled with a continuous-time neural ODE classification model designed for the high-resolution SilCam particle imager.

Result: The verification framework formally guarantees model stability against environmental perturbations, acting as an automated filter that improves reliability and reduces post-processing workload.

Conclusion: The proposed approach enhances autonomous plankton sampling reliability by providing formal robustness guarantees, minimizing the need for manual validation in ambiguous conditions.

Abstract: The assessment of planktonic standing stocks and microorganism structures is critical for understanding upper ocean biological processes. Currently, autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) equipped with in-situ optical imaging and artificial intelligence (AI) methods offer a promising solution for persistent surveillance, mapping and monitoring of planktonic life. However, current AI methods often lack robustness in dynamic, unstructured environments, where environmental noise and non-biological artifacts lead to frequent misclassifications. Standard convolutional neural network (CNN) classifiers often struggle with such conditions, leading to misclassifications that require time-consuming manual validation by marine biologists.
  To address this issue, we propose a novel robustness verification framework for in-situ plankton classifiers based on reachability analysis. We also introduce a continuous-time neural ordinary differential equation (neural ODE) classification model leveraging the high-resolution imaging capabilities of the SilCam particle imager. In this paper, we demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework by formally verifying the robustness of the neural ODE model against environmental perturbations. We demonstrate that our verification framework acts as an automated filter providing formal guarantees of model stability against ambiguous data, thereby improving the reliability of autonomous sampling and reducing the post-processing workload.

</details>


### [157] [Mask2Real-WM: Segmentation Masks as a Sim-to-Real Bridge for Controllable Dexterous World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04546)
*Riccardo O. Feingold,Davide Liconti,Chenyu Yang,Robert K. Katzschmann*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Mask2Real-WM is a two-stage action-conditioned world model for dexterous manipulation that decouples pixel prediction into a dynamics model (predicting future segmentation masks from past masks and 23-DoF actions) and a rendering model (mapping predicted masks to photorealistic RGB via ControlNet-augmented Stable Video Diffusion). Pretraining on 50h of synthetic data and fine-tuning on <2.5h of real data enables per-DoF controllability, outperforming monolithic baselines on fine-grained joint-level prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing action-conditioned world models struggle with dexterous manipulation due to high-dimensional action spaces (23 DoF) and the sim-to-real gap. The authors aim to achieve per-DoF action controllability by leveraging the smaller domain gap in segmentation space for better generalization from simulation to real.

Method: 1) Decouple world modeling into a dynamics model (predicts future segmentation masks conditioned on past masks and 23-DoF actions) and a rendering model (ControlNet-augmented Stable Video Diffusion for mask-to-RGB mapping). 2) Pretrain the dynamics model on >50h of synthetic simulation data, then fine-tune on <2.5h of real demonstrations.

Result: On a dexterous pick-and-place benchmark, Mask2Real-WM achieves per-DoF action controllability across all 23 degrees of freedom. Both mask conditioning and simulation pretraining are required for this fine-grained control. Monolithic baselines capture coarse hand/end-effector trajectories but fail to reflect per-joint action effects.

Conclusion: Decoupling dynamics from rendering and pretraining in segmentation space is an effective strategy for building action-conditioned world models that support fine-grained control in high-dimensional dexterous manipulation tasks with limited real-world data.

Abstract: Action-conditioned world models allow robots to predict the future consequences of candidate actions without additional physical interaction, supporting policy evaluation, planning, and data augmentation. We present Mask2Real-WM, a two-stage action-conditioned world model for dexterous manipulation that decouples pixel prediction into a dynamics model and a rendering model. The dynamics model predicts future segmentation masks from past masks and 23-DoF action sequences. The rendering model maps the predicted masks to photorealistic RGB using a ControlNet-augmented Stable Video Diffusion backbone. The smaller sim-to-real gap in segmentation space enables the dynamics model to benefit from large-scale pretraining on over 50 h of synthetic simulation data, followed by fine-tuning on fewer than 2.5 h of real demonstrations. Experiments on a dexterous pick-and-place benchmark show that mask conditioning and simulation pretraining are both required for per-DoF action controllability across all 23 degrees of freedom. In contrast, monolithic baselines capture broad hand and end-effector trajectories but do not reliably reflect fine-grained, per-joint action effects.

</details>


### [158] [HUGS: Guiding Unified Dexterous Grasp Synthesis Across Modes and Scales via Learned Human Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04554)
*Mingrui Yu,Yongpeng Jiang,Yongyi Jia,Kangchen Lv,Li Huang,Yi Ren,Xiang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HUGS is a human-prior-guided framework for unified dexterous grasp synthesis across diverse object scales and contact modes (two-finger to bimanual). It learns an object-conditioned human prior from a compact dataset (1.8K grasps, 304 objects) to guide force-closure-aware optimization, adaptively proposing contact modes and wrist initializations. It synthesizes 3.2M grasps over 157K scenes, enabling real-world grasping from screws to large boxes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dexterous grasp synthesis methods rely on manually designed expected contacts and initialization heuristics, which struggle to balance synthesis success rate and diversity across varying object scales and contact modes.

Method: HUGS learns an object-conditioned human prior from a self-collected human grasp dataset (1.8K grasps, 304 objects). The prior captures human grasp preferences and guides downstream force-closure-aware optimization. During synthesis, it adaptively proposes contact modes and wrist initializations to improve coverage and success rate balance.

Result: Synthesized 3.2M robotic grasps over 157K scenes, spanning object half-diagonal lengths 2 cm to 30 cm and modes from two-finger to bimanual grasps. Models trained on this dataset autonomously select appropriate contact modes in real-world settings.

Conclusion: HUGS effectively balances contact-mode coverage and synthesis success rate compared to heuristic-based methods, enabling a unified grasp synthesis framework that generalizes across scales and contact modes for real-world dexterous grasping.

Abstract: Dexterous grasping across diverse object scales requires contact modes ranging from two-finger pinches to bimanual grasps. Existing dexterous grasp synthesis methods reduce the high-dimensional optimization space with manually designed expected contacts and initialization heuristics, which struggle to balance synthesis success rate and diversity. We present HUGS (Human-prior-guided Unified Dexterous Grasp Synthesis), a human-prior-guided framework for unified dexterous grasp synthesis across modes and scales. Instead of directly retargeting human demonstrations, HUGS learns an object-conditioned human prior that captures human grasp preferences and guides downstream force-closure-aware optimization. The prior is trained on a compact self-collected human grasp dataset with 1.8K grasps over 304 objects, providing broad coverage of object scales and contact modes. During synthesis, HUGS adaptively proposes contact modes and wrist initializations, substantially improving the balance between contact-mode coverage and synthesis success rate over heuristic-based methods. With HUGS, we synthesize 3.2M robotic grasps over 157K scenes, spanning object half-diagonal lengths from 2 cm to 30 cm and modes from two-finger to bimanual grasps. Models trained on the synthesized dataset autonomously select appropriate contact modes in the real world, enabling grasping from screws to large boxes.

</details>


### [159] [Simple-to-Complex Structured Demonstrations for Vision-Language-Action Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04591)
*Xinchuan Qiu,Yi Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a simple-to-complex structured demonstration collection strategy for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, addressing the overlooked aspect of how demonstrations are organized. The approach decomposes tasks into sub-skills, standardizes environments, and progressively increases task complexity. Evaluated on block sorting and towel folding tasks, it shows improved success rates and training stability over end-to-end demonstration collection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLA research focuses on model architectures, training strategies, and dataset scale, but little attention is paid to how demonstrations are collected and organized. Demonstration organization directly affects policy learning efficiency, training stability, and policy generalization, yet it remains a fundamental but overlooked aspect of imitation learning.

Method: The authors propose a simple-to-complex structured demonstration collection strategy with three principles: (i) decomposing complex manipulation tasks into progressively learnable sub-skills, (ii) standardizing the interaction environment to reduce unnecessary variability, and (iii) organizing demonstrations according to progressively increasing task complexity. This enables VLA models to acquire fundamental skills before learning more complex task compositions.

Result: Evaluated on block grasping & sorting and towel folding tasks using a dual-arm robotic platform. The proposed strategy shows consistent improvements in task success rate and training stability compared to the baseline method of directly collecting end-to-end complete task trajectories.

Conclusion: Demonstration organization is a previously underexplored but important factor in VLA learning. The findings provide practical insights into efficient skill acquisition, scalable dataset construction, and long-horizon robotic manipulation.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have demonstrated strong capabilities in robotic manipulation by integrating visual perception, language understanding, and robot action generation. Existing research has primarily focused on improving model architectures, training strategies, and dataset scale, while little attention has been paid to how demonstrations are collected and organized. We identify demonstration organization as a fundamental yet overlooked aspect of imitation learning, as it directly affects policy learning efficiency, training stability, and policy generalization. To address this gap, we propose a simple-to-complex structured demonstration collection strategy for VLA learning using a dual-arm robotic platform. Our approach systematically organizes data through three general principles: (i) decomposing complex manipulation tasks into progressively learnable sub-skills, (ii) standardizing the interaction environment to reduce unnecessary variability, and (iii) organizing demonstrations according to progressively increasing task complexity. This structured design enables VLA models to first acquire fundamental manipulation skills before learning increasingly complex task compositions, facilitating more effective learning of long-horizon manipulation tasks. We evaluate the proposed strategy on two representative robotic manipulation tasks: block grasping and sorting, and towel folding. Experimental results show consistent improvements in task success rate and training stability compared with the baseline method of directly collecting end-to-end complete task trajectories. These findings highlight demonstration organization as a previously underexplored but important factor in VLA learning and provide practical insights into efficient skill acquisition, scalable dataset construction, and long-horizon robotic manipulation.

</details>


### [160] [SEAM: Smooth Execution of Action-Chunked Motion for Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04609)
*Dijia Zhan,Xuemiao Xu,Jinyi Li,Jie Tang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SEAM is a training-free inference-time method for flow matching VLA policies that reduces action chunk discontinuities by exploiting previously executed tail chunks as consistency references, using a closed-form Velocity-guided Loss Steering correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA policies with fixed-length action chunks suffer from multimodal bifurcation — adjacent chunks from independent Gaussian latents converge to incompatible trajectory modes, causing discontinuities at chunk boundaries. Existing solutions are costly (backpropagation, rejection sampling, or retraining).

Method: SEAM (Smooth Execution of Action-Chunked Motion): a training-free inference-time method. Core mechanism is Velocity-guided Loss Steering (VLS), which derives a time-dependent target from the previous chunk's unexecuted tail and applies a closed-form correction after each Euler step without backpropagating through the policy network.

Result: On LIBERO-10 with pi_0.5, SEAM reduces boundary jerk by 28%, reduces chunk transition discontinuity by 27%, preserves baseline-level task success, and keeps denoising-loop cost near the unguided baseline.

Conclusion: SEAM effectively smooths action chunk transitions in VLA policies without training overhead, offering a practical inference-time solution that maintains task performance and computational efficiency.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies that execute fixed-length action chunks can exhibit multimodal bifurcation: a cross-chunk inconsistency in which adjacent chunks generated from independent Gaussian latents can converge to incompatible trajectory modes, producing abrupt discontinuities at chunk boundaries. Existing remedies either require backpropagation through the policy at each denoising step, rely on rejection sampling, or require retraining, each trading computational cost or task reliability for smoother transitions. We propose SEAM (Smooth Execution of Action-Chunked Motion), a training-free inference-time method for flow matching VLAs. SEAM exploits a simple synchronous-execution insight: after the robot consumes the executed prefix, the previous chunk's unexecuted tail is already available as an analytic consistency reference. Its core mechanism, Velocity-guided Loss Steering (VLS), derives a time-dependent target from this tail and applies a closed-form correction after each Euler step without backpropagating through the policy network. On LIBERO-10 with pi_0.5, SEAM reduces boundary jerk by 28%, reduces chunk transition discontinuity by 27%, preserves baseline-level task success, and keeps denoising-loop cost near the unguided baseline.

</details>


### [161] [RoboVista: Evaluating Vision Language Models for Diverse Robot Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04610)
*Shuangyu Xie,Kaiyuan Chen,Ziyang Chen,Simeon Adebola,Yixuan Huang,Zehan Ma,Tianshuang Qiu,Wentao Yuan,Dhruv Shah,Pannag R. Sanketi,Ken Goldberg*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces RQA (Robot Question Answering), a modular evaluation framework, and RoboVista, a benchmark of 474 VQA instances across 39 robotic task types from real systems and expert annotations. Experiments reveal that state-of-the-art VLMs have significant gaps on this benchmark, with strong correlation to real-world robot task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing robot benchmarks rely on teleoperated, end-to-end datasets that fail to capture the modular decision components needed for aligning Vision-Language Models with diverse robot applications across varied embodiments, visual conditions, and planning complexities.

Method: The authors propose RQA (Robot Question Answering), a modular evaluation framework, and curate RoboVista — a benchmark of 474 VQA instances with human-annotated reasoning, sourced from real robotic systems, research papers, and expert annotations, covering 39 unique task types across agricultural, industrial, domestic, surgical robotics, autonomous driving, and open robot datasets.

Result: State-of-the-art VLMs exhibit substantial performance gaps on RoboVista, and physical robot experiments demonstrate a strong correlation between RoboVista performance and real-world task execution.

Conclusion: The RQA framework and RoboVista benchmark effectively expose VLM limitations for robotic reasoning, and performance on the benchmark is predictive of real-world robotic task success.

Abstract: Diverse applications for robotics, such as industry and agriculture, require robots to operate across various embodiments, changing visual conditions, and complex planning. Vision-Language Models (VLMs) offer a promising foundation for general-purpose and interpretable robotic reasoning. Aligning VLMs with diverse robot applications requires a modular understanding of the individual decision components that underlie robotic behavior. Capturing such structure is challenging for conventional robot benchmarks that are primarily based on teleoperated, end-to-end datasets. We propose Robot Question Answering (RQA), a modular evaluation framework and RoboVista, a benchmark curated from real robotic systems, research papers, and expert annotations. RoboVista contains 474 Visual Question Answering (VQA) instances with human annotated reasoning and covers 39 unique task types in agricultural, industrial, domestic, surgical robotics, autonomous driving, and open robot datasets. Experiments on RoboVista show that state-of-the-art VLMs exhibit substantial gaps. Physical robot experiments suggest strong correlation between RoboVista performance and real-world task execution.

</details>


### [162] [DIVO: Continuous-time DVL-Inertial-Visual Odometry for Unmanned Underwater Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04615)
*Kyungmin Jung,Angad Bajwa,Junha Yoo,Arturo Del Castillo Bernal,James Richard Forbes*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A novel acoustic-visual-inertial odometry system using continuous-time Gaussian process trajectory estimation for underwater vehicles, combining DVL, stereo camera, and IMU data with a learning-based visual frontend, outperforming state-of-the-art SLAM methods on real underwater datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater environments pose challenges for visual localization (light attenuation, illumination variance, particulate matter), motivating the use of multiple sensing modalities and a robust visual tracking pipeline.

Method: First continuous-time Gaussian process trajectory estimation framework fusing asynchronous DVL, stereo camera, and IMU measurements; learning-based feature extraction and matching visual frontend robust to subsea conditions; seamless integration of additional sensors in continuous-time.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art visual-inertial and acoustic-visual-inertial SLAM algorithms in accuracy, robustness, and trajectory coverage on real-world underwater inspection datasets, despite using only short-term visual data associations.

Conclusion: The proposed continuous-time framework with learning-based visual frontend achieves superior underwater odometry performance and is adaptable to different environments without reconfiguration.

Abstract: This paper presents a novel acoustic-visual-inertial odometry solution leveraging a continuous-time trajectory estimation framework for unmanned underwater vehicles. Underwater environments present unique challenges for visual localization and mapping, such as light attenuation, illumination variance, and the presence of particulate matter. This motivates the use of additional sensing modalities and a visual tracking pipeline that is robust to diverse subsea conditions. The proposed system is the first continuous-time trajectory estimation framework based on Gaussian processes to fuse asynchronous measurements from a Doppler velocity log, a stereo camera, and an inertial measurement unit. Additionally, a novel visual frontend is proposed, incorporating learning-based feature extraction and matching that is robust to the specific challenges that subsea environments present. The proposed framework enables seamless integration of additional sensor modalities in continuous-time and is adaptable to different environments without reconfiguration. The proposed system is extensively tested on real-world underwater inspection datasets, where it outperforms state-of-the-art visual-inertial and acoustic-visual-inertial SLAM algorithms in accuracy, robustness, and trajectory coverage. Notably, the proposed system outperforms the state-of-the-art despite only forming short-term visual data associations.

</details>


### [163] [SILO: Simulation-in-the-Loop Sim-to-Real Transfer for Multi-Stage Cable Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04616)
*Stone Tao,Jie Xu,Hesam Rabeti,Yashraj Narang,Yijie Guo,Iretiayo Akinola*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces a sim-to-real RL framework for multi-stage cable routing that leverages GPU-parallelized simulation, achieving higher success rates and 2x reduction in cycle times compared to prior methods, with a novel SILO deployment strategy combining localized RL policies and robust state estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear-deformable manipulation (e.g., cables, ropes) is challenging due to complex deformations. Prior imitation learning approaches require thousands of demonstrations for specific tasks and cable types, limiting scalability and generalization.

Method: A sim-to-real RL framework using GPU-parallelized simulation to approximate linear deformable behaviors. The deployment strategy combines a Simulation In the LOop (SILO) execution framework, localized RL policies, and robust cable state estimation to bridge the sim-to-real gap.

Result: Achieves higher success rates and 2x reduction in cycle times compared to prior state-of-the-art learning methods on real-world cable routing tasks. First successful sim-to-real transfer of RL policies for multi-stage cable routing.

Conclusion: Training across thousands of parallel simulations enables learned policies to generalize across diverse cable geometries and deformation patterns, and the SILO framework successfully bridges the sim-to-real gap for cable routing.

Abstract: Linear-deformable manipulation remains challenging due to the complex deformations of objects such as cables and ropes. Prior data-driven approaches, particularly imitation learning, have shown some promise in narrowly defined settings but typically require thousands of demonstrations for specific tasks and cable types, limiting scalability and generalization. We introduce a sim-to-real reinforcement learning (RL) framework for multi-stage cable routing that leverages GPU-parallelized simulation to approximate linear deformable behaviors. Training across thousands of parallel simulations enables the learned policies to generalize across diverse cable geometries and deformation patterns. To bridge the sim-to-real gap, we propose a novel deployment strategy that combines a Simulation In the LOop (SILO) execution framework, localized RL policies, and robust cable state estimation. On real-world cable routing tasks, our approach achieves higher success rates and 2x reduction in cycle times compared to prior state-of-the-art learning methods. To our knowledge, this is the first successful sim-to-real transfer of RL policies for multi-stage cable routing. Videos and additional visualizations are available at https://silo-cable-routing.github.io/

</details>


### [164] [Governed Caste Reassignment in Heterogeneous Swarms: An Asymmetric-Trust Protocol with Audited Operator Countersignature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04634)
*Xue Qin,Simin Luan,Cong Yang,Zhijun Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes an asymmetric-trust protocol for caste reassignment in heterogeneous robot swarms, where automatic tightening to lower-privilege roles is allowed, but relaxation to higher-privilege roles requires operator countersignature against a per-axis budget. Each transition is recorded in a hash-chained Merkle audit log, verified offline via identity manifest. The protocol resists four explicit attacks and includes a distributed audit layer with quorum-committed total order and fork exclusion, validated on simulated and real multi-process deployments up to 100 robots/processes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches treat caste reassignment as an internal allocation algorithm without external authorization, but in regulated embodied deployments, elevating a robot's privilege envelope is a governance event that must be auditable and externally authorised.

Method: An asymmetric-trust protocol: auto-tightening (to lower-privilege castes) is automatic; bounded relaxation (to higher-privilege castes) requires operator countersignature against per-axis budget. Each transition carries a signed cause-chain committed to a hash-chained Merkle audit log, verifiable offline from an operator-signed identity manifest. A distributed audit layer replicates logs across N replicas with quorum-committed total order and cryptographic fork exclusion.

Result: Auto-tightening completes in single-digit to low-double-digit milliseconds on 100 robots. The governed protocol refuses all four attack types by construction; a randomized fuzz adversary finds no admission. Distributed audit achieves agreement and fork exclusion in both simulation and real multi-process deployment (up to 100 processes) with a Byzantine equivocator.

Conclusion: The construction generalises a single-agent persona-mutation governance gate to swarm-level caste governance, providing cryptographically auditable and externally authorisable privilege transitions for heterogeneous robot swarms.

Abstract: In heterogeneous robot swarms, caste reassignment (rebinding a robot to a new capability-bound role) is a high-frequency runtime event driven by battery, payload, and priority changes. Existing approaches treat it as an internal allocation algorithm and do not expose the reassignment to external authority. We argue that for regulated embodied deployments a caste change that elevates a robot's privilege envelope is a governance event that must be auditable and externally authorised. We propose an asymmetric-trust protocol: auto-tightening reassignments (to safer, lower-privilege castes) are admitted automatically, while bounded relaxation (to higher-privilege castes) requires an operator countersignature against a per-axis budget. Each transition carries a signed cause-chain, committed to a hash-chained Merkle audit log that an offline auditor verifies from an operator-signed identity manifest alone. We evaluate a reference implementation with real Ed25519 signatures over fleets up to 100 robots: auto-tightening completes in single-digit to low-double-digit milliseconds, and the governed protocol refuses four explicit attacks (caste laundering, repeated-relaxation escalation, operator impersonation, cause-chain forgery) by construction, with a partially-governed baseline isolating which gate stops which attack and a randomized fuzz adversary finding no admission. A distributed audit layer replicates the log across N per-member replicas with quorum-committed total order and cryptographic fork exclusion; we prove agreement and fork exclusion and validate them both in simulation and as a real multi-process deployment over TCP sockets (up to 100 real processes) with a Byzantine equivocator, on which every honest replica agrees, detects the equivocation, and commits no fork. The construction generalises a single-agent persona-mutation governance gate to swarm-level caste governance.

</details>


### [165] [KAM-WM: Kinematic Affordance Maps from Latent World Models for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04652)
*Xinyu Shao,Keru Zhou,Guowei Huang,Yajun Gao,Tongtong Cao,Xiu Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: KAM-WM extracts coarse directional interaction cues from a frozen latent video world model (Flow Matching backbone) as Kinematic Affordance Maps, which are compressed via a Perceiver to condition a diffusion policy. It achieves strong results on LIBERO (90.6%) and RoboTwin2.0 (65.7% Easy, 22.4% Hard), showing that directional priors from video models outperform static mask priors for few-shot manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning manipulation from few demonstrations requires visual priors capturing both where and how to interact; static priors (e.g., segmentation masks) only encode spatial localization, missing directional interaction cues.

Method: KAM-WM queries a frozen Flow Matching image-to-video backbone once, interprets its single-step latent velocity as a Kinematic Affordance Map (KAM) providing task-conditioned interaction regions and coarse motion structure, then uses a lightweight Perceiver to compress KAM into tokens that condition a diffusion policy with RGB and proprioception.

Result: 90.6% average success on LIBERO; 65.7% and 22.4% success rates in Easy and Hard settings on RoboTwin2.0. Controlled comparisons show directional information outperforms zero-order mask priors.

Conclusion: A frozen video model can provide a useful first-order visual prior for control without test-time rollout cost, with directional cues improving over static spatial localization alone.

Abstract: Learning manipulation from few demonstrations requires visual priors that capture not only where to interact, but also how the interaction should begin; static priors such as segmentation masks encode only the former. We present KAM-WM, a framework that extracts a coarse directional interaction cue from a frozen latent video world model without rollout or world-model fine-tuning. KAM-WM queries a Flow Matching image-to-video backbone once and interprets its single-step latent velocity as a Kinematic Affordance Map (KAM), which provides task-conditioned interaction regions and coarse motion structure. A lightweight Perceiver compresses KAM into tokens that condition a diffusion policy together with RGB observations and proprioception. Across LIBERO and RoboTwin2.0, KAM-WM reaches 90.6% average success on LIBERO and achieves 65.7% and 22.4% success rates in the Easy and Hard settings on RoboTwin2.0, respectively. Controlled comparisons against a zero-order mask prior suggest that part of the gains comes from directional information beyond spatial localization alone. These results indicate that, in the evaluated settings, a frozen video model can provide a useful first-order visual prior for control without the test-time cost of future rollout.

</details>


### [166] [Do Vision-Language-Action Models Mean What They Say? On the Role of Faithfulness in Embodied Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04681)
*Matthew Foutter,Matteo Cercola,Lena Wild,Yunshan Wang,Michelle Li,Daniele Gammelli,Marco Pavone*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper distinguishes between functional reasoning (improving task performance) and faithful reasoning (truthfully reflecting policy's internal decision process) in Embodied Chain-of-Thought for VLA models. It shows that current alignment strategies yield reasoning that can be unfaithful (ungrounded, inconsistent, disconnected), limiting generalization. Through human evaluation of an autonomous driving reasoning model, they find weak coupling between reasoning quality and trajectory improvement. They introduce Pinocchio, a learned critic that scores embodied faithfulness via observation grounding and stepwise coherence, used as dense reward for post-training with RL. The post-trained planner improves faithfulness by 4-18% over baselines while maintaining performance, and improves responsiveness to rare counterfactual scenarios by 1.6x, demonstrating that faithful reasoning leads to more robust and generalizable embodied intelligence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether verbalized Chain-of-Thought in embodied VLA models truthfully reflects the policy's underlying decision process, and to address the gap that current alignment strategies produce reasoning that can be unfaithful (ungrounded, disconnected, inconsistent), which limits policy generalization.

Method: They distinguish functional vs. faithful reasoning, conduct human evaluation of a SoTA reasoning model for autonomous driving, then operationalize a behavioral surrogate for embodied faithfulness via a learned critic (Pinocchio) that scores observation grounding and stepwise coherence, and use this critic as a dense reward signal in post-training an embodied policy with reinforcement learning.

Result: The post-trained planner improves faithfulness by 4% over SoTA alignment baselines and 18% over trajectory error post-training baselines while maintaining competitive task performance. On synthetic OOD test sets, post-training for faithfulness improves policy responsiveness to rare counterfactual scenarios by 1.6x over a SoTA policy.

Conclusion: Faithful reasoning traces contribute to more robust, generalizable, and interpretable embodied intelligence, and current alignment strategies provide only a necessary but insufficient notion of faithfulness.

Abstract: Embodied Chain-of-Thought has emerged as a promising mechanism to enhance robot decision-making and interpretability in black-box Vision-Language Action (VLA) models. However, whether this verbalized Chain-of-Thought truthfully reflects the policy's underlying decision process remains poorly understood. We distinguish between functional reasoning, in which reasoning improves task performance, and faithful reasoning, in which reasoning truly reflects the policy's internal decision process. We argue that SoTA alignment strategies offer a necessary but insufficient notion of faithfulness, admitting reasoning whose intermediate steps can mask the causal links in action prediction through confounding factors (e.g., reasoning that is ungrounded in the environment and internally disconnected or inconsistent), restricting policy generalization. We study this gap through a human evaluation of a SoTA reasoning model for autonomous driving, revealing an inconsistent coupling between reasoning quality and downstream trajectory improvement. We then operationalize a behavioral surrogate for embodied faithfulness through a learned critic, Pinocchio, scoring observation grounding and stepwise coherence, and use this critic as a dense reward signal in post-training an embodied policy with reinforcement learning. Across withheld driving benchmarks, our post-trained planner improves faithfulness by 4% and 18% over SoTA alignment and trajectory error post-training baselines, respectively, while maintaining competitive downstream task performance. Finally, on a synthetic out-of-distribution test set, post-training for faithfulness improves policy responsiveness to rare counterfactual scenarios by 1.6x that of a SoTA policy, suggesting that faithful reasoning traces contribute to more robust, generalizable, and interpretable embodied intelligence. Project page: https://mjf-su.github.io/pinocchio/

</details>


### [167] [A Reliable Context-Aware and Temporal Planning Framework for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04689)
*Argho Dey,Yunfei Yin,Swachha Ray,Md Minhazul Islam,Zheng Yuan,Sijing Xiong,Hongyu Liu,Zhiqiu Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RCT-AD is a framework that improves autonomous driving safety under degraded camera conditions by introducing a Reliable Context Awareness module with quality-gated FILO memory to filter unreliable observations, paired with a Temporal Trajectory Planner that leverages long-term dependencies and multi-agent interactions, achieving strong results on nuScenes benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Camera observations in dense urban driving are frequently degraded by occlusion, motion blur, illumination changes, and sensor noise; indiscriminately aggregating such corrupted observations over time leads to unstable trajectory planning and increased collision risk. Existing BEV-based methods fuse temporal information without assessing observation reliability.

Method: The framework consists of: (1) a Reliable Context Awareness module that scores per-frame reliability and selectively retains trustworthy features via a quality-gated First-In-Last-Out (FILO) memory mechanism, reconstructing degraded observations from reliable historical context; (2) a Temporal Trajectory Planner that captures long-term dependencies and multi-agent interactions for smoother, safety-aware trajectories; (3) a joint detection-and-segmentation head that injects semantic and motion cues into the shared BEV space.

Result: On nuScenes: 61.5 NDS, 52.9 mAP, 52.3 mIoU, with competitive computational efficiency suitable for real-time deployment. Outperforms recent end-to-end baselines in perception accuracy, motion prediction, and planning robustness.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling feature quality and temporal consistency via reliability gating and historical context reconstruction enables safer, more consistent planning under degraded sensing conditions without sacrificing real-time performance.

Abstract: Safe operation of autonomous vehicles in dense urban traffic depends on perception and planning that remain reliable when onboard sensing is degraded. In real driving conditions, camera observations are frequently corrupted by occlusion, motion blur, illumination change, and sensor noise, and when such degraded observations are aggregated indiscriminately over time, trajectory planning becomes unstable and collision risk rises for both the ego vehicle and surrounding road users. Recent Bird's-Eye-View (BEV) approaches unify perception and planning through a shared spatial representation, but most fuse temporal information across frames without assessing the reliability of the underlying observations. We present a Reliable Context-Aware and Temporal Planning framework for Autonomous Driving (RCT-AD) that explicitly models feature quality and temporal consistency to support safer, more consistent planning. A Reliable Context Awareness module scores per-frame reliability and selectively retains trustworthy features through a quality-gated First-In-Last-Out (FILO) memory mechanism, reconstructing degraded observations from reliable historical context so that corrupted inputs do not destabilize the scene representation. A Temporal Trajectory Planner captures long-term dependencies and multi-agent interactions to produce smoother, safety-aware trajectories, while a joint detection-and-segmentation head injects semantic and motion cues into the shared BEV space to strengthen scene understanding. Experiments on the nuScenes autonomous driving benchmark show that RCT-AD improves perception accuracy, motion prediction, and planning robustness over recent end-to-end baselines, achieving 61.5 nuScenes Detection Score, 52.9 mean Average Precision, and 52.3 mean Intersection over Union, while maintaining competitive computational efficiency suitable for real-time deployment.

</details>


### [168] [Geometry-Aware Motion Latents for Learning Robust Manipulation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04714)
*Yunchao Zhang,Yijia Weng,Ruizhe Liu,Ming Hu,Leonidas Guibas,Yanchao Yang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GeoMoLa learns discrete motion latent codes by predicting point cloud evolution during manipulation, achieving state-of-the-art manipulation performance from single-view RGB-D input by focusing on 3D geometric transformations rather than visual appearance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for learning motion latents rely on reconstructing visual observations, which capture appearance patterns rather than actual physical motion. The authors hypothesize that effective action abstractions require understanding three-dimensional geometric transformations, motivating a geometry-aware approach.

Method: GeoMoLa learns discrete motion latent codes by predicting how point clouds evolve during manipulation — a 4D objective (spatial geometry changing through time) — using only single-view RGB-D input, contrasting with prior methods requiring multi-view reconstruction.

Result: GeoMoLa achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse manipulation benchmarks using only single-view RGB-D, whereas existing methods require multi-view reconstruction. Ablations show geometric prediction drives performance, and learned codes generalize to novel scenes with physically consistent transformations.

Conclusion: Effective motion latents for robot control emerge better from understanding motion through its 3D geometric effects rather than pixel-level patterns, validated by robust real-world manipulation with minimal demonstrations in cluttered environments.

Abstract: Learning motion latents for robotic manipulation heavily relies on extracting motion patterns from visual sequences, yet effective action abstractions require understanding three-dimensional geometric transformations. Here, we introduce GeoMoLa (Geometry-Aware Motion Latents), which learns discrete motion latent codes by predicting how point clouds evolve during manipulation rather than reconstructing visual observations. This four-dimensional objective -- spatial geometry changing through time -- forces latent representations to encode actual physical motion rather than appearance patterns. GeoMoLa achieves state-of-the-art performance using only single-view RGB-D input, while existing methods require multi-view reconstruction, succeeding across diverse manipulation benchmarks. Our ablations reveal that geometric prediction is the key to driving performance, quantitatively validating that manipulation depends on spatial understanding. Furthermore, the learned codes exhibit effective motion abstraction: applying them to novel scenes produces physically consistent transformations regardless of visual context. Our real-world experiments also confirm this robustness capability, achieving robust manipulation with minimal demonstrations in cluttered environments where geometric reasoning determines success. Thus, we demonstrate that effective motion latents for robot control can better emerge from understanding motion through its three-dimensional effects rather than pixel-level patterns.

</details>


### [169] [Aerial Manipulation: Contact, Medium Coupling, and the Geometry of Readiness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04719)
*Antonio Franchi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This review proposes a medium-aware interpretation of aerial manipulation, arguing that aerial robots' interaction with objects is intrinsically coupled with their flight dynamics through the surrounding medium, and develops a framework of interaction modes, capability ladder, and actuation-geometric viewpoint to explain challenges and future directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aerial manipulation is fundamentally different from ground-based manipulation because flying agents maintain flight through continuous momentum and energy exchange with the surrounding medium, making support, locomotion, stabilization, and task interaction inseparable—a fact not adequately addressed by simply mounting manipulators on flying bases.

Method: The paper organizes biological and robotic examples into interaction modes and a capability ladder, then develops an actuation-geometric viewpoint where redundancy creates task-equivalent fibers, with internal motion along these fibers enabling trade-offs between energy, active readiness, aerodynamic promptness, and passive medium coupling.

Result: A framework clarifying why aerial manipulation is difficult, why biological flyers outperform robotic systems, and a perspective on how future platforms can command forces while also shaping how the medium acts back on them.

Conclusion: Aerial manipulation requires a medium-aware perspective that recognizes the inherent coupling between flight dynamics and physical interaction, with future platforms needing to both command forces and shape medium feedback for broader capability.

Abstract: Aerial robots are increasingly moving from remote observation toward physical interaction with objects, surfaces, structures, loads, and surrounding flows. This review argues that aerial manipulation cannot be understood as classical manipulation simply mounted on a flying base. Because flying agents remain aloft through continuous momentum and energy exchange with the surrounding medium, support, locomotion, stabilization, and task-directed interaction are intrinsically coupled. Building on broad views of manipulation as intentional environmental regulation through physical interaction, we propose a medium-aware interpretation of aerial manipulation in which interaction may be mediated by contact, by the surrounding fluid, or by both. The review organizes biological and robotic examples into a repertoire of interaction modes and a capability ladder, then develops an actuation-geometric viewpoint in which redundancy induces task-equivalent fibers. Internal motion along these fibers can trade energy for active readiness, aerodynamic promptness, and passive medium coupling. This perspective clarifies why aerial manipulation is difficult, why biological flyers remain broader than robotic systems, and how future platforms may command forces while also shaping how the medium acts back on them.

</details>


### [170] [Spatial Attention: Adapting Execution Horizons for Diffusion Policies via Observation Sensitivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04739)
*Che-Sang Park,Junsu Ha,Jianlong Fu,Frank C. Park*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces adaptive execution horizons for action chunks in robotic learning from demonstration, using Spatial Attention (gradient norm of action log-likelihood w.r.t. observation) to dynamically adjust chunk length — shorter horizons when attention is high (sensitive phases) and longer when low — improving success rates without increasing average execution horizon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative action chunk methods for robotic learning use fixed execution horizons, failing to balance responsiveness (short chunks) and computational cost (long chunks) effectively, especially under disturbances.

Method: Defines Spatial Attention as the expected squared norm of the gradient of action log-likelihood w.r.t. observation. Derives that under fixed sampling budget, optimal horizon minimizing cumulative likelihood drop decreases with increasing Spatial Attention. Uses a forecasting module to predict future Spatial Attention alongside action chunks and dynamically assigns shorter horizons to high-attention phases and longer horizons to low-attention phases.

Result: Experiments on standard and perturbed tasks in simulation and on a real robot show improved success rates over fixed-horizon baselines while maintaining the same average execution horizon.

Conclusion: Adaptive horizon adjustment based on Spatial Attention effectively balances responsiveness and computational efficiency, outperforming fixed-horizon approaches in robotic manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Sampling action chunks via generative models has become a widely adopted methodology for robotic learning from demonstration. However, existing methods often struggle to balance responsiveness and computational cost because they execute each action chunk for a fixed execution horizon. In this paper, we adaptively adjust the execution horizon of sampled action chunks, balancing responsiveness and computational efficiency. We introduce Spatial Attention -- defined as the expected squared norm of the gradient of the action log-likelihood with respect to the observation -- which indicates the sensitivity of the policy's action distribution to variations in the observation. We show that, under a fixed budget of chunk samplings, the execution horizon that minimizes the cumulative likelihood drop induced by disturbances decreases as Spatial Attention increases. By forecasting future Spatial Attention values alongside the action chunk, our framework dynamically assigns shorter execution horizons to phases with high Spatial Attention, and longer horizons to phases with low Spatial Attention. Experiments on standard and perturbed tasks, in both simulation and on a real robot, show that our method significantly improves success rates over fixed-horizon baselines while maintaining the average execution horizon.

</details>


### [171] [Trajectory-Anchor Optimization for Overconfident Thermal Visual Place Recognition: Zero-Leakage OOD Auditing and Kidnapped-Robot Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04745)
*Zhiyuan Lu,Kanji Tanaka*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TAO compresses multi-hypothesis tracking into a batched SE(2) Procrustes alignment problem with O(KN) complexity, serving as an efficient geometric fail-safe filter that detects hallucinated loop closures by exploiting the spatial dispersion of K=100 trajectory hypotheses beyond a 5m macroscopic threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation model-based TIR-VPR frontends exhibit overconfident false positives under OOD/unmapped conditions, while classical MHT backends are computationally prohibitive for real-time robotics due to exponential complexity.

Method: TAO reformulates multi-view temporal verification as a batched SE(2) Procrustes alignment problem using tensor-level vectorization and single-invocation batched SVD, achieving O(KN) per-frame complexity with bounded execution loops.

Result: Within 5m, hallucinations exhibit locally consistent geometry that deceives rigid alignment; beyond 5m, K=100 hypotheses disperse spatially, breaking temporal co-visibility constraints (N=20) and causing joint optimization residuals to rise sharply, enabling reliable detection of catastrophic topological breaks.

Conclusion: TAO establishes a macroscopic convergence basin (10m) where multi-view geometric consistency reliably isolates catastrophic false acceptances, serving as an efficient fail-safe filter for VPR under a strict zero-leakage protocol.

Abstract: Modern thermal visual place recognition (TIR-VPR) frontends based on foundation models achieve remarkable closed-set retrieval but suffer from an overconfident forced-matching failure mode. Under out-of-distribution (OOD) or unmapped conditions, they generate highly plausible yet false loop candidates without a drop in similarity scores. While classical multi-hypothesis tracking (MHT) backends can mitigate these ambiguities by maintaining divergent trajectory beliefs, their exponential computational overhead violates real-time robotic constraints. To bridge this gap, we present Trajectory-Anchor Optimization (TAO). To counter the combinatorial challenge of evaluating parallel hypotheses (e.g., K=100), TAO compresses multi-view temporal verification into a batched SE(2) Procrustes alignment problem. By leveraging tensor-level vectorization and single-invocation batched SVD, this formulation bypasses the dynamic tree expansion of MHT, guaranteeing a strictly bounded per-frame execution loop of O(KN). Under a strict zero-leakage evaluation protocol, we show that while a passive geometric backend cannot mathematically separate metric localization errors from coherent hallucinations at a micro-scale (<5m) due to local visual ambiguities, TAO serves as an efficient fail-safe filter at a macro-scale. Within a 5m radius, hallucinations often possess a locally consistent geometry that deceives rigid alignment. However, beyond this threshold, the K=100 disparate hypotheses disperse spatially across the global map. This dispersion breaks the rigid temporal co-visibility constraint within the sliding window (N=20), causing the joint optimization residual to escalate sharply. Consequently, TAO establishes a distinct macroscopic convergence basin (10m) where multi-view geometric consistency reliably isolates catastrophic topological breaks and suppresses critical false acceptances.

</details>


### [172] [SLAM: Structured and Localized Analytic Manifold Adaptation for Lifelong VPR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04764)
*Kenta Tsukahara,Kanji Tanaka,Rai Hisada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SLAM is a closed-form analytic framework for lifelong Visual Place Recognition that unifies Unscented transformation, GMM-based topological partitioning, and H∞ robust optimization, achieving 27.5% nominal accuracy (U+G) while providing mathematically guaranteed worst-case robustness via a single tunable parameter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual Place Recognition (VPR) in lifelong deployment requires continuous adaptation to new environments without catastrophic forgetting.

Method: Proposes SLAM framework unifying: (1) uncertainty-aware smoothing via Unscented transformation, (2) topological space partitioning using Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM), and (3) H∞ robust bound optimization—all in a closed-form analytical recursion.

Result: U+G configuration achieves 27.5% nominal accuracy (state-of-the-art); full H∞ deployment provides mathematically guaranteed minimax robust bound without architectural split.

Conclusion: The system seamlessly modulates the trade-off between nominal precision and worst-case disturbance attenuation via a single regularization parameter.

Abstract: Visual Place Recognition (VPR) in lifelong deployment requires continuous adaptation to new environments without catastrophic forgetting. In this paper, we propose SLAM, a Structured and Localized Analytic Manifold adaptation framework. Our framework elegantly unifies uncertainty-aware smoothing via Unscented transformation, topological space partitioning through a Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM), and $H_\infty$ robust bound optimization into a singular, unified closed-form analytical recursion. Exhaustive ablation studies demonstrate that while the synergistic combination of uncertainty smoothing and localized mapping (U+G configuration) achieves the state-of-the-art nominal accuracy of 27.5%, the full deployment of the $H_\infty$ bound does not require an architectural split; rather, it introduces a mathematically guaranteed minimax robust bound. This formulation enables the system to seamlessly modulate the intrinsic trade-off between nominal placement precision and worst-case disturbance attenuation through a single regularization parameter.

</details>


### [173] [CAC-VLA: Context-Gated Action Conditioning for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04816)
*Yifu Xiong,Wenhao Yu,Jiaxuan Lin,Bojun Zou,Jiahao Li,Lu Zhang,Yanyong Zhang,Jianmin Ji*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CAC-VLA introduces a context-gated latent-action conditioning framework that enables VLA models to learn action representations directly within the VLM, achieving state-of-the-art performance on LIBERO benchmarks (98.3%) and LIBERO-Plus (89.5%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLA models use visual-language representations for action generation but do not explicitly optimize them for action conditioning, requiring separate action-generation frameworks to bridge multimodal understanding and motor control.

Method: CAC-VLA learns a lightweight latent-action interface within the VLM by predicting coarse-to-fine latent actions from future action segments, and adaptively conditions the action expert via a context gate that calibrates the influence of latent-action guidance.

Result: Achieves 98.3% average success rate on LIBERO and 89.5% on LIBERO-Plus, demonstrating effectiveness over prior methods.

Conclusion: Context-gated latent-action conditioning provides an effective interface for continuous expert control, enabling VLM-native action conditioning without separate action-generation frameworks.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have become a promising paradigm for generalist robot manipulation, where visual-language representations are used to condition continuous action generation. However, these representations are not explicitly optimized for action conditioning, leaving the action expert to bridge the gap between multimodal understanding and precise motor control. Recent action-reasoning methods introduce additional modules to generate explicit action plans or action-space reasoning signals, demonstrating the benefit of action-level guidance but often requiring separate action-generation frameworks. We propose CAC-VLA, a Context-Gated Action Conditioning framework that learns a lightweight latent-action interface directly within the VLM. Instead of generating executable trajectories, CAC-VLA trains the VLM to predict coarse-to-fine latent actions, which are structured representations encoded from future action segments, and adaptively leverages them to condition the action expert via a context gate. This enables VLM-native action conditioning while calibrating the influence of latent-action guidance on expert action generation. Experiments on LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus demonstrate the effectiveness of CAC-VLA, achieving 98.3% average success rate on LIBERO and 89.5% LIBERO-Plus, suggesting that context-gated latent-action conditioning is an effective interface for continuous expert control.

</details>


### [174] [Athena-WBC: Capability-Aligned Policy Experts for Long-Tail Humanoid Whole-Body Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04837)
*Yuan Jiang,Ningyuan Zhang,Xicun Yang,Shidi Li,Yuzhi Jiang,Zhiyi Rong,Shuaikang Ma,Chuanzheng Li,Jie Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Athena-WBC improves humanoid motion-tracking by addressing capability mismatches in training recipes via a teacher-student pipeline with dynamic and balance policy experts, outperforming strong baselines on long-tail and held-out motions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing humanoid motion-tracking controllers attribute failures to insufficient exposure to difficult motions; this paper shows that the root cause is a mismatch between motion demands and the capability induced by default training recipes, not just sampling strategy.

Method: A teacher-student pipeline (Athena-WBC) with two expert types: (1) dynamic experts using a tracking-focused, constraint-aware objective without conservative effort/temporal penalties, and (2) balance experts using a gravity curriculum for early-training survivability. Motion-routed DAgger distillation compresses experts into a single deployable controller with RL fine-tuning.

Result: Athena-WBC improves recovery of long-tail training-set motions and held-out tracking on a full-size humanoid compared to a strong SONIC-recipe baseline, using only a small number of experts.

Conclusion: Mismatch between training recipe capability and motion demands is a key bottleneck in whole-body control; capability-aligned experts can efficiently resolve this without extensive expert scaling or resampling strategies.

Abstract: Large-scale humanoid motion-tracking controllers are commonly improved by reallocating training effort: difficult motions are sampled more often, isolated into smaller subsets, or assigned to specialized experts. We show that this view is incomplete. In strong whole-body-control baselines, a residual set of feasible training clips remains unsolved even under targeted training, especially for high-dynamic transitions and balance-critical motions. These failures arise not only from insufficient exposure, but from a mismatch between the motion demands and the effective capability induced by the default training recipe. We propose Athena-WBC, a compact teacher-student pipeline with capability-aligned policy experts for long-tail humanoid whole-body control. Dynamic experts use a tracking-focused, constraint-aware objective that removes conservative effort and temporal-control penalties while preserving physical feasibility constraints; balance experts use a gravity curriculum to improve early-training survivability. The resulting privileged teachers are motion-routed for DAgger distillation and then compressed into a single controller with deployable observations followed by RL fine-tuning. Experiments on a full-size humanoid show improved recovery of training-set long-tail motions and better held-out tracking than a strong SONIC-recipe baseline, using only a small number of experts.

</details>


### [175] [WinTA-GIL: Windowed Trajectory Alignment for GNSS-IMU-LiDAR Heading Refinement in Intermittent Signal Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04879)
*Kaixin Feng,Zhichao Wen,Zhaohong Liao,Xin Xia,You Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WinTA-GIL is a novel heading refinement framework for multi-source fusion positioning that uses temporal window-based optimization of GNSS, IMU, and LiDAR data to dynamically correct heading estimates, overcoming limitations of conventional single-initialization alignment methods. It achieves superior accuracy and robustness by transforming heading estimation into a repeatable trajectory-based consistency optimization problem with an adaptive re-estimation mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-source fusion positioning systems rely on a singular initial alignment during startup for heading estimation, lacking dynamic adaptability. This makes them vulnerable to accumulated drift and observation noise during prolonged navigation or after GNSS signal outages.

Method: WinTA-GIL integrates GNSS, IMU, and LiDAR via a temporal window-based optimization strategy. It uses high-precision local trajectories from LiDAR-Inertial Odometry (LIO) registered against filtered GNSS observations, formulating heading estimation as a repeatable, trajectory-based consistency optimization problem. An adaptive re-estimation mechanism based on state discrimination triggers heading corrections as needed.

Result: Extensive experiments on both open-source and self-collected datasets demonstrate that WinTA-GIL significantly outperforms state-of-the-art approaches in both estimation accuracy and system robustness.

Conclusion: WinTA-GIL effectively addresses the heading drift and weak observability issues in complex environments, providing a robust and accurate heading estimation framework that dynamically corrects heading beyond the initial alignment phase.

Abstract: Although multi-source fusion positioning systems have achieved significant progress, accurate and reliable heading estimation remains a critical challenge due to the lack of gravitational constraints and the inherent weak observability of heading in complex environments. Most existing methodologies are specifically tailored for the startup phase, relying on a singular initial alignment to establish the heading reference. Consequently, these approaches lack the adaptability required to refine heading estimates dynamically, which renders the system highly vulnerable to accumulated drift and observation noise during prolonged navigation or immediately following GNSS signal outages. To address these limitations, this paper proposes WinTA-GIL, a novel heading refinement framework that integrates information from Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS), Inertial Measurement Unit (IMU), and Light Detection and Ranging (LiDAR) through a temporal window-based optimization strategy. Unlike conventional alignment methods restricted to the startup phase, WinTA-GIL leverages high-precision local trajectories from LiDAR-Inertial Odometry (LIO) to register against filtered GNSS observations. This approach transforms heading estimation into a repeatable, trajectory-based consistency optimization problem. In particular, an adaptive re-estimation mechanism based on state discrimination is incorporated to trigger heading corrections whenever necessary, thereby effectively suppressing the inertial drift accumulated during challenging conditions. Extensive experiments on both open-source and self-collected datasets demonstrate that WinTA-GIL significantly outperforms state-of-the-art approaches in both estimation accuracy and system robustness.

</details>


### [176] [PRISM: Personalized Robotic Dataset Generation via Image-based Scene and Motion Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04880)
*Dogyu Ko,Haneul Kim,Chanyoung Yeo,Dowoon Lee,Taeho Park,Hyoseok Hwang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PRISM generates personalized robotic datasets from a single image and language instruction, creating semantically/geometrically aligned "digital cousin" scenes and executable demonstrations without teleoperation, achieving up to 100% success on real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying pretrained vision-language-action models in user-specific environments is challenging due to limited generalization, requiring costly teleoperation for data collection or simulation that lacks target environment resemblance and task-specific trajectories.

Method: PRISM is an end-to-end pipeline that takes a single image and natural-language instruction, constructs digital cousin scenes aligned with the user environment at semantic/geometric levels but diverse at instance level, and synthesizes executable demonstrations without human teleoperation.

Result: Outperforms baselines on LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus; achieves up to 100% success rate on three real-world manipulation tasks; maintains stronger performance under distribution shift from training environments.

Conclusion: PRISM effectively bridges the gap between simulation and real-world deployment by generating task-specific, environment-aligned datasets, reducing the need for costly teleoperation while improving policy generalization.

Abstract: Recent advances in large-scale pretrained vision-language-action models have improved robot policy learning, but directly deploying such policies in user-specific environments remains challenging due to limited generalization, which inevitably requires collecting a dataset tailored to the target environment. Teleoperation yields well-aligned data but is costly and difficult to scale, whereas simulation scales easily but struggles to resemble the target environment and generate task-specific trajectories. To meet both simultaneously, we propose PRISM, an end-to-end pipeline that generates personalized robotic datasets from a single image and a natural-language instruction. PRISM constructs digital cousin scenes that are semantically and geometrically aligned with the user environment yet diverse at the instance level, and synthesizes executable demonstrations without human teleoperation. Extensive experiments show that policies trained on PRISM-generated datasets outperform those trained on baseline-generated datasets on LIBERO and LIBERO-Plus, achieve up to 100\% success rate on three real-world manipulation tasks, and maintain stronger performance when evaluated in environments that differ from those seen during training.

</details>


### [177] [DSWAM: A Dual-System World Action Foundation Model for Fine-Grained Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04927)
*Jian Zhu,Jianjun Zhang,Taiyi Su,Tianbin Liu,Zhangyuan Wang,Kai Xie,Zitai Huang,Chong Ma,Youzhang He,Tianjian Wang,Hanyang Wang,Weihao Ding,Yi Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DSWAM introduces a dual-system architecture combining a System 1 world action model executor with a System 2 vision-language subtask planner, enabling both efficient execution and task decomposition for complex multi-step household manipulation, with controlled real-robot comparison against VLA policies under the DeMaVLA benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing World Action Models (WAMs) lack explicit language-level planning for decomposing coarse instructions into fine-grained subtasks, which is critical for complex multi-step household tasks. Additionally, fair real-robot comparisons between WAMs and VLA policies are missing due to differences in data, embodiments, and protocols.

Method: DSWAM uses a dual-system design: System 1 is a WAM executor (trained with action prediction and video co-training, inference via direct action chunking) as default control; System 2 is a VLM-based subtask planner activated when decomposition is needed. Practical deployment uses TensorRT acceleration, asynchronous execution, and real-time chunking. Evaluation is done under the DeMaVLA setting with matched platform and criteria.

Result: Not explicitly stated in abstract, but the system achieves practical real-robot deployment with non-blocking control via RTC, and enables fair comparison with VLA policies on the DeMaVLA deformable manipulation benchmark.

Conclusion: DSWAM bridges the decomposition gap between WAMs and VLAs, and provides a framework for fair comparative evaluation between the two paradigms on real robots.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) provide a promising alternative to Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies by using video-based world modeling as dense supervision for robot action learning.
